# PREFACE

-><image-desc>A small decorative printer's ornament featuring a central circular element flanked by stylized scrollwork or floral motifs.</image-desc><-

No special apology is necessary for the publication of an English translation of the *Sushruta Samhita* <note>A foundational Sanskrit text on medicine and surgery, attributed to the sage Sushruta</note>. The vast medical literature of ancient India remains practically unexplored even now. Any undertaking that aims to make that *unknown territory* <note>original: "terra incognita"</note> known to the scientific world is bound to be welcomed by the public. Occasional <note>original: "spasmodic"</note> attempts have been made by several scholars and learned organizations to produce an English translation of the *Sushruta Samhita*, as it is the most representative work of *Ayurveda* <note>The traditional Hindu system of medicine</note>, but we regret to say that such efforts have so far failed. In spite of incomplete information on the subject, many drugs from the Ayurvedic *Materia Medica* <note>The body of collected knowledge about the therapeutic properties of substances used for healing</note> have been adopted by different foreign systems of medicine. This has given us a fresh motivation to issue an English translation of the book, which not only deals with the essentials of Indian therapeutics but embraces the whole range of the science of Ayurveda as it was understood and practiced by the Vedic sages.

We sincerely hope that the English translation of *Sushruta* which we have undertaken will, when completed, fill a long-standing gap and help to initiate a more thorough inquiry into the properties of the native drugs of India. Many institutions have already been established both in England and Germany with the sole purpose of studying the *causes* <note>original: "ætiology"</note> of tropical diseases and of developing a practical <note>original: "empirical"</note> system for their prevention and cure. We hope an English translation of the *Sushruta Samhita*, embracing as it—

<summary>This preface introduces the first complete English translation of the Sushruta Samhita, arguing that ancient Indian medical knowledge is a vital but neglected resource for global science. The authors aim to provide a comprehensive guide to Ayurvedic therapeutics and the medicinal properties of Indian plants.</summary>

<keywords>Sushruta Samhita, Ayurveda, Indian medicine, Materia Medica, Vedic sages, tropical diseases, medical translation, pharmacology</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from page 10, detailing the benefits of ancient Indian medical knowledge and the editorial methodology used for this English translation.</meta>

...the results of the research conducted by our ancient sages <note>original: "Rishis"</note> in their native land may provide a significant amount of useful information to those organizations. "We have many things to learn," observes Lieutenant Colonel C. P. Lukis, M.D., F.R.C.S., I.M.S., Principal of the Medical College in Calcutta, "from the people of this country regarding medicine and medical science." We do not doubt that an accurate knowledge of the contents of this magnificent monument of the *Ayurveda* <note>The traditional Hindu system of medicine</note> in fields where it can be utilized and improved upon will better equip humanity to fight against the hardships of life.

A few explanatory remarks regarding the methods we used to edit this work are necessary. We have carefully compared <note>original: "collated"</note> all available versions of the *Sushruta Samhita*, whether printed or handwritten. We have removed from the main body of our work any passages that are of questionable authenticity—even if not proven to be entirely fake—and placed them in footnotes as "Different Readings" or "Additional Texts." 

When we encountered doubts or differing opinions, we chose to follow the guidance of our respected teacher, the Great Scholar <note>original: "Mahamahopadhyaya"</note> Kaviraj Dvarkanath Sen, Kaviratna. We have also included explanatory phrases within brackets where a strictly literal translation would not capture the true meaning of the text. In many cases, it is impossible to find exact English equivalents for the technical terms of the *Ayurveda*. In these instances, we have placed approximate English words in brackets after the original Sanskrit terms. For example, we translated the term *Ojah* as "albumen" <note>A protein found in blood plasma or egg whites; in this context, it refers to a vital essence in the body</note>. However, the exact nature of *Ojah* in *Ayurveda* is a matter of debate. It may refer to something similar to, but not exactly, albumen; perhaps it is glycogen, which contributes significantly to...

<summary>This page discusses the value of ancient Indian medical research for modern science and explains the editors' rigorous process of cross-referencing Sanskrit texts and translating technical terms.</summary>
<keywords>Sushruta Samhita, Ayurveda, Rishis, medical translation methodology, Dvarkanath Sen, Ojah, medical history</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from page ii, discussing the editorial philosophy regarding scientific interpretation and providing an overview of the work's structural division and acknowledgments.</meta>

...the reproductive activity of the body in certain instances would appear to be the more correct description. In cases like this, we have not presented our readers with any suggestions of our own, but have left them free to draw their own conclusions. For this purpose, we further intend to append to the last volume of this work an index and a glossary illustrating the possible meanings of the Ayurvedic terms, with English and Latin synonyms wherever possible. The true meaning of the Ayurveda can be better explained or understood only in the light of modern science, and we leave it to our European colleagues to carry on the research along the lines we have suggested regarding this ancient system of medicine; a better knowledge of its principles and methods will enable them to do so.

Due to a regrettable oversight, the terms *Vayu*, *Pittam*, *Kaphah*, and *Dosha* were translated as wind, bile, phlegm, and humor in the first few chapters.

For the sake of convenience, we have divided the entire work into three volumes: the first contains only the *Sutrasthanam*; the second contains *Nidanam*, *Sharira*, and *Chikitsa*; and the third contains *Kalpa* and *Uttara Tantram*.

We have adopted the diagrams of surgical instruments from that most valuable work by the Thakore Saheb of Gondal, titled *The History of Aryan Medical Science*, for which I am particularly indebted to His Highness.

In conclusion, we wish to express our sincerest thanks to our teacher's son, Kaviraj Jogendranath Sen Vidyabhusana, M.A.; Dr. U. D. Banerji, L.R.C.P., M.R.C.S. (London); Lt. Col. K. P. Gupta, M.A., M.D., I.M.S.; and Professor Janakinath Bhattacharya, M.A., B.L., P.R.S., for having kindly examined the different portions of the manuscript. I am grateful to Dr. Surendranath

<meta>This page begins the introduction to the work, following the author’s preface dated 1907. It moves from personal acknowledgments to the historical and philosophical context of the Sushruta Samhita.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

**Sushruta: His age and personality:** A few preliminary observations regarding the technique of the <term>Ayurvedic</term> system of medicine are necessary at the start to correctly understand the aim and scope of the Sushruta Samhita. Who was Sushruta? When and where did he live and flourish? These are questions that would naturally suggest themselves to the readers of the following pages; but they can only be imperfectly answered, like all similar questions regarding the lives of our ancient figures of merit. In a country like India, where life itself was regarded simply as an illusion, the lives of kings or commoners were deemed matters of little importance to the vital essence of the people; and all histories and biographies were looked upon as the embodiment of the trivial vanities of life. The lives of saints and canonized kings were used in certain instances as themes for national epics. But they were intended more to explain or state the doctrines of certain schools of Ethics or Metaphysics than to record any historical fact or event. We have no authentic history beyond chronicles of state events and royal names in some instances; and those which are usually found in the Sanskrit Puránas are strange combinations of myths and legends, which often contradict each other. Therefore, it is completely futile to attempt to explain a historical fact by the light of a votive medal or tablet unearthed, perhaps, from the ruins of one of our ancient cities. Such an effort serves, in most cases, only to make the "darkness visible" and the confusion even greater.

<summary>This introduction discusses the difficulty of establishing a historical biography for Sushruta, noting that ancient Indian culture prioritized metaphysical and ethical doctrines over the recording of historical facts. It highlights that sources like the Puranas often blend myth with history, making traditional historical research challenging.</summary>

<keywords>Sushruta, Ayurvedic medicine, Sushruta Samhita, Indian history, Puranas, Metaphysics, historiography, ancient India</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction, focusing on the lineage and identity of Sushruta and the historical challenges in dating the original Sushruta Samhitá.</meta>

<page-num>ii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION. IV</header>

### **Identity of Sushruta and Divodása**:—One can only safely assert that Sushruta belonged to the lineage of Vishvámitra. The *Mahábháratam* (1) represents him as a son of that royal sage. This matches the description given of him in the current version of the *Samhitá*. The *Garuda Puránam* (2) places Divodása as fourth in descent from Dhanvantari, the first teacher of medical science on earth, while the *Sushruta Samhitá* describes the two as the same person. However, this apparent inconsistency in the *Samhitá* can be explained if we consider that in some parts of India, the custom still exists of adding the name of one’s father or a famous ancestor to one’s own name for better identification. It is therefore not surprising that Divodása (the teacher of Sushruta), who was a firm believer in the doctrine of psychic transmigration, should represent himself as an incarnation of Dhanvantari and adopt his name and title in the usual way. Beyond this scant genealogy, we possess no trustworthy information regarding the life and personality of Sushruta, the father of Indian Surgery.

### **Age of the Sushruta Samhitá**:—We have no means of determining what the *Samhitá* was like as originally written by Sushruta, as the current version is only a recension, or rather a

विश्वामित्रामजाः सर्वे मुनयो ब्रह्मवादिनः ॥

->Mahábháratam—Anushasan Parva, Chapter IV<-</note>

वनवडेः फलवतङ्गात्त सर्वांवशाचमन्द्रपः ।

यत्रामत्दाचधीकाः।सुन काश्याईर्वतमासथा ।
देवी धनलगिस्कापात केतुमांयवदातजः ।

# INTRODUCTION.

...recension of recensions, made by Nāgárjuna (1). All opinions agree in identifying him with the celebrated founder of the Mádhyamika school of Buddhist philosophy—a fact which significantly helps us in fixing the age of the present Samhitā. A few quotations from the Vriddha (old) Sushruta are all that are preserved of the original Samhitā. But their authenticity is questionable, and we are not sure whether they are the work of less significant figures, or of ancient though less renowned commentators, attributed to the master to grant them greater sanctity and authority—a practice which was quite common among the bibliographers of Ancient India.

### **Date of Nāgárjuna:**

At all events, Nagarjuna, who revised the Sushruta Samhita, lived about the latter part of the fourth century before the Christian era (2) and the,

<note>(1) "यत्र यत्र परोक्षं नियोगस्तत्र तत्रैव प्रतिसंस्कर्तृमतं ज्ञातव्यं। प्रतिसंस्कर्त्ता-
पीहनागार्जुन एव"।
->Dallana's Commentary, Sutrasthānam, Ch. I. 1.<-

Dallana mentions the names of Jejada, Gayadasa, etc., as the editors of the original Samhitā, and rejects as fake or of doubtful authority the texts which cannot be found in their editions of the work. Most probably the authoritative verses are quotations from the Vriddha Sushruta.

Revision or <term>Pratisamskāra</term> consists of shortening statements that have been made excessively detailed, and in expanding upon truths that have been very briefly handled in the original book. A Redactor or <term>Pratisamskartā</term> makes an old book new again.

->संक्षिप्रत्यतिविस्तॄणां लेशोक्तं विवृणोति च।<-
->संस्कर्ता कुरुते तन्त्रं पुराणञ्च पुनर्नवं॥<-

A Samhitā, on the other hand, deals with aphorisms contained in the Vedas.

->वेदवाक्यनिबद्धत्वात् संहितास्ताः प्रकीर्त्तिताः।<-</note>

<note>(2) तदा भगवत: शाक्यसिंहस्य परनिर्वृते:।
अस्मिन्महीलोकधातौ सार्द्धं वर्षशतं ह्यगात् ॥
बोधिसत्त्वश्च देशेऽस्मिन्नेको भूमीश्वरोभवत्।
स च नागार्जुन: श्रीमान् * * * ॥
->Rājatarangini I. Taranga. Vs. 172-173.<-</note>

--- Page 17 ---

<page-num>iv</page-num>
# INTRODUCTION.

The original or *Vriddha Sushruta* must have been written at least two centuries earlier in order to acquire that ancient authority and established status of age, which alone could have justified a revision at the time. Several scholars, on the authority of a very vague and general statement concerning the revision of the *Samhitá* in Dallana's commentary, attribute the authorship of the *Uttaratantram* (the latter portion of the *Sushruta Samhitá*) to Nágárjuna. We, on the other hand, hold the *Uttaratantram* to be neither an interpolation nor a subsequent addition, but that it forms an integral portion of the book as it was originally written, though not planned by the *Rishi*.

In the first Chapter of the *Sutrasthánam*, Divodása formally divides the science of *Áyurveda* into eight subdivisions, such as the *Shalya* (surgery), *Shálakya* (the portion treating diseases restricted to regions above the collarbone, such as the eyes, etc.), and *Káya-Chikitsá* (general diseases such as fever, etc.), but he does not say anything about them in the first five *Sthánas* or subdivisions of the book. It is only once, in the 25th chapter of the *Sutrasthánam*, that he mentions the name of *Netravartma* (diseases of the eyelids) in connection with the classification of surgical operations. 

It is impossible that Divodása would fail in his duties by omitting to give instructions on all the subdivisions of the *Áyurveda* as he promises at the outset, or that Sushruta would leave his *Samhitá*, which is pre-eminently a work on surgery, incomplete by excluding ophthalmic surgery, laryngotomy, or fever-therapeutics from his work. From the general plan of the book, we can safely assert that Sushruta dealt with easier or more elementary topics in the first five subdivisions of his *Samhitá* in the manner of our modern progressive readers, reserving the discussion of those requiring a more advanced knowledge and skill for the *Uttaratantram*. The *Uttaratantram* has not been included within the five original subdivisions of the *Samhitá* insofar as it embraces and more elaborately discusses

<summary>The author argues that the Uttaratantram is an original and essential part of the Sushruta Samhita, rather than a later addition by Nagarjuna, because it contains the advanced medical branches promised in the work's introduction.</summary>
<keywords>Sushruta Samhitá, Uttaratantram, Nágárjuna, Divodása, Áyurveda, Shalya, Shálakya, Káya-Chikitsá, medical history, ancient Indian surgery</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION.

topics that legitimately belong to, or are only incidentally mentioned in, those subdivisions. Hence, it is more like an appendix or supplement, arising from the requirements of the original subdivisions. It is probable that Nāgārjuna might have edited this part of the *Samhitā* along with its other portions.<note>Mahāmahopādhyāya Kavirāj Dvārakā Nāth Sen Kaviratna of Calcutta agrees with this opinion —Translator.</note>

**Western opinions on the subject:**—The consensus of Western opinions places Nāgārjuna in the first quarter of the third century B.C.<note>Beal's Buddhistic Records of the Western World, Vol. II, p. 212. Stein's *Rājataranginī*.</note> and identifies Sushruta as a contemporary of Shakyasinha Buddha. It is argued that the era immediately preceding Shakya Muni was a period of decline in Hindu thought; and the *Sushruta Samhitā* must have been the result of a revived intellectual activity that usually follows the arrival of a new creed—an assumption that supports the hypothesis of Greek influence on the Hindu system of medicine. But there were great men in India before Buddha. The age that immediately preceded the age of Buddha was by no means an age of decline; properly speaking, the age that followed the downfall of Buddhism shows, on the contrary, signs of true decline. India had eminent philosophers and scientists living almost at the same time as the great Buddha. The chronological facts collected above from the *Mahābhāratam* and the *Garuda Purānam* could have been interpreted to prove that the age of Sushruta was earlier than that of the *Mahābhāratam*, if not for the internal evidence provided by the *Samhitā* itself regarding the probable date of its composition, which we shall have occasion to address later on.<note>*Lalita-Vistara*—Raja R. L. Mitra's Edition, Chapter I.</note>

**Extraneous Evidence:**—Sushruta is mentioned in the

.VI

# INTRODUCTION.

Vārtikas of (1) Kātyāyana (4th Century B.C.) and we have no hesitation in saying that the original *Samhitā* was written at least two centuries before the birth of Buddha. We are equally ready to admit, on the other hand, that the final recension of the *Samhitā* by Nāgārjuna—at least the form in which we have it—was made about the 2nd Century B.C.

**Two Nāgārjunas:**—Several scholars, on the authority of Dallana (the celebrated commentator of the *Sushruta Samhitā*), endeavor to establish the identity of Nāgārjuna (the redactor of this *Samhitā*) with his namesake, the celebrated alchemist of the 10th century (2). But their contentions fall to the ground when we know that many verses of the *Sushruta Samhitā* occur in the works of Vāgbhata (*Ashtāngahridayam*) and Mādhava (*Nidānam*), which are two of the works that were translated by order of the Caliph (3) in the 8th century. The internal evidence of the book does not supply us with any authentic material to compose anything like a biography of this father of Hindu surgery.

**Internal Evidence:**—The line in the *Samhitā* which has formed a veritable bone of contention among scholars of all shades of opinion, as it sheds light upon the probable date of its composition, occurs in the *Shārira-Sthānam* in connection with the development of the fetal body. It reads: "Subhūti Gautama said that it is the trunk that first developed."

**Conflicting testimonies and the uncertain indication**

# INTRODUCTION.

certainty that Subhūti was one of the personal disciples of Śākya Siṅha Buddha, and that it was customary among contemporary Buddhists to append the title of their (1) lord (Gautama or Bodhisattva) to the name of a convert to emphasize his wisdom and sanctity in the world. A certain group of scholars never tires of using this line as conclusive evidence that the *Samhitā* was, at best, a contemporary production of early Buddhism. But they ignore the opinions of Shaunaka and others on the subject, quoted in the exact same part of the book, which places the date of its composition at least several centuries earlier. Shaunaka, who was sixth in the direct line of discipleship from the immortal Vyāsa, was the author of the renowned *Shaunaka Samhitā* of the *Atharvan*. These facts lend a very plausible weight to our hypothesis that the original *Sushruta Samhitā*, which was first composed perhaps at the same time as the later portions of the *Atharvan*, naturally discussed the opinions of Shaunaka and other Vedic embryologists, while Nāgārjuna, at the time of revising that book, quoted the opinion of his contemporary Subhūti for the purpose of giving him an equal status with the Vedic Rishis, if for nothing else.

**Greek Influence:**—Regarding Hellenic influence on the Hindu system of medicine and on the *Sushruta Samhitā* in particular, we must rid our minds of all sentiments of racial vanity and proceed to investigate the case in a scientific and unprejudiced spirit before giving a more detailed account of the contents of the *Sushruta Samhitā*.

viii

# INTRODUCTION.

**Sushruta and Hippocrates:—** From the very apparent similarity that exists between the contents of this *Samhitā* and the aphorisms of Hippocrates, many Western scholars are likely to conclude too hastily that the ancient Indians drew their inspiration in the art of healing from the medical works of the Greeks. However, the reverse may be said of the Greeks with even greater confidence, because such an assertion is supported by historical facts and confirmed by the research of Western scholars <note>(1)</note>. According to all accounts, Pythagoras was the founder of the art of healing among the Greeks and the Hellenic peoples in general <note>(2)</note>. This great philosopher learned his mysteries and metaphysics from the Brāhmanas of India. Mr. Pocock, in his *India in Greece*, identifies him with *Buddhagurus* or Buddha, and it is a simple inference to suppose that he carried many recipes and aphorisms of his master's *Āyurveda* with him. The sacred bean of Pythagoras is thought to have been the <note>(3)</note> Indian *Nelumbium* (*Utpalam*). We know that simultaneously with the birth of Buddhism, Buddhist *Sramanas* were sent out to Greece, Asia Minor, Egypt, and other distant countries to preach their new religion. They were known to the Greeks, and there is good reason to believe that the Greek *Simnoi* (venerable ones) were none other than the Buddhist *Sramanas* <note>(4)</note>. Now, a missionary usually teaches the sciences of his country in addition to preaching his gospel. The distant mission stations or monasteries of Buddhism were

Hippocrates, though he did his utmost to liberate medical science from the bondage of speculative philosophy, might still have thought it necessary to retain only those truths of the *Ayurveda* which Pythagoras and the Buddhist brotherhood might have imported into his country, and which do not specifically belong to the domain of pure metaphysics. Of course, it is quite possible for men of different nationalities to arrive at the same truth or conclusion independently. There are coincidences in science as in art and philosophy. (1) Gravitation and the circulation of blood (2) were known to the Indians long before the births of Newton and Harvey in Europe. The celebrated atomic theory was preached in the Gangetic valley some five hundred years before the birth of Christ (3). But we may well ask those who still cling to this Greek-centric view to look at the other side of the picture as well. It may be stated, without the slightest fear of contradiction, that the *Charaka* and *Sushruta*, through the channel of Arabic, Persian, and Latin translations, still form the

**Sushruta prior to Charaka:** The general consensus of expert opinion is to place Charaka before Sushruta in terms of time. However, the *Puranas* unanimously describe Sushruta as a disciple of Dhanvantari, the first proponent of medical science. The long compound words (*samásas*) he used, and the prose and metrical portions of the *Sushruta*—modeled after Jaimini, Patanjali, and other philosophical writers who adopted prose or verse according to the explanatory or rationalistic nature of their subjects—have all been cited to prove that Sushruta was a contemporary of the *Darshanas* or of Buddha. But these may serve, at least, to fix the date of the revision by Nagarjuna (that is, the *Sushruta Samhita* as we currently have it), but they can never help determine the chronology of the original Sushruta, the disciple of Dhanvantari "who emerged from the churning of the primordial ocean in the golden age (*Satya Yuga*)" <note>(3) क्षीरोदमथने वैद्यो देवो धन्वन्तरिस्ततः ।

the testimonies of the Puranas have any historical worth, we can safely place him somewhere in the Satya Yuga (Age), at least in those dim centuries that immediately followed the composition of the Atharvan. Charaka, too, in connection with his discourse on the development of the fetal body, has cited the opinion of Dhanvantari (1) on the subject (the same as promoted in the *Sushruta Samhita*) and referred his disciples to the Dhanvantari school of surgeons (meaning Sushruta and his school) in cases where surgical aid and knowledge are necessary; this proves that Sushruta lived before Charaka.

**Sushruta as a Surgeon:**—Sushruta was emphatically a surgeon, and the *Sushruta Samhita* is the only complete book we have that deals with the problems of practical surgery and midwifery. Almost all the other *Samhitas* written by Sushruta's fellow students are either lost to us or are only imperfectly preserved. To Sushruta may be attributed the glory of elevating the art of handling a lancet or forceps to the status of a practical science, and it may not be out of place here to give a short history of the Ayurveda as it was practiced and understood in pre-Sushrutic times, if only to emphasize the improvements he introduced in every branch of medical science.

**Commentators of the Sushruta Samhita:**—We would be guilty of ingratitude if we closed this portion of our dissertation without expressing a deep sense of our obligation to Jejjada Acharya, Gayadasa, Bhaskara, Madhava, Brahmadeva, Dallana, and Chakrapani Datta—the celebrated commentators and scholars of the *Samhita*—who have labored much to make the book a repository of priceless

**Origin and History of the Áyurveda:**—In the science of medicine, as in all other branches of study, the ancient Áryans claim to have derived their knowledge from the gods through direct revelation. Suśhruta, in his *Samhitá*, has described the Áyurveda as a subdivision (<term>Upánga</term>) of the Atharvan (1), while according to others, the science of the Áyurveda has its origin in the verses of the Rik Samhitá (2). Indeed, the origin of the science is lost in dim antiquity. Death and disease have existed in the world since the arrival of man; it was by following the examples of lower animals in sickness that our primitive ancestors acquired, by chance, knowledge about the properties of many valuable medicinal drugs. There is a verse in the Rigveda which shows that the lower animals were the teachers of man in matters of selecting foodstuffs and medicinal simples (3). Individual experiences in the realms of cure and hygiene were collected and codified, and thus formed the basis of the present Áyurveda. The verses in the Vedas clearly mark each step in the progress of medical knowledge. The properties of a new drug were always praised in a Vedic verse with a regularity which enables us to pinpoint the very time when a particular drug of our <term>Materia Medica</term> first came to be of service to man (4).

# INTRODUCTION.

**Discrepancies accounted for:—** Verses on medicine, hygiene, and surgery are scattered throughout the four Vedas. Those pertaining specifically to medicine occur most frequently in the Rigveda, and perhaps for this reason Agnivesha, who was a physician, ascribed the origin of the Áyurveda to revelations in the Rik Samhitá. Precepts relating to the art and practice of surgery are found most often in the Atharvan (1), which fully accounts for Sushruta’s opinion that the Áyurveda is a subdivision of the Atharvan, as he was primarily a surgeon himself.

**Different kinds of physicians:—** Vedic India, like Ancient Egypt, recognized the principle of the division of labor among the practitioners of the healing arts. There were Shalya Vaidyas (surgeons), Bhisaks (physicians), and Bhisag-atharvans (magic doctors). We find that by the time of the Mahábháratam, which closely approaches the age of our author, the number of these groups had increased to five, which were named Rogaharas (physicians), Shalyaharas (surgeons), Vishaharas (poison curers), Krityaharas (demon-doctors), and Bhisag-atharvans (2).

In the Vedic age (before the time of Sushruta), physicians had to go out into the open streets, calling out for patients (3). They lived in houses surrounded by gardens of medicinal herbs. The Rigveda mentions the names of a thousand and one medicinal drugs (4). Verses praising the virtues of water as a universal healer, and of certain trees and herbs as purifiers of the atmosphere, are not uncommon in the Vedas. Indeed, the rudiments of embryology, midwifery, child management (pediatrics), and sanitation were formu-

<warning>The Sanskrit text in the footnote is slightly blurred and uses an archaic typeface.</warning>

lated in the age of the Vedas and Bráhmanas, and we shall soon see how from these meager and disorganized materials, Sushruta created a science and a Samhitá that command the admiration of the world even after thousands of years of human progress.

**Origin of Ayurvedic Surgery:**—In India, as in all other countries, curative spells and healing mantras preceded medicine <note>(1) Berdoe's *Origin of the Healing Art*, and Sir John Lubbock's *Prehistoric Times*.</note>; and the first medical practitioner in India was a priest, a Bhisag Atharvan, who held a higher social position than a surgeon. The first Aryan settlements in the Punjab were often attacked by the dark indigenous inhabitants of the country, and in the wars that followed, surgeons frequently had to attend to the Aryan chiefs and soldiers. Thus, in the Rigveda (2) we find that legs were amputated and replaced with iron substitutes, injured eyes were removed, and arrow shafts were extracted from the limbs of the Aryan warriors. Indeed, we have reason to believe that many difficult surgical operations were successfully performed, though some of them sound almost incredible. But although the help of surgery was constantly sought, surgeons were not often allowed to associate with the Bráhmanic society of Vedic India. The author hints at this when he says that it was during the wars between the gods and demons that the Ashvins, the surgeons of heaven, were not entitled to any sacrificial offering until they had made themselves eligible for it by reattaching the head of the god of sacrifice to his decapitated body. The story of the progress of Ayurvedic surgery is long and interesting, but it must be enough here to mention that with the

->तच्चा मक्षोणासव्याविवच्च भाषत्त दसाभिमज्ञापयन्नं ॥<-

<header>INTRODUCTION. xv</header>

With the return of peace, the small Aryan settlements grew in number and prosperity. Rich Aryan nobles now traveled in stately carriages; because there were constant accidents, a class of surgeons arose who devoted themselves exclusively to treating injured animals. These surgeons, no longer needed in camps and on battlefields, had to attend to wealthy ladies at baronial castles during childbirth. The magic doctor (Bhisag Atharvan), who could soothe fevers and concoct love potions <note>(1)</note>, was considered the greatest among them. However, the Vedic Aryans possessed a regular arsenal against pain and suffering that is in no way inferior to our modern Materia Medica. We shall discuss that later in connection with the therapeutics of Sushruta.

**The scope and nature of Sushruta's Surgery:**—So much for the history of Vedic surgery. It is in the *Sushruta Samhita* that we first encounter a systematic method for organizing the surgical experiences of earlier surgeons and collecting the scattered facts of the science from the vast range of Vedic literature. Sushruta had no desire to abandon the Vedas to obscurity and embark on an independent voyage of discovery. The crude methods and even cruder cutting implements, such as bits of glass and bamboo skins described in the *Samhita*, may be relics of a primitive instrumentology favored by our ancestors long before any verse of the *Rigveda* was composed. Practical surgery requires a good knowledge of practical anatomy. The butchered animals at the Vedic sacrifices provided excellent materials **for the framing of a comparative anatomy** <note>(2)</note>. Sushruta devoted his whole life to the pursuit of surgery proper, to

->Rigveda Samhita. Book 10, Hymn 145, Verse 1.<-</note>

<keywords>Aryan settlements, Bhisag Atharvan, Materia Medica, Sushruta Samhita, Vedic surgery, anatomy, Rigveda, Aitareya Brahmana</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sushruta's surgical methodology, specifically his classification of operations, the design of surgical instruments, and early practices resembling modern antiseptic techniques.</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

to which he brought a mind filled with insightful analogies from lower animals. It was he who first classified all surgical operations into five different kinds and grouped them under headings such as <term>Aharya</term> (extractions of solid bodies), <term>Bhedya</term> (excising), <term>Chhedya</term> (incising), <term>Eshya</term> (probing), <term>Lekhya</term> (scarifying), <term>Sivya</term> (suturing), <term>Vedhya</term> (puncturing), and <term>Visrávaniya</term> (evacuating fluids). The surgery of Sushruta recognizes 125 different instruments, modeled after the shapes of beasts and birds, and authorizes the surgeon to devise new instruments according to the requirements of each case. The qualifications and equipment of a surgeon are practically the same as those recommended at the present time. A light refreshment is directed to be given to the patient before a surgical operation, while abdominal operations and operations in the mouth are advised to be performed while the patient is fasting. Sushruta directs the sick room to be fumigated with the vapors of white mustard, bdellium, <term>Nimva</term> leaves, and resinous gums of <term>Shála</term> trees, etc., which foreshadows the antiseptic (germ) theory of modern times. The number of surgical implements described in the *Samhita* is certainly small in comparison with the almost inexhaustible resources of Western surgery, and one may naturally be led to suspect the authenticity of the glorious achievements claimed to have been performed by the surgeons of the past; but then their knowledge of the properties and virtues of drugs was so great that cases which are considered surgical today were cured with the help of medicines applied internally. "*Tantram*," says the *Tantram*, is mutilation, not healing (1). It should only be employed when the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sushruta's surgical methodology, focusing on the specific operations he performed and his influence on modern plastic surgery and ophthalmology.</meta>

<page-num>xvii</page-num>
<header>• INTRODUCTION.</header>
<sig>B</sig>

When the affected vital energy is not strong enough to effect a cure alone, the surgeon is justified in using his knife. We find in the *Samhita* that ophthalmic, obstetric, and other operations were performed with the utmost skill and caution.

**Plastic and Rhinoplastic Operations:** —Doctor Hirschberg of Berlin says, "The whole of plastic surgery in Europe took a new flight when these ingenious devices of Indian workmen became known to us." The transplanting of sensate skin-flaps is also an entirely Indian method (Sushruta, *Sutrasthanam*, Ch. XVI). It is Sushruta who first successfully demonstrated the feasibility of mending a clipped earlobe with a patch of sensate skin-flap scraped from the neck or the adjoining part.

To Sushruta is attributed the glory of discovering the art of cataract-couching, which was unknown to the surgeons of ancient Greece and Egypt. Limbs were amputated, abdominal sections were performed, fractures were set, and dislocations, hernias, and ruptures were reduced. Hemorrhoids and fistulas were removed, and we take pride in saying that the methods recommended in the *Sushruta Samhita* sometimes prove more successful than those adopted by the surgeons of modern Europe, as we shall have the opportunity to observe later. In cases where the intestines are injured, Sushruta advises that "the protruded part should be gently replaced by *following with the finger.*" A surgeon should enlarge the wound, if necessary, by means of a knife.

<page-num>xviii</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

When the intestine is severed, the severed parts should be held together by applying living black ants to their ends. Then, their bodies should be cut off, leaving only the heads to serve the same purpose that animal tissue like catgut is expected to fulfill in modern, improved European surgery. After this, the intestine should be carefully replaced in the abdominal cavity, and the external opening should be stitched and properly dressed. We will refrain here from a lengthy description of the different methods recommended by Sushruta in cases of abdominal and peritoneal wounds. We only ask our readers to compare this chapter (*Chikitsasthanam*, Chapter II) of the *Sushruta Samhita* with the chapter in any work on European surgery that deals with the same subject. Certain medicinal plasters were used to localize the shafts of arrows embedded in the limbs of wounded soldiers; their exact locations were determined by the inflammation caused by applying such a plaster, with a precision that would sometimes be welcome even in these days of X-rays.

**Lithotomic Operations:** —In these cases, elaborate instructions have been given for making the perineal incision, as well as for the care and general management of the patient after the operation. In a case of <term>Shukrashmari</term> (seminal or spermatic concretion)—the formation and existence of which have been very recently discovered by English pathologists—Sushruta mandates that the stone, if in the urethra, should be removed with the help of <term>Anuvasanam</term> <note>A medicinal oil enema.</note> and urethral enemas. If these fail, the penis should be cut open and the concretion extracted with the help of a hook. Kaviraj Umesh Chandra Gupta, in the introduction to his *Vaidyaka Shabda-Sindhu*, remarks that he and Dr. Durgadasa Gupta, M.B., translated the chapters on lithotomic operations and instrumental childbirth of the *Sushruta Samhita* for the review of Dr. Charles, the then-Principal of the Medical College in Calcutta.

It emphasizes the sophistication of Sushruta's methods, comparing them to modern European surgery and the use of X-rays for locating embedded objects.</summary>

<page-num>xix</page-num>
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

"Dr. Charles highly praised the process of delivery in difficult cases and even confessed that, with all his great experience in midwifery and surgery, he never had any idea of the like being found in all the medical works that came under his observation."

**Amputation:** — Amputations were performed frequently, and medicated wines were given to the patients as anesthetics <note>(1) For the use of <term>Sammohinis</term> (anesthetics) for surgical purposes, see <term>Bhoja Prabandha</term> by <term>Ballala Pandit</term>.</note>. These conclusively show that the surgery of Sushruta does not rest content with the mere lancing or opening of an abscess and the healing of the resulting wound, but also establishes procedures for major operations. The removal of the scar (<term>cicatrix</term>) until it becomes the same color as the surrounding skin and the encouragement of hair growth upon it are suggestions which we find nowhere else.

**Ophthalmic Surgery:** — Of the seventy-six varieties of eye diseases, Sushruta maintains that fifty-one are surgical (<term>Uttara Tantram</term>, Chapter VIII). The mode of operation to be performed in each case has been elaborately described in the <term>Samhita</term>, and in most instances, it does not compare unfavorably with modern methods of ophthalmic surgery. Sushruta was aware of the fact that the angle of reflection is equal to the angle of incidence, and that the same ray which strikes the retina serves the double purpose of illuminating the eye and the external world, and is in itself converted into the sensation of light.

**Midwifery:** — It is in the field of practical midwifery that one becomes so impressed with the greatness of Sushruta. The different turning, flexing, and gliding movements, the application of forceps in cases of difficult labor, and other obstetric operations involving the destruction and mutilation of the child—such as craniotomy—were first systematically described in the <term>Sushruta Samhita</term> long before fillets and forceps were even dreamed of in Europe, and thousands of years before the birth of Christ. Sushruta, who

<page-num>xx</page-num>
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<header>->INTRODUCTION.<-</header>

advocates Caesarean section in hopeless cases of obstruction, and lays down the rule that the instrument should be employed only in those cases where the proportion between the child and the maternal passage is so defective that medicated plasters, fumigations, etc., are not sufficient to effect a natural delivery. His directions regarding the management of the postpartum state, lactation, the care of the child, and the choice of a wet-nurse are substantially the same as those found in modern scientific works by European authors. A feeling of pride and joy moves our hearts when we contrast these glorious achievements of our ancestors with the meager results which modern Europe has gained in this department of midwifery. In those old days, perhaps there were no hospitals to huddle patients together in the same room and thereby artificially create septicemic poisons, which are now so common and so fatal in maternity rooms. A newly built delivery room in an open space, abundantly supplied with the rays of the sun and the heat of a burning fire for each individual case, and the recommendation of a fresh bamboo-chip for the cutting of the umbilical cord, are suggestions the value of which the West has yet to learn from the East.

**Dissection:** — Sushruta, himself a practical surgeon, was the first to advocate the dissection of dead bodies as indispensable for a successful student of Surgery. The *Paruschittas* of ancient Egypt perhaps learned their art from the *Puruschattas* (Dissectors) of ancient India. With a candor less common among Western scholars, Dr. Wise observes that, "the Hindu philosophers undoubtedly deserve the credit of having, though opposed by strong prejudice, entertained sound and philosophical views respecting the uses of the dead to the living, and were the first scientific and successful cultivators of the most important and essential of all the departments of medical knowledge: practical anatomy." A bungling surgeon is a public danger, and Sushruta says that, "theory without practice is like a one-winged bird that is incapable of flight"—

<page-num>XXI</page-num>
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<header>->INTRODUCTION.<-</header>

**Study of Practical Surgery: —** To provide efficiency in surgical operations, the pupils of Dhanvantari (Sushruta, etc.) were required to practice with their knives repeatedly on natural and artificial objects resembling the diseased parts of the body before undertaking an actual operation. Incision, for example, was practiced on *Pushpafala* (*Cucurbita maxima*), *Alábu* (*Lagenaria vulgaris*), or *Trapusha* (*Cucumis pubescens*); practicing drainage on leather bags full of water and on the urinary bladders of dead animals; and scarification on the hides of animals where the hair was left in place. Venesection (bloodletting) was practiced on the vessels of dead animals and on the stalks of the water lily; the art of stuffing and probing on bamboo reeds, etc.; the extraction of solid bodies on *Panasa* (*Artocarpus integrifolia*) and similar fruits; scraping on wax spread on a *Shálmali* (*Bombax malabaricum*) plank; and suturing (stitching) on pieces of cloth, skin, or hide. Ligaturing (tying) and bandaging were practiced on dummies; cauterization (both actual and potential) on pieces of flesh; and catheterization on unbaked earthen vessels filled with water. It is almost with a feeling of wonder that we hear him talk of the removal of uterine growths and speak on the necessity of exercising caution when surgically operating on uterine tumors (*Raktárvuda*). These facts should be kept in mind, as they help us greatly in accounting for the numerous anomalies found in the anatomical sections of the *Samhita*.

**Study of Practical Anatomy: —** As previously stated, the quartered sacrificial animals provided excellent materials for the development of comparative anatomy. The *Aitareya Bráhmana* contains specific instructions for the quartering of such animals (1), and we are told that the teachers took advantage of religious meetings to

demonstrate the lessons on practical anatomy. We come across such terms as the heart, stomach, brain, intestines, anus, liver, spleen, uterus, etc., in the *Rigveda* and the *Aitareya Brahmana* (1). There is an entire hymn (*Rik*) devoted to the subject and treatment of Phthisis (*Rája Yakshma*) which becomes completely unintelligible without an accurate knowledge of the structure of the lungs and the mechanism of the human heart. The Vedic Aryans fully understood the composite nature of the human organism. The *Rik Mantra*, which to this day is recited during funeral ceremonies, amply testifies to the fact that they used to look upon the mortal frame as the product of the combination of the five physical elements (2). They understood the effects of different drugs upon digestion and the functions which the tendons, muscles, flesh, and nerves, etc., respectively serve in the bodily system. It is in the *Sushruta Samhitá* that we find a systematic attempt at arranging the facts of anatomical observation. The age of Sushruta, the *Acháryic* age of the Ayurveda, was a period of scientific investigation. The sturdy Aryan colonists exchanged their simple mode of living for luxury and ease. The number of general diseases was great. In

In vain did the holy Narada (1) preach the gospel of plain living and high thinking, and exhort them, like Cato, to return to their simple mode of life. The long peace brought opulence in its train, and wealth gave rise to indolence and disease. Men like Bharadvaja, Angira, Yamadagni, Atreya, Gautama, Agastya, Vamadeva, Kapisthala, Asamarthya, Bhargava, Kushika, Kapya, Kashyapa, Sharkaraksha, Shaunaka, Manmathayani, Agnivesha, Charaka, Sushruta, Narada, Pulastya, Asita, Chyavana, Paingi, and Dhaumya, etc., began to write Samhitas. Each hermitage was a College of Ayurveda, and the empirical method of investigation was introduced into every department of the science of healing.

**Anatomical Anomalies in the Samhita:** Having come this far in our analysis, before passing on to the study of the anatomical portion of the Sushruta Samhita, we must try to account for the many anomalies and discrepancies that have crept into or have been allowed to remain in the present version of the book. Take, for example, the line in which Dhanvantari is made to speak of three hundred bones in the human organism. It is impossible that the human frame, in so short a time, has lost so many of its skeletal parts simply through disuse, or because of them becoming superfluous in the altered conditions of its environment. It is even more absurd to think that Sushruta, who discards all authority except the evidence of direct observation, would write something that no one but the blind would believe in a dissecting room. The spirit of the age in which he flourished made the possibility of such an error unlikely.

<margin>(2) The command of the Hindu Shastras is that "the corpse of persons more than 2 years old should be burned." Since the cremation of dead bodies</margin>
**Anomalies accounted for:** In ancient India, subjects chosen for the demonstration of practical anatomy were always children (2), and naturally those bones, which are

fused or anastomosed into one whole during adult life, have been listed separately—a circumstance which may, to some extent, account for the excess in the number of bones described in this Samhita (1). Likewise, the theory that Sushruta might have included the teeth and the cartilages within the list of skeletal bones comes very near the truth, but it does not reflect the whole truth either. The fact is that the original Sushruta Samhita has passed through several versions; and we have reasons to believe that the present one by Nagarjuna is neither the only nor the last one made. The editors, according to their own understanding, have made many additions to the text, and when they were Brahmins, they tried to reach a sort of compromise on points of disagreement with the teachings of the Vedas (2). Therefore, we encounter such statements in the Samhita as "there are 360 bones in the human body, so it is in the Vedas, but the science of surgery recognizes three hundred skeletal bones." What gives more weight to the hypothesis is that Sushruta, who in the Chapter on <term>Marma Shariram</term>, has so accurately described the unions of bones and ligaments, connections of nerves, veins, and arteries, etc.,

<note>...obligatory for the Government, as well as for private individuals, it was almost impossible to obtain a full-grown anatomical subject in Pauranic India, especially when we consider that Hindus look upon the lack of cremation and the mutilation of a corpse with a particular horror, as it prevents the spirit from cleansing its impurity in the funeral fire and bars its access to a higher spiritual life. Naturally, in later and more ceremonial times, the buried corpses of infants less than two years old had to be exhumed and dissected for anatomical purposes; and these portions of the Sushruta Samhita might have been modified by subsequent commentators in order to make them conform to visual evidence.—T. <meta>This page continues the introduction's analysis of the Sushruta Samhita, moving from a discussion of skeletal anatomy to the vital points (Marmas) and the historical context of biological study in ancient India.</meta>

<page-num>xxv</page-num>

must have described their courses and locations; otherwise, it would have been quite impossible for practical surgeons, for whom it was intended, to follow the directions of the <term>Samhita</term> when surgically operating on their patients' limbs and to avoid those vulnerable unions or anastomoses as directed there. These <term>Marmas</term> have been divided into three classes: the <term>Sadya-prana-hara</term>, <term>Kala-prana-hara</term>, and <term>Vaikalya-hara</term>, depending on whether an injury to any of them proves immediately fatal, fatal over time, or results in a maimed condition of the affected limb. The fact is that the study of practical anatomy was essentially forbidden during the reign of <term>Ashoka Piyadarshi</term>, as all religious sacrifices were prohibited by a royal decree (1), and the later commentators (who also acted as minor editors) of the <term>Sushruta Samhita</term>, lacking any certain knowledge on the subject, had to find their way through the darkness as best they could. This led to the careless corruption of texts and hopeless confusion of verses in the <term>Sharira Sthanam</term> of the modern <term>Sushruta Samhita</term>, which must be rearranged and restored to their proper chapters before any definite opinion can be given regarding the anatomical knowledge of the holy <term>Sushruta</term>.

**<term>Sushruta</term> as a Biologist:**— In the first chapter of his <term>Sharira Sthanam</term>, <term>Sushruta</term> discusses the questions: What is man? Wherein lies his individuality? Why does he come into being? Why does he die at all? Like all Indian philosophers, <term>Sushruta</term> argues the question down from the universe to man. The factors or laws that govern the evolution of the universe in its physical aspect are extended to cover the evolution of the physical aspect of man (organic evolution). There is only one law and one force that run through the three planes of mind, matter, and spirit. Physiology, which fails to look

---

<summary>This page describes the classification of Marmas (vital points) and explains how the prohibition of sacrifices under Emperor Ashoka led to a decline in anatomical knowledge and the corruption of medical texts. It also introduces Sushruta’s view of man as a product of the same evolutionary laws that govern the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Marmas, anatomy, Ashoka Piyadarshi, Sushruta Samhita, organic evolution, Sharira Sthanam, biology, physiology</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction's exploration of Sushruta as a biologist, transitioning from his inquiry into the nature of man to a comparison between ancient Indian concepts and modern biological theories regarding life and the soul.</meta>

<page-num>xxvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

into the nature of life and its background and tries to explain away this intelligent, living force as the product of chemical action in organic cells, is no physiology at all. A cell is not life, but there is life within a cell. Cells may be called the true bearers of life. Dr. Weismann insists that it is more correct to speak of the continuity of the general protoplasm than of "the germ cells." Professors Geddes and Thomson observe that "the bodies are but the torches which burn out, while the living flame has passed throughout the organic series unextinguished. The bodies are the leaves which fall in death from the continuously growing branch." Thus, although death takes an inexorable grasp of the individual, the continuation of life is still, in a deep sense, unaffected; the reproductive elements (cells) have already claimed their protozoan immortality and are already <unclear>creating</unclear> a new body. But to grant these reproductive cells immortality, and to deny the same to the individual self—which directs and controls these protoplasms, and exists before and behind them—is like the statement of Prof. Huxley when he admits the possibility of the physical transmigration of the organic constituents of the human body, and yet denies the possibility of an individual self continuing in any other form. "It is sensibility," observes <term>Sushruta</term>, "that precedes the senses and self; sensibility proceeds from the <unclear>atman</unclear> to which all such conditions are referred as 'mine.'"

**Sushruta's Theory of Cosmogony** is based on the old <term>Sankhya</term> duality of <term>Prakriti</term> (Objective) and <term>Purusha</term> (Subjective). The two are eternal and co-extensive realities. Out of the <term>Avyakta</term> (unmanifested) <term>Prakriti</term> has evolved the <term>Mahat</term> (the <unclear>animating soul of the world</unclear> matter). Out of this cosmic matter has evolved <term>Ahamkara</term> (the sense of individuality or <unclear>mere conscious egoism</unclear>), which is divided into three kinds: the <term>Vaikarika</term> (phenomenal, thought-form), <term>Taijasa</term> (kinetic), and <term>Bhutadi</term> (pertaining to the first form of matter). Thus, <term>Vaikarika Ahamkara</term> in combination with the <term>Taijasa Ahamkara</term> has fathered

<summary>This page compares modern biological theories of protoplasmic continuity with Sushruta’s views on the individual self and sensibility. It also introduces Sushruta's cosmogony, which is rooted in Sankhya philosophy and the evolution of matter from Prakriti and Purusha.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction's exploration of Indian biological and cosmological theories, specifically detailing the derivation of the senses and the nature of the "Self" (Purusha) in relation to the material world.</meta>

<page-num>xxvii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

the eleven sense organs, which, in combination with the <term>Bhutadi</term>, have produced the five <term>Tanmitras</term> (the "proper sensibles" of touch, sight, hearing, etc.). The material principles of sound, light, taste, smell, etc., are but the modifications of these five <term>Tanmitras</term>, of which <term>Akásha</term> (ether), <term>Váyu</term> (air), light, and sound, etc., are the grosser forms. In other words, these <term>Tanmitras</term> may be defined as the atomic essences of the material principles of sound, light, ether, and so on. In addition to these, <term>Sushruta</term>, like <term>Kapila</term>, admits the existence of a kind of atom-like units of consciousness, which he calls <term>Purusha</term>. The combination of the sixteen aforesaid categories and the <term>Purusha</term> is for the expansion and liberation of the latter. A human being (the individual), who is the proper subject for medical treatment, is the product of the combination of <term>Purusha</term> with the five primordial material principles (<term>Mahabhutas</term>). The <term>Purushas</term>—the real selves of beings, the sources of their vital energy, and the controllers and directors of all organic or mental actions—are extremely subtle in their essence and manifest themselves only through the combination of the seed (paternal element) and the ovum (maternal element). It is <term>Karma</term> (the dynamics of acts performed by a person in a prior existence) which determines the nature of the body it will be clothed with, as well as the nature of the womb it shall be conceived in during its next incarnation.

### Nature of Self :—

Self is a simple substance, and, as such, is immaterial. Force is substance and substance is force. It is endowed with constructive intelligence and, like gravitation or cohesion, can permeate a material body without disturbing it in any way. It is adaptive or elective; in other words, it chooses the kind of parents for its selves that are best suited to the purposes of its being. Man is the outcome of an influx of a self, a force, a dynamis with its path determined by the dynamics of the deeds of its prior existence. To think that vitality starts from

<summary>This page explains the Sankhya-based cosmology of Sushruta, detailing how the sense organs and material principles arise from Tanmitras and the role of the Purusha (Self) and Karma in human incarnation. It describes the Self as an immaterial, intelligent force that permeates the physical body and is directed by the actions of previous lives.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s exploration of the nature of the "Self" and its relationship to the material body, transitioning from a metaphysical discussion of consciousness to the specific principles of Ayurvedic embryology and the biological constituents of life.</meta>

<page-num>xxviii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

<term>Protoplasm</term> is insanity. Chemically examined, <term>protoplasm</term> is only C, O, H, N, and S. But no amount of Carbon, Oxygen, Hydrogen, Nitrogen, and Sulfur put together will constitute life. The idea that life has nothing prior to it—that the force which controls the coordination of a person's physiological system perishes with the death of the organism—is quite childish. Life is expansion and not creation; as such, it is linked to those unseen realities which constitute its prior and future selves. We see only the middle link in the chain of existence which we call life, but we take no notice of the preceding or succeeding ones which are invisible <note>1</note>. The coarser material body is linked to a finer, immaterial one, inasmuch as nothing can exist without being attached to its antecedent. So, at each conception, there is an influx of a new self, for the lifeless components of a human body cannot create a human being, no matter how many chemical or physiological actions may be claimed to come to their rescue.

### Ayurvedic Embryology:—

Before entering into the discussion of <term>Sushruta's</term> theory of conception, we shall take a little more trouble to fully explain the Vedic theories on the subject. "The child is the fruit of the combination of <term>sperm</term> and <term>ovum</term>" <note>2</note>. It lies with its head downward inside the uterus—a fact which facilitates its passage out and protects its form from the effects of any injury done to that internal organ <note>3</note>. <meta>This page continues the introduction’s exploration of fetal development, moving from the physical positioning of the child in the womb to the mythological and physiological factors described in the Rig Veda that govern conception and growth.</meta>

<language>English, Sanskrit</language>
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>xxix</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

as the cephalic portion of the fetal body is first developed. The factors that are essential to the development of the fetal body, from the time of fecundation to the appearance of the characteristic sense organs, have been described in a verse of the Rig Veda <note>1</note>. In Vedic mythology, each organic function is dedicated to the guardianship of a presiding deity, and a Vedic Aryan preferred to call a thing by the name of its divine custodian more often than by its own name. Properly translated, the verse reads as follows:

> "May Vishnu (the presiding deity of ether and nerve force) expand your uterus; may Tvashtá (the presiding deity of heat and metabolism) bring about the full differentiation of the limbs and the sex of the fetus; may Prajápati (the presiding deity of the ovum) sprinkle your uterus, and may you conceive through the blessing of the lord of human destiny. May Sarasvatí (goddess of intellect) and the Ashvins, the surgeons of the gods (the presiding deities of fission, etc.), help you in receiving the seed."

Now, the development of the fetal body follows the pattern of its father's species, and this conformity to the pattern of its species represents an act of intelligence. Therefore, the aid of the goddess of intellect has been invoked along with that of the celestial surgeons, who preside over the process of cell division, which is so essential to the formation of the fetal limbs. Stripped of its allegory, the verse means:

> <note>1</note> विष्णोर्योनिं कल्पयतु त्वष्टारूपाणि पिंशतु ।
> आसिंचतु प्रजापतिर्धाता गर्भं दधातु ते ॥
> गर्भं धेहि सिनीवाली, गर्भं धेहि सरस्वति ।
> गर्भन्ते अश्विनौदेवावाधत्तां पुष्करस्रजा ॥
> हिरण्ययी अरणी यं निर्मथतो अश्विना ।
> तं ते गर्भं हवामहे दशमे मासि सूतवे ॥

->Rik Samhitá X. M. 184, S.<-

When we consider the many idle speculations regarding the process of fertilization that were believed as late as the beginning of the 18th century in Europe, and the controversies that arose between the Ovists, Preformists, and Animalculists <note>1. The fundamental principles upon which the Embryology of the Acharyas (Sushruta, Dhanvantari, etc.) was founded are substantially the same as those recently discovered through the research of Western scientists. Sushruta, in his dissertation on the subject, showed the flaws at the root of his predecessors' theories (Sharirasthanam, Chapter II) and continued research from exactly where the Vedic Rishis had left off. He clearly demonstrated the fact that:

> "Through a physiological process known as Rasapaka (metabolism), the lymph chyle is transformed into sperm in men, or into the ovum in women, over the course of a month. The menstrual fluid is carried down into the uterus through its proper ducts. The sperm or ovum is thus the essence of a man's or a woman's body. The sperm meets the ovum (Artavam) in the uterus, which resembles a lotus bud in shape, and whose opening is closed with a mucous deposit as soon as fertilization takes place. The most favorable time for fertilization is between the fourth and twelfth day after the appearance of the flow (Garbhakala)"

**Sexual Dimorphism:—** Some light is shed on the relative dominance of the sperm and ovum in the birth of a female child. "When the maternal element predominates, the child is female; when the paternal element is stronger, the child is male. When both elements are equal, the child is of no sex." In theory at least, Sushruta admits the possibility of the birth of many children from a single conception. "When the seed is divided into two by its inherent force (*Vayu*), twins are born in the womb"—a statement which points to the unavoidable conclusion that multiple births are the result of the multifarious fission of the seed in the womb under certain abnormal conditions. Sushruta gives a reason for believing that, in exceptional circumstances and without sexual union, the unfertilized ovum may give rise to perfect offspring, thus providing a foresight of the modern theory of parthenogenesis. Pathological parthenogenesis has occasionally been observed in higher animals. Oellacher has noted this in hen's eggs, and Janosik has observed it in the ovarian ova of many mammals, such as the guinea pig, etc.<note>2. *The Evolution of Sex*, Chapter XIII, p. 185. *Prof. P. Geddes and J. A. Thompson.*</note> Sushruta extends this probability to human ova under certain conditions. He admits the possibility of conception without the addition of the male germinal element, though he observes that, like all asexual generation, "the development does not proceed far in this case." From such a hypothesis, it is but one step to the theory that states the possibility of conception without actual sexual union.

But to understand his theory of sexual dimorphism, it

II.</note> (**preponderance of the male reproductive element**) and **Shonita-Váhulyam** (**preponderance of the female reproductive element**) etc. Sushruta, in common with the Brahmanic philosophers of India, believed that the distinction of sex has evolved from a primordial hermaphroditism. Manu, in his Institutes, has emphasized the fact <note>2. "Dividing his own body into two, he became male with the one half and female with the other; and in her, the Lord created Viraj." -> Manu Samhitá Ch. I. 32. <-</note>, though in a highly poetic style. He observes that

> "the Purusha (Logos), by a stroke of Will, divided its body (animated cosmic matter) into two, one of which was male, and the other female."

The Tantra says that, 

> "the male part was endowed with an energy (force) of its own, which is called **Pitriká Shakti**; and the corresponding female part, with another, which is called **Mátriká Shakti.**"

**Pitriká Shakti** is a disruptive force; **Mátriká Shakti** is a constructive energy. Although the conception of force in Sanskrit sciences is only partially physical, the nearest approach to the connotations of the Pitriká and Mátriká Shakti is made by the terms **anabolism** and **katabolism** of Western physiologists. Sanskrit physiology recognizes the two opposite poles of vital force in a living organism, and has taken considerable pains to determine their exact locations in man and woman. Mátriká Shakti, it observes, predominates in the left half of a woman's organism, which is negative regarding vital magnetism. <note>3. "The right part is Rajas, the Sun; the left part is the Moon." -> Sáradá Tilaka Tantram. <-</note> Now, Sushruta says that, in cases where female offspring is desired, the expectant mother should

The original hermaphroditism, which forms the prior condition of all subsequent sex distinctions, and the character of the two opposite poles of vital energy, have been very clearly set forth in the Pauranik allegory of **Ardha-Nárishvara** <note>1. The Rishis and Rasasiddhas of ancient India were fully aware of the fact that conception is achieved only at an enormous sacrifice on the part of the mother; that the Mátriká Shakti is the real manufacturer of life, and that the Pitriká Shakti (paternal element) evokes or calls it into play only through its disintegrating or disruptive effect by separating the two opposite life-poles that lie neutralized through contact. Man is both animal and spirit; and Ayurvedic physiology recognizes two distinct sets of systems in his organism that correspond to the different phases of his existence. One helps him perform organic functions which are so essential to his animal existence and maintains the coordination of those internal functions with the events of his environment. The one chains him down to the phenomenal and is governed by the laws of growth and decay; the other opens onto the region of absolute realities where growth and decay have no place. Growth is not the only condition of life. Man may exist without food <note>(1)</note> or respiration, but only if he can manage to dive deep into the realities within himself. Between these two sets of systems there is the <term>Jivátmá</term>, which, by its own peculiar energy (the will-force), can operate in the phenomenal or organic plane, or recede from there into the psychic one, thus being in contact with both the world of the senses and the one that is beyond the darkness of death. Death, in fact, is the great usher to life, which is only the rise of the curtain over life's drama, for which all preparations are made in the green room of death.

A man cannot propagate at will. No amount of willing on the part of the parent-animal can help him in creating offspring. The self of the child, who is about to come into life, chooses its own parents according to the dynamics of its own actions or <term>Karma</term>, from the region of the lunar <term>Pitris</term> or quiescent life, if it is permissible to use such an expression <note>(2)</note>. The self of the future child mixes with the self of its human father and hovers over the reproductive cells of the father's organism, regulating the intensity of the father's sexual desire according to the nature of the sex determined necessary for fulfilling the purposes of its arrival into the world. A greater intensity of the father's desires ensures the dominance of the <term>Pitriká Shakti</term> (catabolism) in the impregnated ovum, which

determines the male sex of the child. Conversely, such a state on the part of the mother at that time is followed by the relative dominance of the <term>Mátriká Shakti</term> (anabolism), which accounts for the femininity of the offspring. Equal intensity of sexual desire in both parents, creating an absence of the relative dominance of the <term>Pitriká</term> and <term>Mátriká Shaktis</term> in the fertilized ovum, leaves the sex of the child practically undetermined. The relative dominance of the <term>Pitriká</term> or <term>Mátriká Shakti</term>—as evidenced by the greater or lesser intensity of the sexual desire of either parent, which results in the quicker emission of the paternal or maternal element (sperm or ovum) during an act of successful conception—is described by the terms "<term>Shukra-Váhulyam</term>" or "<term>Shonita-Váhulyam</term>" by the author of the <term>Samhitá</term>, as can be fully supported by a couplet from the venerable <term>Dáruváhi</term> <note>(1)</note>.

In this regard, <term>Sushruta</term> agrees with the modern Western theory of dominant **katabolism** or **anabolism** in the ovum as the determining factor of sexual dimorphism, to the extent that seeds or reproductive cells are the carriers and not the creators of life; they only contain those categories which foster life and assist its evolution into an organic being. To deny this would be to admit a purely chemical or physiological basis of life—a theory that was never acceptable to the biologists of ancient India.

The number of reproductive cells may be increased by a suitable diet. To say that the immortal reproductive cells, as the creators of life, originate from mortal, organic food is like saying that darkness is the father of light. The question of the immortality of the seed (germ plasm) has

<note>(1) **स्त्रीपुंसयोः सुसंयोगे यदादौ विसृजेत् पुमान् ।**

### INTRODUCTION.

has been elaborately discussed in the commentaries on the <term>Sánkhya Darshanam</term><note>(1)</note>. The <term>Ojah Vindus</term> (germ cells) pulsate with vibrations (rhythmic movements) that are the relics of the primordial ethereal vibrations which ushered in the birth-throes of the universe. As such, they are essential to the evolution of life; and man, as an offspring of the universe, still retains them in his reproductive cells as the best condition for calling out the life in his offspring when its self enters into the impregnated ovum in the mother's womb. Life is the essence of self and not the product of any chemical or physiological process. It is an influx; microscopes and spectroscopes may not reveal the hinterlands of birth and genesis. Perhaps it was this theory of will-force and the intensity of parental desire as the determining factor of sex in the child—together with the facts of parthenogenesis observed in lower animals—from which <term>Sushruta</term> was inclined to extend the analogy to the human species and believed that conception without sexual union is possible in women.

The conception of the nature of these <term>Mátriká</term> and <term>Pitriká Shaktis</term> is more clearly set forth in the <term>Pauránika</term> myth regarding the origin (etiology) of fever; <term>Sushruta</term> relates the story as follows:—<term>Daksha</term>, the father of the universal mother (or constructive metabolism in man), insulted the divine father, her consort (destructive metabolism), by withholding his quota of sacrificial oblations. The wrath of the insulted deity broke out in the form of a <term>morbific heat</term> (<term>hyperpyrexia</term>), which is fever. The process of digestion in man has often been compared to an act

# INTRODUCTION.

of the <term>Homa sacrifice</term> (1) in the <term>Āyurveda</term>. Stripped of its allegory, the myth may be explained in a quite pathological way. It means that when the <term>Pitriká Shakti</term>—the process of destructive <term>metabolism</term> in the body (<term>Pitá</term>, father, or <term>Shiva</term> in Hindu mythology, being the god of destruction or disintegration)—is not properly served by the factors which nourish its constructive <term>metabolism</term> (the Father of the <term>Matriká Shakti</term>), the waste products and the excretory processes of the body are arrested (by the wrathful deity), and the heat generated as a result is fever. Fever, then, is a disease of defective digestion and excretion. Wherever this <term>Pitriká Shakti</term> is disturbed or not properly served, fever occurs, and heat is one of its essential effects.

With a precision and love for detail that mark the best days of <term>Brāhmanic literature</term>, <term>Sushruta</term> lays down rules of diet and conduct to be observed by a pregnant woman, from month to month, during the whole period of <term>gestation</term>, and gives medicinal recipes for the development of a partially atrophied child in the womb.

A reading of the chapter on <term>Marma Shāriram</term> would leave no doubt that <term>anatomical</term> knowledge was cultivated by surgeons and soldiers alike. Knowledge about the locations of the vulnerable joints, nerves, or vein <term>anastomoses</term>, where a blow or a little pressure might enable a person to quickly defeat an opponent, could not help but be highly valued by the soldiery at a time when the fate of a war was often decided by the success of a single champion. We have reason to believe that a scientific system of wrestling was formulated in the light of the <term>Sushruta Samhitā</term> and practiced by the gentry of ancient India much like

<note>(1) He who always watches the sacred fire (Agnihotra) and offers oblations to the internal fires for twenty-six thousand nights, while eating wholesome food.

...the <term>Jiujitsu</term> (Sanskrit: <term>Yuyutsu</term>, the aspiring fighter) of modern Japan (1).

**Sushruta's Physiology:**—But if <term>Sushruta</term> is admired so much for his practical and scientific mindset, his writings on physiology (which is practically the same as the one adopted by all schools of the <term>Ayurveda</term>) have appeared as a stumbling block to the intelligence of many a Western and Eastern scholar. European Sanskrit scholars have seen fit to translate "<term>Vāyu</term>," "<term>Pitta</term>," and "<term>Kapha</term>" (the three main physiological functions) as air, bile, and phlegm. But nothing could be more misleading or erroneous than that. A right understanding of the science of <term>Ayurvedic</term> medicine, in all its branches, hinges on a right conception of the <term>Vāyu</term>, <term>Pitta</term>, and <term>Kapha</term>, so we would like to clear up the nature of these three physiological factors before proceeding further in our inquiry.

**Antiquity of the division:**—A reference to these three physiological factors of <term>Vāyu</term>, <term>Pitta</term>, and <term>Kapha</term>, under the name of <term>Tridhātu</term>, is first met with in the <term>Rig Veda</term> (2). <term>Sayana</term> explains the term as a synonym for <term>Vāyu</term>, <term>Pitta</term>, and <term>Kapha</term>. The Vedic physicians possessed at least a considerable knowledge of the process of digestion (4), the circulation of gas in the human organism, and of
