<page-num>xl</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

...the properties and functions of flesh, fat, muscles, tendons, ligaments, and cartilage. But to the <term>Acharyas</term> of the <term>Ayurveda</term> belongs the glory of first formulating a systematic physiological science, to which end <term>Sushruta</term>, as a surgeon, made a significant contribution. In the light of Western science, the actions of living matter, varied as they are, may be reduced to three categories: (*a*) Sustentative, (*b*) Generative, and (*c*) Correlative functions. The second is not co-extensive with the entire existence of a living organism. <term>Sushruta</term> observes a similar distinction among the functions of a living organism when he names the living body as the "three-supported one" (*<term>Tristhunam</term>*), and describes the normal <term>Vayu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, and <term>Kapham</term> as its three supports. We wonder how the term <term>Vayu</term>, meaning nerve force, can be confused with the same term meaning air, since <term>Sushruta</term> derives the former from the root "*Va*," meaning to move or to spread. <term>Vayu</term>, according to <term>Sushruta</term>, is so named because of its sensory and motor functions, such as smelling, etc. But the <term>Vayu</term> in the <term>Ayurveda</term> is not entirely a physical or organic force; it has its spiritual aspect as well, which does not strictly fall within the scope of our inquiry. It is safe to assert, however, that <term>Ayurvedic</term> physiology, like its sister science in modern Europe, is concerned more with the invisible molecular components of the human organism than with the workings of its larger structures. The venerable <term>Agnivesha</term> warns students of physiology against the danger of regarding the human system as something other than an aggregate of molecules (1).

(1) <note>शरीरावयवास्तु परमाणुभेदेनापरिसंख्येया भवन्ति, अतिबहुत्वादाति-
सौक्ष्म्यादतीन्द्रियत्वाच्च । [The components of the body are innumerable when divided into their constituent atoms (paramanu), due to their extreme number, their extreme subtlety, and their existence beyond the reach of the senses.]</note>

<page-num>xli</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

# **The three fundamental principles of Vàyu, Pittam and Kaphah:**

The actions of living matter vary and can be reduced to three categories. They are either: (1) functions that affect the material composition of the body and determine its mass, which is the balance between the processes of waste on one hand and those of assimilation on the other; (2) functions that support the process of reproduction, which is essentially the detachment of a part gifted with the power to develop into an independent whole; or (3) functions by virtue of which one part of the body is able to exert a direct influence on another, and the body, through its parts as a whole, becomes a source of physical (molar) motion. The first may be termed **Sustentative**, the second **Generative**, and the third **Correlative** functions. The above is the essence of the work that living matter must perform. But setting aside the processes of reproduction as a subject for future discussion, we shall now try to examine what the other two functions are as understood by Eastern thinkers. In the *Mahábháratam*, the *Prána váyu* is described as a force akin to electricity. It is something like a flash of lightning (1). This fact at once shows the error of confusing *Prána váyu* with waste material—that is, with gases generated during the processes of digestion. Sushruta describes it as a force (2) which sets the whole organism into motion. Self-evolved, it acts as the principal

> (1) प्राणनाच्चैव भूतानां प्राण इत्यभीयते ।
> प्रेरयिष्य संङ्घातान् पृथगक्षोस्रजांश्च य ॥
> प्रथनः प्रथमे मार्गे प्रवाहो नाम योऽनिलः ।
> चत्वरे क्षेपनमेत्य तद्विद्वांचीत्तमदुगातिः ॥

> ->*Mahábháratam, Shánti Parva S. 39.*<-

(2) Force may be defined as that which tends to produce motion in a body at rest, or to produce a change of motion in a body which is moving. —**Deschanel.**

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Vayu as the primary regulatory force in the living body, detailing its role in maintaining equilibrium and its fivefold classification.</meta>

<page-num>xlii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

It is the factor that determines the origin, continuation, and disintegration of the living body. It is the primary cause—an all-encompassing force that governs our organic as well as our cognitive faculties. Its special feature is that the vibration produced within it, instead of traveling like light in a transverse direction, follows a path as the controller of the system's correlative functions. It maintains an equilibrium between the *Pittam* and *Shleshmá*, which are said to be inert. (1) Without this adjustment, the living body would be in imminent danger of being consumed like fuel by its internal heat or fire. Taking into consideration the various functions the living body has to perform, Sushruta attempts a classification of *Váyu* into **Prána**, **Udána**, **Samána**, **Vyána**, and **Apána**, which correspond in detail to the divisions of functions performed by the cerebrospinal and sympathetic nerves of Western physiology. Tantric literature abounds in descriptions of the *Nádichakras* (nerve plexuses) and contains a more detailed account of the motor, sensory, and mixed nerves according to the differences in their functions and relations. In short, the term *Váyu* may not only be rightly interpreted to mean nerve force, but it is often extended to include any kind of electromotive or molecular force (as when we speak of the *Váyu* of the soil), though the term is loosely applied now to signify gas or air. The ancient Rishis gave the name *Váyu* to this bodily force because they lacked a better name, never suspecting that foreign translators of their works might confuse it with atmospheric air.

<page-num>xliii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

**Pittam:**—The function of the **Pittam** consists in transforming the chyle, through a gradual series of organic principles, into a protoplasmic substance like sperm in men and the ovum in women. Thus, we see that the **Pittam** of the **Ayurveda** corresponds to the metabolism of Western physiology. However, due to a confusing carelessness in terminology, the excreted portion of **Rasa** and blood—though ultimately connected with those normal physiological processes—has been respectively styled as the **Doshas**, or the defiling principles of Kaphah and Pittam. Again, as in the case of soil, the terms **Váyu**, Pittam, and Kaphah are extended to denote the magnetism, kinetic energy, and humidity of its molecules. The circulation of blood is connected with the Pittam, while the circulation of lymph chyle (**Rasa**) is related to **Shleshmá**, the two together forming what is called the sustentative function in Western physiology.

The term Pittam, which by its etymology signifies the agent of metabolism, has been loosely used by our Ayurvedic physiologists to denote two different organic principles based on an observed similarity in their nature and functions. Pittam in Sanskrit means both bile and the metabolism of tissues, as well as the bodily heat which is the product of the latter.

Hence, a few commentators lean toward the view that Pittam is the heat contained within the bile and the principal agent in performing digestion (1). The real meaning of the term may be gathered from the five subdivisions of the Pittam made by our Rishis according to their functions and locations, which are called the **Páchaka**, **Ranjaka**, **Sádhaka**, **A'lochaka**, and

---

तेन पित्तमव्यपदिश्यते । पतितपित्तायौ गोलकवत् ।
परमार्थतस्तु अग्नि: पित्तान्न एवेति सिद्धान्त: ॥
->Madhukosha.<-</note>

<meta>This page concludes the list of the five types of Pittam by naming the Bhrájaka, then provides a general definition of Pittam as metabolism before detailing the functions of Páchakágni (digestive heat or bile).</meta>

<page-num>xliv</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

**Bhrájaka.** All metabolic processes in the organism, whether constructive or destructive, are called Pittam. It is said to be present in the products of those processes—whether serum, bile, blood, albumen, etc.—which are either essential to the body's substance or to the proper performance of any organic function. Hence, we learn that Pittam is latent in Lasiká (serum), blood, lymph chyle, albumen, etc., and in the organs of touch and sight. In other words, metabolism occurs in those principles and regions of the human organism (1) as either a sustentative or a cognetic physiological process. First, we have the Páchakágni, or the heat of digestion, which is situated in the region between the stomach and the intestines (2); being a liquid fire or fluid heat contained within the secretions of the liver (bile), it is primarily concerned with digesting the four kinds of food (as they meet it in the abdomen). Thus, we see that the Páchakágni of our Āyurveda is the same as the bile of Western physiology; its other functions are to differentiate (precipitate) the nutritive essence of the food from its unusable portion and to act as waste matter. It is this Pittam that makes metabolism in other parts of the body possible (3) by helping the organism acquire fresh energy.

<page-num>xlvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

physiologists to hold it as the seat of cognition (*Vuddhi Sthánam*). <note>(1) The seat of the moon is at the root of the palate and that of the sun is at the root of the navel; the place of the breath is above the sun, and the mind dwells above the moon. Chittam (or the passage between the mind and the spiritual soul) dwells above the sun, and life dwells above the moon.
->**Jnána Sankalini Tantram**, International Journal of Tántrik order (New York) Vol. V. No. 5 p. 109.<-</note>

The fourth, which is the **Alochaka** or the Pittam of sight, indicates the metabolic process in the substance of the retina (*Drishti*) which gives rise to visual sensation. <note>(2) It is supposed that the change produced by the light which falls upon the retina is in fact a chemical alteration in the protoplasm, and that this stimulates the optic nerve endings. Kirk's Physiology Ch. XVII.</note>

The fifth is the **Bhrájakágni** or the Pittam in the skin which produces perspiration or helps exudations from the skin by evaporation. In short, it is the Pittam which keeps active, under certain circumstances, the secretions from the sweat and sebaceous glands of the human skin.

**Kaphaḥ** :— Sushruta agrees with Foster that "the animal body dies daily, in the sense that at every moment some part of its substance is suffering decay, is undergoing combustion." The etymological significance of the term *Shariam* (Sanskrit *Shri*, to wither up) demonstrates his knowledge of the combustion that goes on within the human system. Three kinds of fire are detected in the body, which are sure to feed upon its constituent principles in the absence of proper fuel in the shape of food and air. It is food and the fundamental bodily principle of *Shleshmá*, which is cooling or watery in its essence, that come to the rescue of the organism; the latter (*Shleshmá*) fills it with its own essential moisture and keeps the integration of its component molecules intact.

The Rasa, or lymph chyle which is formed out of the ingested food, prevents the internal bodily fires from

The **Rasa**, thus produced, undergoes a type of purification, the purified portion being called ***Prasádabhuta***, and the excreted portion ***Malabhuta***, such as those found as waste products deposited in certain pores of the body. **Kaphah** or **Sleshmá** is that portion of **Rasa** which fills all the intercellular spaces of the body, thus holding them together in a kind of cooling embrace (Sanskrit **Slish**, to embrace) and prevents (1) the dreadful combustion which would otherwise have been caused by organic heat. Our **Achárjas** <note>teachers or authorities</note> have classified the **Kaphah** into five different kinds, such as the ***Kledaka***, ***Avalamvaka***, ***Vodhaka***, and ***Shlesmaka***, according to their different functions and locations in the bodily system.

**Dosha** :—The lymph chyle, produced from the digested food, and which flows through the body, potentially contains the elements which build the different tissues of the human organism. Under the influence of metabolic heat, it is progressively transformed into blood, flesh, fat, bone, marrow, semen, and **Ojah**. In other words, through the process of physiological metamorphosis, the lymph chyle releases that part of its constituents (2) which possess blood-

<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

forming properties, and are eventually transformed into blood—its unused or excreted portion being eliminated through the body's natural openings—and so on, through the progressive stages of metabolism to *Ojah Dhátu*. Thus, when the bodily *Váyu* (which enables the lymph chyle to flow freely through its vessels) is disturbed, the *Pittam* (tissue metabolism) in any specific part of the body is also affected because it is trapped. This causes an increase or decrease in the excreted portion of the *Rasa*, which is another name for *Kapham* during the progression of metabolism. Thus, we see that *Váyu*, *Pittam*, and *Kapah*—which, in their healthy state, are the three supporting principles of the body—are transformed into a disease-causing state <note>morbific diathesis</note> by increasing or decreasing bodily heat, secretions, or excretions.†

Therefore, congestion and depletion <note>inanition or atony</note> are the two primary forms of disease recognized by Ayurvedic pathologists; the former is considered treatable by resolution or elimination, and the latter by local feeding or nourishment.

**Agni and Dhátvagnis:** We cannot conclude this part of our discussion better than by saying a word or two about *Agni*. *Sushruta* asks whether there is any kind of fire in the human organism other than *Pittam*, or if they are identical. *Sushruta* maintains that *Pittam* is the only fire present in the system, because all functions—from the digestion of food to the breakdown of tissues—are performed with the aid of *Pittam*. This term encompasses what Western physiologists mean by anabolism and catabolism. However, *Agniyesha* and certain groups of Ayurvedic *Acharyas* <note>teachers or authorities</note> believe that there

## INTRODUCTION.

...called *Dhátus* or fundamental principles of the organism, when by virtue of their correlative and sustaining functions, or with the help of their subservient processes of metabolism and lymphatic circulation, they ensure a balance among the different vital and physiological processes in the whole system which is essential to its perfect health. Biologically considered, they are but the primary subtle dynamics of organic life, or as Sáyana expresses it, the three fundamental principles of the body.* But when this healthy equilibrium is disturbed either through the agency of any external or internal factor, when any one of them is abnormally increased or dominates the other two, thus altering their mutual relation in the system, naturally certain pathological conditions arise which form the *essence* of a disease;† or in the language of the Ayurveda, they are said to have been transformed into **Doshas** or disease-causing states. Even blood, which, according to our Acharyas, forms one of the fundamental principles (Dhátu) of the organism, may be designated as a **Dosha** (disease-causing state), when, owing to its congestion in any particular organ or part of the body, it brings about a disturbance in its general vascular system and produces pathological conditions which are results of its own deficient or disturbed circulation. They are called **Malas**, ‡ when observed in even coarser or superficial principles of the organism, producing those excretions or organic *lesions* which belong to the sphere of morbid Anatomy. Thus we see that the Ayurvedic principles of Váyu, Pittam, and Kaphah embrace both the biological and pathologi-

<summary>This page defines the transition of the three vital principles—Váyu, Pittam, and Kaphah—from healthy "Dhátus" (sustaining principles) to "Doshas" (disease-causing states) and "Malas" (waste products or lesions) when the body's equilibrium is disturbed. It emphasizes that even vital fluids like blood can become pathological if their circulation is impaired or congested.</summary>

<page-num>liii</page-num>
## INTRODUCTION.

physical principles of the organism; or in other words, Ayurvedic physiology explains and investigates the causes through which the same principles that sustain life and the organism are transformed into the dynamics of disease, finally pointing out the grosser excretory changes and organic lesions on the external or superficial plane of existence, which form the subject of morbid anatomy and are sometimes confused with the disease itself. In the **Váyu**, **Pittam**, and **Kaphah** of the **Acháryayas**, we have at once a complete picture of the finer sustaining forces of the human economy as well as their antithesis—the constructive as well as the expulsive forces of the inner man—together with an exhaustive analysis of their grosser products, which legitimately fall within the sphere of morbid anatomy. A real knowledge of the nature and functions of **Váyu**, **Pittam**, and **Kaphah** may be useful in providing a deeper and clearer insight into the principles of true biology or pathology. It is incorrect to translate **Váyu**, **Pittam**, and **Kaphah** as air, bile, and phlegm, except under certain circumstances. **Váyu**, **Pittam**, and **Kaphah** are air, bile, and phlegm only when they are transformed into *Malas* or grosser organic excretions, which are supposed to be very intimately connected with factors, whether pathogenic or pathological; but they are not air, bile, and phlegm in those stages of their functions that determine the genesis, growth, and survival of the organism, as well as its death, decay, and disintegration. Knowledge of a region without knowledge of its opposite is but half-knowledge, and the principle of **Váyu**, **Pittam**, and **Kaphah** is the only one of its kind that tries to embrace the whole sphere of organic existence.

• **Ojah-Dhátú:**—From what has been stated before, it will appear that during the process of tissue-formation, the lymph or chyle, under the influence of **Pittam**, or metabolic heat, is transformed into the tissue, while the refuse or unusable portion of it is passed off through the

The **Ojah-Dhátu** is present in the reproductive energy that lies latent in every organic principle, namely lymph, blood, muscles, bone (synovia), marrow, and in the male and female reproductive elements. Hence, it is not surprising when we find in Ayurvedic works that this **Soma**, or **Ojah-Dhátu**, is mentioned as being diffused throughout the human organism and described as the essence of the lymph, chyle, blood, etc. <note>1. healthy continuance in the human organism. "Health and strength," observes our Rishi, "reside latent in the **Ojah-dhátu**, just as butter (**Ghritam**) lies latent in milk. (1)

**Dallana Mishra**, the celebrated commentator of the **Sushruta Samhitá**, has defined **Ojah** as a fatty substance that is completely combustible in character. Thus, in the course of tissue combustion, its excess quantity is deposited especially in the female body as fat, which produces that peculiar softness and elegance. (2) The presence of **Ojah** in urine is said to induce **Madhumeha** (3). Taking this fact alone into consideration, one is inclined to believe that **Ojah** must be something in the nature of sugar. As a consequence of these different interpretations of **Ojah**, the question arises whether there is present in the human organism any such common element that produces either of these two important oxidizing materials—namely, fat and sugar.

It is a demonstrated fact in modern physiology that **glycogen** is found in other tissues and organs besides the liver. Tissues of embryos and young animals, as well as newly formed pathological growths, may be said to contain **glycogen**. The activity of the heart, as well as the development of the fetal body (4), is largely dependent

<header>INTRODUCTION:</header>

...upon this **Ojah-dhátu**, which may be best translated as **glycogen** in the terminology of Western physiology. In fact, our **Achárjyayas** have used the term "**ojah**" to denote that vital principle in the organism which is essential to the maintenance of healthy combustion in its tissues and to the proper performance of their normal functions and activities, regardless of whether that principle is apparent in the form of **protoplasm**, **protoplasmic albumen**, **glycogen**, or **mucosin** (**Prákrita shleshmá**) in accordance with the differences in their functions, origins, and conditions of protoplasmic metabolism. In short, they were aware of the fact that fat and sugar are evolved out of a common basic principle in the organism, as has been very learnedly demonstrated by **Dr. S. N. Goswámi**, B.A., L.M.S., in his treatise on ***Pumsavanam***.† It is far from our intention to impose this opinion on anyone; we have simply stated our conclusion in the matter and will welcome the results of fresh inquiries on this subject.

-> * A. प्राकृतस्तु वलं श्लेष्मा विकृतो मल उच्यते । <-
-> स चैवोजः स्मृतः काये स च पापोपदिश्यते ॥ <-
-> *Charaka Samhitá Sutrasthánam*, Chap. XVII. <-

-> B. देहगतमपि बलमत्राववक्तव्यं ज्ञानविशेषात् कार्यविशेषाच्च । <-
-> **Chakradatta's** Commentary *S. Samhitá, Sutrasthánam* Chap. XV. <-

> † "From these extracts, it appears to us still more clearly that our countrymen also discovered, like **Dr. Pavy**, the importance of fat and sugar in the animal economy, as well as the way in which they can be developed from one common principle (76-78). A comparative study of the two systems of medical science, Indian and European, has led us to arrive at this conclusion; if we, therefore, are not inclined to identify **Ojah** with **albumen**, as has been done by some modern Indian commentators, we have reason to believe that the aforementioned extracts have not yet received sufficient consideration from them as forming the nutritive basis of the procreative elements. In short, the subject has been neglected until now or, at least, has been placed in the background from <note>Likely "from"; the letter 'f' is missing in the original print.</note> a want of attention on the part of those whose business it was to investigate the truths of Science. To hold that **Ojah** is kept in deposit in the heart, as a reserved food material, for the maintenance of its own work...

<summary>This page equates the Ayurvedic concept of Ojah-dhátu with glycogen, arguing that ancient Indian physicians understood it as a vital principle for tissue metabolism and the common source of fat and sugar. It critiques contemporary scholars who narrow the definition of Ojah to albumen, urging a more comprehensive scientific investigation.</summary>

<page-num>lviii</page-num>
### INTRODUCTION.

fact that growth is not the only condition of life, that vitality is somewhat independent of physiological processes, that the inner man, with the help of <term>Yoga</term>, can survive for a long time even without food and respiration,* and that death and decay may be arrested to a considerable degree by completely stopping many of those physiological processes in the body,† which Western scientists consider so essential to living, then Hindu pathology is unique in its conception of the nature of disease:

**Sushruta's Pathology:—** What is it in a person, asks <term>Sushruta</term>, that falls sick? What is it that we treat medicinally? The body or the mind? <term>Sushruta</term> says that, “anything that afflicts the inner man (the self or <term>Purusha</term>) is disease‡ and that disease has its primary seat in the inner spring of vitality from which it flows out to the surface, the external body.” In a human being, as in everything else in the universe, the direction of the inherent force is from the center to the circumference. The shock is felt first at the center of vitality, from where it is transmitted outward and thus affects the energy which holds the molecules together—the <term>Dvyanuks</term> and <term>Tryanuks</term> (binary and tertiary atoms) of which the physical body is composed—and further opposes the dissolution of those molecules into their elemental parts in the living organism. Even in cases of external injuries such as snake bites, etc., the potency of the venom is carried at once to that center, from where it is almost instantaneously transmitted through the body's external channels to its surface.

<page-num>lix</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

otherwise, what purpose does the <term>váyu</term> (nerve force) serve in the human economy? What do those myriads of <term>Chaitanya-váhini Nádis</term> (sensory nerves) exist for in the human system? In all diseases, the subjective sensations are the first to be experienced. "I am ill," "I feel hot," etc., are the voices of sensations, which form the *esse* (essence) of the disease. Disease, then, is a force and not matter.<note>That Hahnemann's theory of disease was foreshadowed long ago by <term>Sushruta</term> will appear from the above extracts from his works. Hahnemann observes that, when a person falls ill, it is only this spiritual self-acting vital force, everywhere present in the organism, that is primarily deranged by the dynamic influence of a morbific (disease-producing) agent inimical to life—*Organon*.</note>

**Pathology of <term>Tridosha</term> :—**<term>Sushruta</term>, though adopting the Vedic pathological dictum of <term>Tridhátu</term>, has expressed a very clear opinion on the subject. He observes that the relation between a disease and the deranged <term>váyu</term> (nerve force), <term>Pittam</term> (metabolism), and <term>Kapham</term> (unutilized products of the system), and the pathogenic factors which lie at the root of that disease, is not inherent but contingent. These morbific principles may permeate the whole organism without creating any discomfort, and it is only when they find a distinct lodging place, and are centered in some distinct part or tissue of the body, that they become the exciting factors of disease.

**Drug Potency :—**The next question which naturally arises in connection with such a theory of pathogeny (pathogenesis) is: what is medicine, or in other words, what is it in the drug that cures? <term>Sushruta</term>, after closely investigating all the theories on the subject, inclines toward the opinion that it is the potency of the drug that is curative, though he observes that because potency cannot exist independently of a drug, a drug is of primary interest for all practical purposes in therapy.

**Drug-Dynamization :—**"It is the potency of a drug that cures a disease." The potency is administered best

<summary>Sushruta defines disease as a dynamic force affecting the sensory nerves and internal vitality, predating modern theories like Hahnemann's homeopathy. He argues that disease arises when the three principles (Váyu, Pittam, and Kapham) become lodged in specific tissues, and that a drug's curative power lies in its internal potency.</summary>

In the medicated <term>Ghritas</term> or oils of our <term>pharmacopoeia</term>, which are prepared by successively boiling or cooking them with herbal decoctions, we cannot detect even a trace of any of the component drugs; yet, we know how powerful and effective they prove in the hands of our <term>Vaidyas</term>. When <term>Sushruta</term> formulated the process of preparing medicinal oils and <term>Ghritas</term>, and established the use of <term>Shatadhautam Ghritam</term> (clarified butter washed with water one hundred times in succession), <term>Sahasrapák Tailam</term> (medicinal oil cooked one thousand times in succession), or <term>Kumbha-Ghritam</term> (clarified butter one hundred years old), it may be fairly said that he recognized the principle of drug dynamization.

**Principles of Ayurvedic Treatment:—** Ayurvedic physicians practically recognize two different sets of principles in the field of practical <term>therapeutics</term>, which may be expressed in the terms of their Western colleagues as the **Laws of Similars and Contraries.**\* This apparent contradiction has been fully accounted for and explained in the writings of later commentators, but it is not within our scope to enter into these detailed discussions. In addition to these, <term>Sushruta</term>, along with the <term>Acharyayas</term> of his time, never fails to emphasize the value of psychotherapy in

<page-type>preface</page-type>

those forms of mental or nervous disorders for which <term>Mesmer</term> now rightly receives so much honor. Since the creation of humanity, the touch of the "Saintly" has been credited with the power of curing the sick; and <term>Avesha</term> (auto-hypnotism) and <term>Samádhi</term> (higher phases of clairvoyance) have achieved many miracles in the art of healing in India, which was the first country where it was successfully practiced for the welfare of humanity.

**Samshodhanam and Samshamanam:**—All kinds of treatment may be grouped under two heads, such as <term>Samshodhanam</term> and <term>Samshamanam</term>; that is, either the body should be cleansed (<term>Samshodhitam</term>) of the disease-causing disposition with the help of emetics or purgatives, or steps should be taken to restore the deranged <term>Váyu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, and <term>Kapham</term> to their normal condition with the help of proper medicinal drugs without resorting to any eliminating process. But in cases of inflammation, <term>Sushruta</term> commands that, instead of any <term>Samshamanam</term> remedies, diaphoresis <note>perspiration</note> should be first used. In cases where counter-irritants are indicated and in parts which are directly accessible, leeching and cauterization should be practiced with due regard to the season of the year and the requirements of the case. We find in his <term>Samhitá</term> a detailed account of the several species of leeches with their habits and habitats.

**Forms of medicine:**—Powders, lambatives <note>medicines to be licked</note>, decoctions, as well as medicated oils, <term>Ghritas</term>, confections, and wines are the forms in which, according to <term>Sushruta</term>, medicines should be given. The different drugs, such as roots, leaves, etc., should be gathered in the seasons proper to each. He classified the soil into five different kinds for the purpose of growing drugs of different therapeutic properties. Even the virtues of different flavors and colors were determined with regard to their respective actions on the deranged disease-causing principles of the body.

**Rasáyaham:**—As the <term>Áyurveda</term> is the science of life and health, the holy <term>Agnivesha</term>, at the very beginning

...of the therapeutic portion of his work,* he has described several medicinal compounds that improve general health and arrest the ravages of time. Theoretically speaking, the science of *Ayurveda* recognizes no preordained limit to human existence. Life can be prolonged with the help of suitable medicines. Through observation and patient research, our Rishis devised many such treatments that can rejuvenate an elderly person and supply to an old and exhausted human body those vital elements which ebb away with the passing years. Hence, we find many rejuvenating medicines prescribed for healthy men that arrest decay and guard against the onset of senility by increasing the body's fundamental vital principles and preventing *Váyu*, *Pittam*, and *Kapham* from being transformed into disease-causing conditions <note>morbific diatheses</note>.

**Diet**—"A good and proper diet during disease is worth a hundred medicines, and no amount of medication can benefit a patient who does not observe a strict dietary regimen." Our *Ayurveda*, instead of being content with specifying the nature of diet in diseases in general, mentions the names of food items that should or should not be consumed in any specific illness, judged in light of their properties for aggravating *Váyu*, *Pittam*, or *Kapham*. The dietary or therapeutic properties of a large number of items of human consumption, as well as the chemical changes they undergo in the digestive systems of different mammals, have been studied and analyzed. Thus, we find in our physical and medical *Samhitás* such instructions as those regarding barley grains passed undigested with the feces of a cow or

It was a fundamental doctrine of Ayurvedic dietitians that a patient's craving for any particular kind of food during a certain disease emphatically shows that their organism lacks those elements which make up the composition of the item offered. Therefore, elaborate dietary guidelines were formulated, which cannot help but be acceptable even to the most fastidious patient.

**Therapeutics:—** The exclusion† of salt and water from the food of a patient with <term>ascites</term> <note>abdominal dropsy</note> or <term>anasarca</term> <note>generalized edema</note>, as laid down in our *Samhitás*, shows that our Rishis possessed a deeper chemical knowledge regarding the effects of organic matter on the human system than many of us are ready to credit to these pioneers of medical science.

kingdom into Vriksha, Gulma, Vanaspati, and Virudha. This classification has been minutely worked out in works on Hindu Botany, where we find such specific subdivisions as Agravija (whose tops only are planted), Mulaja (whose roots only are planted), Parnayoni, Skandaja, Vījaruha (germinated from seeds), and Sannurudhaja. But the botany of Sushruta is more in the nature of a *Materia Medica* than a work on botany proper, though he sometimes mentions the habitat and describes the foliage of certain plants so that they may be distinguished from others of a related species.

The uses of metals and minerals for therapeutic purposes in India are as old as the *Rigveda* * itself. Sushruta describes the methods of preparing oxides, sulfates, or chlorides of the six metals as the case may be. Mercury has only been mentioned once in the *Samhitá*, and then very vaguely as well. Processes for the preparation of alkalis and the leaching of ashes are very elaborately described. Beyond these, the chemical knowledge of Sushruta scarcely extends.

**Hygiene and Public Health:**—As a writer on hygiene and public health, Sushruta emphasizes the importance of cleanliness of both spirit and body. Water, whose disinfecting virtues have so often been praised in the *Vedas* †, forms the subject of discussion for an entire chapter of the *Samhitá*. Outbreaks of epidemics have been attributed to unfavorable seasons, to the floating of minute particles of poisonous flower pollen in the air, and to the sin or unrighteous conduct of the community. Earthquakes, famines, and physical phenomena, which are currently attributed to magnetic disturbances of the earth, have been

<meta>This page continues the discussion on hygiene and public health, specifically how Sushruta describes natural phenomena as precursors to epidemics and the importance of seasonal regimens and timing in medical treatment.</meta>

described by Sushruta as the usual precursors of devastating epidemics such as the plague, etc. Mortality among birds and unusual deaths among rats and other burrowing rodents have been included among other foretelling signs of a visitation from Providence. When asked about the cause of such outbreaks, Dhanvantari observes that the **Váyu** (molecular energy) of the soil is disturbed or affected by earthquakes and seasons of unnatural drought or flood, deranging their **Pittam** (kinetic energy) and **Shleshmá** (humidity), which produce disease-causing factors that affect a whole community. Sushruta, as a true physician, has elaborately dealt with the regimen of diet and behavior during the different seasons of the year (Ch. 24 - U. T. 64). If strictly followed, these should act as a good prevention against attacks of many epidemic diseases. These regimens are designed with a most careful regard for the conditions of life found there and ward off those sad breakdowns in health which are, in many instances, the result of an unsuitable mode of living in this country.

**Twofold division of Time, etc.:**—It is a fundamental principle of Sushruta that in a case of medical treatment, the prevailing season of the year should be taken into account. In his **Samhitá**, we find two distinct classifications of seasons: one based on the peculiar physical phenomena that distinguish the different seasons of the year—a fact which emphatically proves that Sushruta was an inhabitant of the sub-Himalayan Gangetic Doab—and the other for the purpose of showing the respective accumulation, worsening, and subsidence of disease-causing imbalances (**Doshas**). In the same manner, the different quarters of the day and night have been minutely charted or set down to show the spontaneous worsening and subsidence of the deranged **Váyu**, **Pittam**, and **Kaphah** during the twenty-four hours. The influence of planets on the production of certain diseases, such as smallpox, measles, scarlet fever, etc., is almost a proven fact. As it governs the prevalence and non-prevalence of certain maladies, the worsening and

<meta>This page continues the discussion of environmental and astronomical influences on health, specifically the lunar influence on the vegetable kingdom and the classification of regions for medical purposes.</meta>

<page-num>lxvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

The non-aggravation of certain existing disorders also owes much of its origin to this potent factor. The vegetable kingdom, from which we gather our daily food, is also subject to this influence; hence, the discernment we exercise in selecting our food on certain days of the lunar month.

Regions have been divided into <term>Jángala</term> or <term>A'nupa</term> according to whether their physical features take on the character of a dry plateau or a swamp or marsh, with a <term>Sádhárana</term> region possessing features common to both. Diseases that occur naturally or are spontaneously relieved in each of these types of region have been treated with the same scientific insight that characterizes modern medical works on seaside or spring sanitariums. The properties of the waters of various Indian rivers were determined for the purposes of practical <term>therapeutics</term>. The therapeutic properties of the milk of a goat, buffalo, mare, elephant, or woman—as well as their derivatives such as curd, whey, etc.—along with the properties of the flesh and urine of various groups of female animals indigenous to the land, were studied and analyzed. This provided a practical physician with a <term>dietary</term> list for different diseases designed to satisfy even the most fastidious patient's taste, while remaining potent enough to cure the ailment they suffer from without the help of any special medicine. Thus, we find our <term>Vaidyas</term> prescribing the meat of many carnivorous animals as a diet for consumption, goat's meat for <term>phthisis</term>, goat's milk for <term>colitis</term>, and <term>Tittira's</term> meat for fever, etc.

**Diseases of the Kidneys and Bladder:**—In treating diseases of the kidneys, bladder, and the urethra, <term>Sushruta</term> described the symptoms and the color of the urine in each specific variety without establishing a specific method for testing the urine. However, we know that Sushruta urged his readers at the very beginning of his work to refer to other related branches of science for information not contained in his book. In the same manner,

<summary>This page discusses the classification of Indian regions by climate and the use of specific animal products and diets, such as goat's milk and meat, as therapeutic treatments. It also begins to address Sushruta's descriptions of urinary and kidney diseases.</summary>

<page-num>lxvii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

We can account for the absence of any instructions regarding the checking of the pulse as an important aid in making a correct diagnosis. We need only repeat the statement that the readers of this <term>Samhitá</term> must look for this information in Kanada's <term>Nádi Vijnánam</term>, which has made our <term>Vaidyas</term> such expert <term>sphygmologists</term> <note>pulse diagnosis experts</note>.

**Kalpa:**—In the <term>Kalpasthánam</term> of his <term>Samhitá</term>, Sushruta has described the symptoms of hydrophobia and snake bites, etc., as well as those developed in cases of vegetable poisoning, together with their therapeutic treatment and remedies, which, if rightly studied and investigated, may yet throw new light upon the subject.

**Sushruta as an Observer:**—It has been lately discovered by a German physiologist that tubercle bacilli do not thrive in goat's blood. The importance of goat's milk in colitis as an efficient agent in checking fermentation in the intestines, or the close contact with a goat as a powerful aid in curing tuberculous <term>phthisis</term> <note>tuberculosis</note>, was first demonstrated by Sushruta. Not only this—but the inhalation of the air of a cattle shed, and especially the fact that exhalations from goats' bodies tend to destroy the <term>phthisis</term> germs, did not fail to attract the attention of the Indian <term>Rishis</term>. The fumigation of the sickroom with antiseptic preparations such as अष्टाङ्गधूप (<term>Asthánga dhupas</term>) is purely Indian in its origin and in no way inferior to the modern introduction of Cogghill's respirators. The microscopic germs that are said to propagate septic fever, otherwise called भूताभि षङ्गोत्य विषमज्वर (<term>Bhutabhishangotya Vishamajvara</term>), are often found to disappear under this Indian device where no medicines produce any impression. Thus, many a wonderful discovery like the one above hails from the dimness of a bygone age. Many truths lie embedded in the vast medical literature of the <term>Brahmanas</term>, which has claimed close attention and devout study even by Western savants. We have not labored in vain if these pages can help a little to revive the old genius of the <term>Ayurveda</term>, or help the progress of human science one step onward toward the attainment of its goal.

<page-num>lxvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

The fact that certain existing disorders do not worsen also owes much of its origin to this powerful factor. The vegetable kingdom, from which we obtain our daily food, is also subject to this influence; this is the reason for the care we take in selecting our food on specific days of the lunar month.

Countries have been divided into <term>Jángala</term> or <term>A'ṇupa</term> depending on whether their physical features have the character of a dry plateau or a swamp or marsh, while a <term>Sádhárana</term> region possesses features common to both. Diseases that occur naturally or are spontaneously relieved in each of these types of regions have been treated with the same scientific insight found in modern medical works regarding seaside or spring health resorts. The benefits of the waters of various Indian rivers were determined for practical medical treatments. The therapeutic properties of the milk from a goat, buffalo, mare, female elephant, or woman—as well as their derivatives like curd and whey—along with the properties of the meat and urine of various female animals native to the land, were studied and analyzed. This provided a practicing physician with a list of diets for different diseases to satisfy the tastes of even the most particular patient, while remaining powerful enough to cure the illness they suffer from without needing special medicine. This is why we find our <term>Vaidyas</term> <note>traditional Indian physicians</note> prescribing the meat of many carnivorous animals as a diet for consumption, goat's meat for <term>phthisis</term> <note>tuberculosis</note>, goat's milk for colitis, and <term>Tittira</term>'s <note>partridge</note> meat for fever, etc.

**Diseases of the Kidneys and Bladder:—** In discussing the diseases of the kidneys, bladder, and urethra, <term>Sushruta</term> described the symptoms and the color of the urine for each specific variety without providing any method for testing the urine. However, we know that <term>Sushruta</term> instructed his readers at the very beginning of his work to refer to other related branches of science for information not contained in his book. In the same manner

# INTRODUCTION.

...we can account for the absence of any instructions regarding the feeling of the pulse as an important aid in making a correct diagnosis. We need only repeat the statement that the readers of this <term>Samhitá</term> must look for this information in Kanáda's <term>Nádi Vijnánam</term>, which has made our <term>Vaidyas</term> <note>traditional physicians</note> such expert <term>sphygmologists</term> <note>pulse experts</note>.

**Kalpa:**—In the <term>Kalpasthánam</term> of his <term>Samhitá</term>, Sushruta has described the symptoms of hydrophobia <note>rabies</note> and snake bites, etc., as well as those developed in cases of vegetable poisoning, together with their therapeutic treatment and remedies. If these are rightly studied and investigated, they may yet shed new light upon the subject.

**Sushruta as an Observer:**—It has been recently discovered by a German physiologist that tuberculosis bacilli do not thrive in goat's blood. The importance of goat's milk in colitis as an efficient agent in checking fermentation in the intestines, or the close contact with a goat as a powerful aid in curing tuberculous <term>phthisis</term> <note>tuberculosis</note>, was first demonstrated by Sushruta. Not only this—but the inhalation of the air of a cattle-shed, and especially the fact that the exhalations of goats' bodies tend to destroy the tuberculosis germs, did not fail to attract the attention of the Indian <term>Rishis</term>. The fumigation of the sick-room with antiseptic preparations such as <term>अष्टाङ्गधूप (Asthánga dhupas)</term> is purely Indian in its origin and in no way inferior to the modern introduction of Cogghill's respirators. The microscopic germs that are said to propagate septic fever, otherwise called <term>भूताभिषङ्गज विषमज्वर</term> <note>fever caused by external influences or spirits</note>, are found very often to disappear under this Indian device when no medicines produce any effect. Thus, many wonderful discoveries like the one above come from the dimness of a bygone age. Many truths lie embedded in the vast medical literature of the <term>Bráhmanas</term> which deserve close attention and devoted study, even by Western scholars. We have not labored in vain if these pages can help even a little to revive the ancient genius of the <term>Áyurveda</term>, or help the progress of human science one step onward toward the attainment of its goal.

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA

## SUTRASTHĀNAM.

### CHAPTER I.

Well, we* shall now describe the origin of the Science of Medicine, as revealed by the holy Dhanvantari to his disciple Sushruta (**<term>Vedotpattimadhyayam</term>**).

Once upon a time, when the holy Dhanvantari—the greatest of the mighty celestials, incarnated in the form of Divodása, the king of Kási—was blissfully seated in his hermitage, surrounded by a gathering of holy <term>Rishis</term>, Aupadhenava, Vaitarana, Aurabhra, Paushkalávata, Karavirya, Gopura-rakshita, Sushruta, and others addressed him as follows:—"O Sire, it greatly grieves us to find people, though otherwise well-befriended

---

* <note>This present work, which originally was the subject of a discourse by the holy sage Dhanvantari to his disciple Sushruta, has been compiled in its current form by the venerable Nágárjuna, and is accordingly named the *Sushruta Samhitá*.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the opening of the Sushruta Samhita, where the sage Dhanvantari, incarnated as the King of Kasi, is approached in his hermitage by Sushruta and other disciples seeking medical knowledge. A footnote explains that the text was later compiled by Nagarjuna.</summary>
<keywords>Sushruta Samhita, Sutrasthanam, Dhanvantari, Divodása, Kási, Rishis, Nágárjuna, medical origin, Vedotpattimadhyayam</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the petition of the disciples to the sage Dhanvantari and records his acceptance of them as students. It then describes the divine origin of Ayurveda as a branch of the Atharva Veda, originally composed by Brahma before the creation of man.</meta>

THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. I.

by <unclear>their</unclear> kin and relations, falling prey to disease—mental, physical, traumatic, or natural—and piteously waiting in agony like utterly friendless creatures on earth; and we supplicate you, O Lord, to illuminate our minds with the truths of the Eternal Áyurveda (Medical Science) so that we may faithfully discharge the duties allotted to us in life and alleviate the sufferings of humanity at large. Bliss in this life and hereafter is the gift of this eternal Áyurveda, and for this, O Lord, we have made bold to approach you as your humble disciples." To them, the holy Dhanvantari replied:—"Welcome to all of you to this blissful hermitage. All of you are worthy of the honor of true pupilship or tutelage."

**<unclear>The</unclear> Áyurveda** (which forms the subject of our <unclear>pre</unclear>sent discourse) originally formed one of the sub-<unclear>sec</unclear>tions of the Atharva Veda; and even before the <unclear>crea</unclear>tion of mankind, the self-begotten Brahmá strung <unclear>it to</unclear>gether into a hundred thousand couplets (Shlokas), <unclear>divi</unclear>ded into a thousand chapters. But then he <unclear>tho</unclear>ught of the short duration of human life on earth <unclear>and</unclear> the failing character of human memory, and <unclear>felt it</unclear> prudent to divide the whole of the Áyurveda <unclear>into eight</unclear> different branches, such as: the Salya-<unclear>Tantram,</unclear> the Sálákya-Tantram, the Káya-Chikitsá, the <unclear>Bhuta-Vidya,</unclear> the Kaumára-Bhritya, the Agada-Tantram, <unclear>the Rasáyana-</unclear>Tantram, and the Vájeekarana-Tantram.

-># THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. I.<-
->### Chap. I. ] SUTRASTHANAM.<-

Now regarding the characteristic features of each of these branches of the Science of Ayurveda:

**The Salya Tantram**<note>Any foreign matter lodged in a human organism that causes pain is called a Shalya.</note>—The scope of this branch of medical science is to remove any extraneous substances (from an ulcer), such as fragments of hay, particles of stone, dust, iron, or bone; splinters, nails, hair, clotted blood, or thick pus (as the case may be); or to extract a dead fetus from the uterus, or to ensure safe deliveries in cases of abnormal presentation; and to address the principles and methods of using and handling surgical instruments in general, as well as the application of fire (cautery) and alkaline (caustic) substances, along with the diagnosis and treatment of ulcers.

**The Shalakya-Tantram**<note>The name is derived from the Sanskrit term Shalaká, meaning a probe or a rod, as the use and application of this instrument are primarily included within the scope of this branch of Ayurveda.</note>—This focuses on the treatment of those diseases restricted to the upper cavities or openings of the body (literally: the region above the collarbones), such as the ears, the eyes, the mouth, the nostrils, etc.

**The Kaya-Chikitsa** (General diseases)<note>The term Kaya literally signifies the vital heat or fire which runs through the entire system; therefore, Kaya-chikitsa deals with diseases that may gradually invade the fundamental principles of a living human organism.</note>—This treats diseases which, instead of being simply

<summary>This page defines the first three branches of Ayurveda: Salya Tantram (surgery and extraneous objects), Shalakya-Tantram (diseases of the head and sensory organs), and Kaya-Chikitsa (general internal medicine).</summary>
<keywords>Ayurveda, Salya Tantram, Shalakya-Tantram, Kaya-Chikitsa, surgery, cautery, Shalya, Shalaká, medical branches, internal medicine</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions of the eight branches of Ayurveda, describing the remainder of Kaya-Chikitsa (general medicine) and introducing Bhuta-Vidya (demonic diseases), Kaumara-Bhritya (pediatrics), Agada-Tantram (toxicology), and the beginning of Rasayana-Tantram (rejuvenation).</meta>

-># THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA [Chap. I.<-

restricted to any specific organ or to any particular part of the body, but affect the entire system, such as fever, dysentery, hemoptysis, insanity, hysteria, leprosy, unnatural discharges from the urethra, etc.

**The Bhuta-Vidya** (Demonic diseases)—provides incantations and methods for exorcising evil spirits and making offerings to gods, demons, Gandharvas, Yakshas, Rakshas, etc., for the cure of diseases originating from their harmful influences.

**The Kaumara-Bhritya** (Pediatrics)—deals with the nursing and healthy upbringing of infants, the purification and improvement of breast milk that is found deficient in any of its characteristic traits, and also with cures for diseases specific to infancy due to the use of corrupted breast milk or the influences of harmful stars and spirits.

**The Agada-Tantram** (Toxicology)—deals with bites from snakes, spiders, and venomous insects, and their characteristic symptoms and antidotes. It also aims to eliminate poisons—whether animal, vegetable, or chemical (resulting from incompatible combinations)—from the system of a person overwhelmed by their effects.

**The Rasayana-Tantram** (Science of Rejuvenation)—has as its specific goal the

prolonging human life and invigorating the memory and the vital organs of man. It deals with recipes that enable a man to retain his manhood or youthful vigor into a healthy old age, and which generally serve to make the human system invulnerable to disease and decay.

**The Vajeekarana-Tantram** (Science of Aphrodisiacs)—treats of measures by which a man’s semen—if naturally scanty or deficient in quality—is cleared of its defects; or is purified if disturbed by the corrupted humors of the body (such as wind, etc.); or is invigorated and increased in quantity (if pure and healthy); or acquires its healthy and normal consistency (if thinned and weakened by the indiscretions of youth). [In short, it deals with things that increase the pleasures of youth and make a man doubly endearing to a woman].

Thus, the entire science of Ayurveda is classified into the eight preceding branches. Now tell me, which of them should be taught, and to which of you? The disciples said: “Instruct us all, O Lord, in the science of surgery (Shalya), and let that be the primary subject of our study.” To which the holy Dhanvantari replied: “So be it.” Then the disciples said again: “We are all of one mind in the matter, O Lord: Sushruta shall be our spokesman and ask

<header>THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA [ Chap. I.</header>

...your questions in accordance with the general trend of our purpose. All of us will attentively hear what you will be pleased to say to Sushruta, <note>and that will save you the trouble of teaching us individually</note>." To which the venerable sage replied—"Be it so. Now listen, Sushruta, my dear child. The object or utility of the science which forms the subject of our present discussion may be grouped under two distinct sub-headings, such as (1) the cure of diseased persons, and (2) the preservation of health in those who are not afflicted with any sort of bodily distempers."

The etymological meaning of the term "**Áyurveda**" may be interpreted to denote either a science in the knowledge of which life exists, or which helps a person to enjoy a longer duration of life.

### **The primary position of surgery:**

<note>As regards time and importance among the other allied branches of the Science of Medicine</note>. Hear me speak on the Science of Surgery (<term>Shalya-Tantram</term>), which is the oldest of all the other branches of the Science of Medicine (<term>Áyurveda</term>) and is further corroborated by the four classes of testimonies, namely: Perception, Inference, Analogy, and Scriptural Truths (<term>Ágamas</term>). The primary position of this branch of the Áyurveda, <note>as regards its time or origin</note>, may be inferred from the fact that Surgery lends her aid materially towards the

<summary>Dhanvantari defines the two purposes of Ayurveda as curing the sick and maintaining the health of the well. He identifies surgery (Shalya-Tantram) as the oldest and most foundational branch of medicine, supported by scriptural and logical evidence.</summary>

It is said that the god <term>Rudra</term> severed the head of the God of Sacrifice (<term>Yajna</term>). Consequently, the gods approached the divine <term>Ashvins</term> and addressed them as follows: "You twins, O lords, who are destined to be the greatest of us all, reconnect the head of <term>Yajna</term> with his headless body." The divine <term>Ashvins</term> replied to them: "O lords, we shall do as you command." Then the gods appealed to the god <term>Indra</term> so that a portion of the offerings presented during a sacrifice might be granted to those heavenly twins. As requested, the <term>Ashvins</term> reunited the severed head of <term>Yajna</term> to his body. <note>Hence, this branch of the <term>Áyurveda</term> (<term>Shalyanga</term>) is the oldest of all its subdivisions.</note>

Everyone considers this **Tantram** to be the most important of all the other branches of the **Áyurveda**, because immediate results can be produced with the help of instruments such as,

* <note><unclear>Ulcers</unclear> resulting from blows and sword-cuts had to be dressed and treated during the wars between the gods and the demons long before the appearance of any physical or internal diseases such as fever, etc. Surgery provided everything required to heal those wounds. Therefore, this branch of the <term>Áyurveda</term> is the oldest of all the related branches of the healing art.</note>

with the goal of teaching the science of surgery, along with all its allied branches of study, to mankind.

In this science (**Áyurveda**), the **Purusha** (the self-conscious organic individual) is described as the result of the combination of the soul and the five primary material principles. All medical actions, such as surgical operations, the administration of medicinal remedies, and the application of alkaline substances or cauterization, etc., are restricted to the **Purusha** alone.<note>It may be asked why these acts should be limited to the **Purusha**. Such a question may be successfully answered by the statement that the **Purusha** alone is the receptacle of health and disease, in contrast to the absolute Self or Ego.</note>

Why is this so? The answer is simply because the created world is composed of two distinct classes: the mobile and the immobile.<note>The vegetable world belongs to the latter category, while animals, possessing the power of motion, belong to the former.</note> These two classes, in turn, are further subdivided for the purposes of medical science into two orders: **Ágneya** (hot) and **Saumya** (cold). Hence, the world is composed of five material principles, although it is characterized by the twofold virtues of **Ágneya** (hot) and **Saumya** (cold).<note>It may be argued here that since everything in the universe is composed of the five fundamental material principles (earth, water, fire (heat), air, and sky (ether)), it is not appropriate to assert that the universe possesses only the twofold attributes of **Ágneya** (hot or fiery) and **Saumya** (cold or watery). However, because fire (heat) or water (cold) predominates in all things in the universe alongside the primary virtues of the</note>

## Further classification of the mobile and the immobile:

The living world may be divided into four subdivisions: the <term>Svedaja</term> (born of sweat or heat and moisture, i.e., abiogenous), the <term>Andaja</term> (egg-born or oviparous), the <term>Udbhijja</term> (vegetable), and the <term>Jaráyuja</term> (placental or viviparous). The <term>Purusha</term>, or the subjective personality (man), is the greatest of them all, because all other forms of life are made to serve his needs on Earth.

## Disease: Its Definition:

The <term>Purusha</term> (man) is the vessel for any particular disease, and that which proves to be a source of torment or pain to him is defined as a disease.<note>A disease may be defined as something that afflicts the Purusha (the self-conscious personality); alternatively, those things or incidents that combine to afflict the Purusha are usually interpreted to carry that meaning.</note> There are four different types of disease: Traumatic or of external origin (<term>Ágantuka</term>), Bodily (<term>Shárira</term>), Mental (<term>Mánasa</term>), and Natural (<term>Svábhavika</term>). A disease due to an external blow or injury is called <term>Ágantuka</term>. Diseases due to irregularities in food or drink, or resulting from a deranged state of the blood or of the bodily humors acting either individually or together, are called <term>Shárira</term>. Excessive anger, grief, fear, joy, despondency, envy, misery, pride, greed, lust, desire, malice, etc., are

<note>...other fundamental material principles. It is not improper to classify all under the categories of hot or cold, as a third factor does not exist. Hence, the world (जगत्) possesses the twofold virtues of hot and cold.</note>

<summary>This page classifies living beings into four categories based on their origin and defines disease as any suffering that affects the Purusha (the individual). It further categorizes diseases into four types: traumatic, bodily, mental, and natural.</summary>

Diet comprises four different factors, such as food, drink, electuaries (lambatives), and so on, which, for the purposes of the <term>Ayurveda</term>, are again grouped under three different heads: the pacifier of the deranged bodily humors (<term>Dosha-prashamanam</term>), therapeutic (<term>Vyádhi-prashamanam</term>), and health-giving (<term>Svastha-Vrittikara</term>). <term>Achára</term> (conduct) relates to three different factors: the body, the speech, and the mental acts. The above-mentioned measures, when properly employed, are powerful enough to combat all sorts of bodily distempers, if the specific requirements of each case are carefully taken into consideration.</note>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [Chap. I.

Taste (<term>Rasa</term>) [which cannot exist independently of the substances] in which it is inherent. These substances, which are called the <term>Oshadhis</term>, may be divided into two classes: the mobile and the immobile. The immobile <term>Oshadhis</term>, in turn, can be grouped under four sub-headings: the <term>Vanaspatis</term>, the <term>Vrikshas</term>, the <term>Virudhas</term>, and the <term>Oshadhis</term> proper.

Those trees which bear fruit without blossoming are called the <term>Vanaspatis</term> (such as the <term>Plaksha</term> and the <term>Oudumvura</term>). Those that bear both fruits and flowers are called the <term>Vrikshas</term>. Shrubs and creepers that trail on the ground are called <term>Virudhas</term>, whereas those plants which die when their fruits ripen are called <term>Oshadhis</term> proper, such as cereals.

The mobile <term>Oshadhis</term>, or animals, are divided into four classes: the viviparous, the oviparous, the sweat-begotten, and those that are born of decomposed vegetable matter. Humans and other mammals belong to the first group; birds, snakes, and reptiles belong to the second; ants, worms, etc., belong to the third; while frogs and <term>Indragopas</term> belong to the fourth. For medicinal purposes, bark, leaves, flowers, fruits, roots, bulbs, the expressed juice, and milky or resinous secretions of plants, etc., are obtained from the vegetable world. The

---

* The use of oil extracted from drugs and seeds, as well as their ashes or alkaline preparations, are likewise indicated.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:**—Physicians should look upon these four factors (food, conduct, earth, and time) as the accumulators, aggravators, and pacifiers of the deranged bodily humours and of the diseases resulting from them in man. Diseases due to causes that are external to the body may affect the mind or the body. Thus, I have briefly dealt with the <term>Purusha</term>, Disease, Medicine, Medical Procedures, and the Specific Time. The term <term>Purusha</term> should be interpreted to include within its meaning the combination of its five material components and all things resulting from them, such as the limbs and parts of the body, as well as the skin, the flesh, the blood, the veins, the nerves, etc. The term Disease signifies all ailments incidental to the individual or combined actions of the three deranged bodily humours and the blood. The term Medicine signifies drugs and their qualities, tastes, potency, inherent efficacy (<term>Prabháva</term>), and reactionary properties (<term>Vipáka</term>). Medical procedures (<term>kriyá</term>) denotes such processes as surgical operations, injections, emulsive measures, lubrications, etc. The term Time signifies all opportune moments for medical applications.

### **Authoritative verses on the subject:—**

The primary principle of the science of medicine has thus been briefly stated and will be fully dealt with in the following one hundred and twenty chapters, distributed among the five main subdivisions or <term>Sthánás</term> of the present work. These hundred and twenty chapters will be elaborately discussed according to the specific meaning or significance of their names under the sub-headings of the <term>Sutra-Sthánam</term>

# CHAPTER II

Now we shall discuss the chapter that deals with the rites for the formal initiation of a pupil into the science of medicine (**Shishyopanayaniya-madhyayam**).

This initiation should be granted to a student belonging to one of the three twice-born castes—the <term>Bráhmana</term>, the <term>Kshatriya</term>, and the <term>Vaishya</term>. The student should be young, born of a good family, and possess a desire to learn, strength, energy, contentment, good character, self-control, a strong retentive memory, intellect, courage, and purity of mind and body. They should have a simple and clear understanding, a deep insight into the subjects studied, and should also possess physical traits such as thin lips, thin teeth, and a thin tongue. They should have a straight nose, large, honest, intelligent eyes, a kind expression around the mouth, and a contented mind. They should be pleasant in their speech and dealings and generally hardworking. A person possessing the opposite qualities should not be admitted into the study of medicine.

**Mode of Initiation:**

A <term>Bráhmana</term> teacher should initiate a disciple or student in the following way: A square sand platform, measuring a cubit

The cushion or the platform should be plastered over with a solution of water and cow-dung, and blades of <term>Kusha grass</term> should be strewn over it. Then the gods, the <term>Bráhmanas</term>, and the physicians should be worshiped with offerings of flowers, fried paddy, gems, and sun-dried rice. 

Then, after drawing straight lines across the <term>Sthandilam</term> so as to meet the top of the furthest side of the square, and having sprinkled them with holy water, the preceptor should lay down a blade of <term>Kusha grass</term> tied in the form of a knot, known as the <term>Bráhmana</term>, along the right side of the sacred cushion, and kindle the sacred fire close to his seat. Then, having soaked the twigs of the four sacrificial trees—<term>Khadira</term>, <term>Palásha</term>, <term>Devadáru</term>, and <term>Vilva</term>, or <term>Vata</term>, <term>Oudumvara</term>, <term>Ashvattha</term>, and <term>Madhuka</term>—in curd, honey, and clarified butter, he should perform the rite of <term>Homa</term> according to the rules of a <term>Dárvi Homa</term> ceremony. 

Then libations of clarified butter should be poured into the sacrificial fire while repeating the <term>Mahá Vyáhriti Mantras</term> preceded by the mystic <term>Omkára</term>. After that, libations of clarified butter should be poured into the fire in honor of each of the gods and <term>Rishis</term> (celestial physicians) invoked

In the proper season, you shall trim your nails and clip your hair, put on the sacred cloth dyed brownish-yellow, live the life of a truthful, self-controlled anchorite, and be obedient and respectful toward your preceptor. you shall be guided by my directions. You shall do what is pleasant and beneficial to me; otherwise, you shall incur sin, and all your study and knowledge shall fail to bear their desired fruit, and you shall gain no fame. If I, on the other hand, treat you unjustly even with your perfect obedience and in full conformity to the terms agreed upon, may I incur equal sin as you, and may all my knowledge prove futile, and never have any opportunity for practice or demonstration. You shall help with your professional skill and knowledge the <term>Bráhmanas</term>, your elders, preceptors, and friends, the indigent, the honest, the anchorites, the helpless, and those who shall come to you (from a distance, or those who live close by), as well as your relations and kinsmen <note>to the best of your knowledge and ability</note>, and you shall give them medicine <note>without charging any payment for it whatever</note>, and God will bless you for that. You shall not treat medicinally a professional hunter, a fowler, a habitual sinner, or anyone who has been degraded in life; and by so doing, you shall acquire friends, fame, piety, wealth, and all desired objects in life, and your knowledge shall gain renown.”

—The day of the new moon, the eighth day of the moon's waning, the fourteenth day of the dark fortnight, as well as the corresponding days in

Similarly, a clap of thunder heard at an improper season (the months of <term>Pousha</term>, <term>Phálguna</term>, and <term>Chaitra</term>), or a flash of lightning occurring at a time when such phenomena are naturally rare, or a calamity befalling one's country, relatives, or king, should be considered as occasions prohibiting the study of the <term>Áyurveda</term>. Moreover, one should not read it in a cremation ground, nor while riding an elephant, horse, or any other vehicle, nor on a battlefield, nor in a place of execution. A festival, the appearance of inauspicious omens, and the days of the fortnight usually avoided by the <term>Bráhmanas</term> when studying the <term>Vedas</term>, as well as an unclean state of the body, should be regarded as occasions prohibiting the study of the <term>Áyurveda</term>.

### CHAPTER III.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which deals with the classification of the <term>Áyurveda</term> and the order <insert>in which the venerable <term>Dhanvantari</term> taught them to his pupils</insert>. **<term>Adhyayana-Sampradaníyam</term>.**

It has been stated before that one hundred and twenty chapters have been distributed among the five parts or subdivisions (of the present work) in the following order: forty-six in the part of Definitive Aphorisms (**<term>Sutra-Sthánam</term>**); sixteen in the part dealing with the causes and origins of diseases (**<term>Nidánam</term>**); ten in the part explaining the anatomy and physiology of the human body (**<term>Shárira Sthánam</term>**); forty in the part of therapeutics (**<term>Chikitsitam</term>**); and eight in the part dealing with poisons and their antidotes (**<term>Kalpa-Sthánam</term>**). In addition to these, the **<term>Uttara-Tantram</term>** consists of sixty-six chapters.

**Metrical texts:** —The **<term>Sutra-Sthánam</term>**, which contains forty-six chapters, is so called because it discusses topics in the form of hints, arranges them as aphorisms, and links together subjects relating to longevity. <header>Chap. II. ] UTTRASTHANAM.</header>

**Because of its superiority over the others, the great sages have called this division the Excellent (Uttaram).** Due to the information it provides on varied subjects, it is called the best, the permanent, and the last.

In this division, which is called the last, four divisions of the <term>Ayurveda</term> are included: namely, 1. <term>Shálákyam</term> (treatment of diseases of parts situated above the collarbones), 2. <term>Kaumárabhrityam</term> (management of children), 3. <term>Káyachikitsá</term> (general diseases), and 4. <term>Bhuta-Vidyá</term>.

The division named <term>Vájeekaranam</term> (on the strengthening of virile power, etc.) and <term>Rasáyanam</term> (remedies for preserving vigor, etc.) have been included in the fourth division of this treatise, called <term>Chikitsá</term>.

The doctrine of antidotes comes under the category of <term>Kalpa</term> in this treatise, and <term>Shalyam</term> (surgery) is treated incidentally throughout the book. Thus, these are the eight limbs or divisions of the Science of Medicine proclaimed to the world by the original god. Those who study them with due care and use the knowledge with caution shall preserve the lives of people on this earth. It is absolutely necessary that the book be read; and after having read it, one should attend to the practice of the science. The physician who has learned both of these is fit to be honored by kings.

<summary>This page defines the Uttara-sthanam as the "Excellent" or final section of the text and lists the eight branches of Ayurveda. It emphasizes that a physician must master both the theoretical study and the practical application of medicine to be worthy of royal respect.</summary>
<keywords>Uttara-sthanam, Ayurveda, eight limbs of medicine, Shálákyam, Kaumárabhrityam, Káyachikitsá, Bhuta-Vidyá, Vájeekaranam, Rasáyanam, Kalpa, Shalyam, medical education, physician's duties</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the requirements for a physician, transitioning from the division of the Ayurveda into the necessity of combining theoretical knowledge with practical skill.</meta>

<header>THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. | Chap. III.</header>

# **Authoritative verses on the subject:**

A physician well-versed in the principles of the science of medicine (<term>Áyurveda</term>), but unskilled in his art due to a lack of practice, loses his presence of mind at the bedside of his patient, just as a coward is at his wit’s end to determine what to do when he finds himself in the ranks of a fighting army for the first time. On the other hand, a physician experienced in his art but deficient in the knowledge of the <term>Áyurveda</term> is condemned by all good men as a quack and deserves capital punishment at the hands of the king. Both these classes of physicians are not to be trusted because they are inexpert and half-educated. Such men are incapable of performing the duties of their vocation, just as a one-winged bird is incapable of flying in the air. Even a panacea or a medicine of divine virtues administered by an unpracticed or ignorant physician will prove as positively harmful as a drink of poison, a blow with a weapon, or a thunderbolt. A physician ignorant of the science and art of surgery and emollient measures (<term>Sneha-karma</term>, etc.) is nothing more than a killer of men acting out of greed, and who is allowed to carry on his wicked trade only through the king's oversight. A physician well-versed in the principles of surgery and experienced in the practice of medicine is uniquely capable of curing diseases, just as only a two-wheeled cart can be useful on a battlefield.

<summary>This page emphasizes the necessity for a physician to possess both theoretical knowledge of the Ayurveda and practical clinical experience. It warns that a physician lacking either is dangerous to patients and compared to a one-winged bird or a coward in battle.</summary>
<keywords>Áyurveda, Sneha-karma, medical ethics, physician training, surgery, clinical practice, quackery, medical education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions regarding the education of a physician, focusing on the specific method of recitation and the student's conduct during the study of the Áyurveda.</meta>

<header>Chap. III. SUTRASTHÁNAM.</header>

Now listen to me, my child, as I describe the method of studying the science of the <term>Áyurveda</term>. After the pupil has worshipped and recited his daily prayers, he should sit calmly near his teacher, remaining pure in both body and mind. The teacher should then instruct him in a full <term>Shloka</term> or couplet of the <term>Áyurveda</term>, or a half or a quarter of it, according to the student's intellectual capacity. Then the teacher should provide a full and detailed explanation of the recited couplet or any part of it and ask his pupils to do the same individually. Once the pupils have explained it to the teacher's satisfaction, he should recite the same stanza or couplet again. The passages or <term>shlokas</term> should not be recited too quickly, nor drawled out in a timid or shaky voice, nor with a nasal tone. The voice should be neither too loud nor too weak, but every sound should be clearly and distinctly articulated. The lips, eyes, eyebrows, hands, and other body parts should not be raised or moved to keep time with the recitation. No one should be allowed to walk between the pupil and the teacher during the time of study.

### Authoritative verses on the subject:—

A pupil who is pure, obedient to his teacher, applies himself steadily to his work, and abandons laziness and excessive sleep, will reach the completion of the science he has been studying.

A student or a pupil, having finished the course of
