# CHAPTER IV.

We shall now discuss the chapter that deals with General Explanations (**Prabhāsanīyamadhyāyam**).

The efforts of a man who has studied the entire <term>Ayurveda</term> (<term>shastra</term>) but fails to provide a clear explanation of it are as futile as the labors of a donkey carrying a load of sandalwood, never being able to enjoy its pleasant scent.

**Authoritative verse on the subject:** A foolish person who has read many books without gaining any real insight into the knowledge presented in them is like a donkey laden with logs of sandalwood; he labors under the weight he carries without being able to appreciate its value.

Therefore, the teacher will clearly explain each <term>shloka</term>, or a half or a quarter part of one, as contained in this work—which is divided into one hundred and twenty chapters, as well as the concluding portion of the <term>Uttara-Tantram</term> appended to it—and the student or disciple shall attentively listen to everything explained or discussed by the teacher. Since it is extremely difficult to classify drugs, taste, quality (<term>Guna</term>), potency (<term>Virya</term>), and their transformatory or reactionary effect...

<summary>This chapter emphasizes that studying medical texts without true understanding is as useless as a donkey carrying sandalwood without smelling its scent. It establishes the duty of the teacher to explain the text in detail and the student to listen attentively.</summary>

<keywords>Prabhāsanīyamadhyāyam, Ayurveda, medical education, teacher-student relationship, shloka, sandalwood analogy, Uttara-Tantram, Guna, Virya</keywords>

THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [Chap. IV.

(<term>Vipáka</term>), fundamental bodily principles (<term>Dhátu</term>), bodily excrement (<term>Mala</term>), hollow viscera (<term>Áshaya</term>), vital parts (<term>Marma</term>), veins (<term>Sirá</term>), nerves (<term>Snáyu</term>), joints (<term>Sandhi</term>), bones (<term>Asthi</term>), and the fecundating principles of semen and ovum; or how to extricate any foreign matter lodged in an ulcer; or how to ascertain the nature and position of ulcers or fractures; or the palliative, curable, or incurable nature of a disease, and so forth. Since these subjects perplex even the most profound intellects, even though they are discussed and pondered over a thousand times—not to mention men of comparatively smaller intellectual capacity—it is therefore absolutely mandatory for a pupil or a disciple to listen attentively to the explanation of each <term>shloka</term>, or even a half or a quarter part of one, provided by the teacher while studying the science of medicine.

For explanations of truths and principles quoted from other branches of science or philosophy and incidentally discussed in the present work, the student is referred to the explanations made by the masters of those specific sciences or philosophies, as it is impossible to deal with all branches of science in a single book within such a short scope.

### Authoritative verses on the subject:

By the study of a single <term>Shástra</term>, a person can never grasp the true meaning of this Science of Medicine. Therefore, a physician should study as many allied branches of science or philosophy as possible. The

physician who studies the Science of Medicine from the lips of his preceptor, and practices medicine after having acquired experience in his art by constant practice, is the true physician, while any other man dabbling in the art should be looked upon as an impostor.

The <term>Shalya-Tantras</term> (surgical works) written or propagated by <term>Aupadhenava</term>, <term>Aurabhra</term>, <term>Sushruta</term>, and <term>Paushkalávata</term> are the bases of the works or <term>Tantras</term> written by others (<term>Karavirya</term>, <term>Gopura-rakshita</term>, etc.).

# CHAPTER V.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats the preliminary measures in connection with the curative remedies of a disease.* **(Agropaharniyam-adhyæyam).**

The entire course of medical treatment in connection with a disease may be grouped under three subheadings: the preliminary measures (**Purva-karma**); the principal therapeutic or surgical applications (**Pradhána-karma**); and the after-measures (**Paschát-karma**). These measures will be discussed under the heading of each disease as we have occasion to deal with them. As the present treatise principally

<note>* Several authorities hold that acts such as fasting, the administration of purgatives, etc., should be included within the preliminary measures; the application of absorbent (**Páchana**) or healing medicinal agents within the second or the principal measures; and the administration of tonics or restoratives within the third or the after-measure group. Others, on the contrary, lay down that measures adopted for the absorption, lubrication (pacification by the application of oily substances), or elimination of the deranged bodily humors by sweating should be grouped under the first subheading (**Purva-karma**); the administration of active purgatives, emetics, etc., under the second (**Pradhána-karma**); and the giving of rice meals, etc., to the patient under the last (**Paschát-karma**). While according to others, the active medicinal agents employed to cope with the deranged humors in the incubative stage of a bodily disease until the appearance of its first characteristic symptoms should be called the preliminary measure; measures employed for the suppression of a disease in its patent or fully developed stage as the **Pradhána-karma**; and measures employed to guard against the recurrence of a disease and for the restoration of health in a patient is the sequel treatment or the **Paschát-karma**.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the three stages of medical treatment: preliminary measures (Purva-karma), principal applications (Pradhána-karma), and after-measures (Paschát-karma). It includes various scholarly perspectives on which medical actions, such as fasting or the use of tonics, belong to each stage.</summary>

Since this treatise principally deals with surgical acts or operations, we will discuss them and their accessories at the outset.

Surgical acts or operations are divided into eight different kinds: Incising (*Chhedya*), Excising (*Bhedya*), Scraping (*Lekhya*), Puncturing (*Vedhya*), Probing (*Eshya*), Extracting (*Ahárya*), Draining fluids (*Visrávya*), and Suturing (*Seevya*). A surgeon (*Vaidya*) called upon to perform any of these eight types of operations must first equip himself with accessories such as surgical appliances and instruments, alkalis, fire, probes or directors (*Shaláka*), horns, leeches, a gourd (*Alávu*), *Jámvavoushtha* (a pencil-shaped rod made of slate with its tip shaped like a Jambu fruit), cotton, lint, thread, leaves, fiber (*Patta*), honey, clarified butter, lard, milk, oil, *Tarpanam* (powdered wheat soaked in water), decoctions (*Kasháya*), medicated plasters, paste (*Kalka*), a fan, cold water, hot water, and cauldrons, etc. Moreover, he should secure the services of loyal and steady-nerved attendants.

Then, under the auspices of favorable astrological combinations, and having propitiated the Bráhmanas and the physicians with gifts of curd, sun-dried rice, cordials, and gems, and after making offerings to the gods and uttering benedictions, the surgeon should begin his work. The patient should be given light food before the procedure and made to sit with

An incision (<term>Vrana</term>) that is wide, long, and equally and evenly divided should be considered the best.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:**—An incision that is wide, long, well-divided, does not involve any vital part, etc., of the patient, and is optimally timed, is the best of its kind <note>Certain commentators interpret the couplet as follows: A boil or an abscess which is wide, long, well-defined in its shape, equally suppurated in all its parts, and does not involve any vital part of the body is the most suitable for a surgeon's knife—Translator.</note>. Courage, dexterity, steady hands, a lack of sweating, sharp instruments, self-confidence, and self-command are the qualities a surgeon should possess when opening a boil or an abscess.

Two or three incisions should be made if a single opening does not seem large enough for the purpose.

**Authoritative verse on the subject:**—The knife (lancet) should be freely used wherever a fissure, sinus, or a cavity appears in a boil, so as to ensure the complete flowing out of the pus accumulated in it.

**The authoritative verses on the subject:**—Accordingly, a wound or an ulcer should be made to heal only after both its interior and exterior have been fully purified. Even after the wound has healed, the patient should carefully avoid all sexual intercourse, indigestible foods, fatiguing physical exercises, and indulgence in emotions of grief or fear, or in ecstasies of joy, until the <term>cicatrix</term> <note>scar tissue</note> has acquired enough toughness. The dressings and bandages should be untied and changed every third day in winter, in spring, and in the season of <term>Hemanta</term> <note>the early winter or cool season</note>, and every other day in summer and during the rainy season. However, a physician should not be guided by these rules in cases where there are reasons to fear imminent danger; in such cases, the wound or the ulcer—like a house in flames—should be treated as speedily as possible.

Clarified butter <note>ghee</note> boiled with <term>Yashtimadhu</term> <note>licorice</note> and applied warm to a surgical wound is sure to alleviate the excruciating pain that is usually experienced in such an affected area.

# CHAPTER VI.

Now we shall discuss the chapter that treats the characteristic features of the different seasons of the year and their influence on health and drugs (**<term>Ritucharyādhyāyam</term>**).

Eternal Time is without origin, middle, or end; it is self-begotten and the lord of all attributes. The variation or consistency of the natural attributes of drugs or substances endowed with characteristic tastes, such as sweet, etc., are brought about by time; and time is the principal factor that controls the birth and death of all beings.

## **Etymology of the term <term>Kāla</term> (time):—**

The <term>Kāla</term>, or Eternal Time, is so named because it does not allow even one of its own minutest particles or subdivisions (<term>Kalā</term>) to perish, though it is perpetually moving and in constant motion within itself; or it derives its name from the fundamental quality of destroying all beings and laying their dead remains in heaps in succession. Some assert that the name is due to the fact that time blends (<term>kalanam</term>) all beings with misery or happiness according to their respective actions, or because it leads all beings to destruction (<term>kāla</term>).

The Sun-god, through his unique motions, divides eternal time, which is measured by years (<term>Samvatsaras</term>)

<vocab>Ritucharyādhyāyam, Kāla, Kalā, kalanam, Samvatsaras, Time, Sun-god, Etymology</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter VI, defining "Eternal Time" (Kāla) as a self-begotten force that governs the attributes of drugs and the life cycles of all beings. It explores the etymology of the word Kāla, linking it to concepts of preservation, destruction, and the movement of the sun.</summary>
<keywords>Kāla, time, Ritucharyādhyāyam, seasons, medicine, etymology, Samvatsaras, Sun-god, Ayurveda, philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of time from the previous page, detailing how the Sun-god divides eternal time into smaller, progressive units and categorizes the months into six distinct seasons.</meta>

THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. VI.

...into increasingly progressive and smaller subdivisions such as <term>Nimeshas</term> (the time taken to close the eyelids), <term>Kashthas</term>, <term>Kalas</term>, <term>Muhurtas</term>, days and nights, fortnights, months, seasons, solstices, years, and <term>Yugas</term>.

The time taken to articulate any of the short vowels (such as 'A', etc.) is called an <term>Akshi-Nimesha</term>. Fifteen <term>Akshi-Nimeshas</term> make one <term>Kashtha</term>. Thirty <term>Kashthas</term> make one <term>Muhurta</term>. Thirty <term>Muhurtas</term> make one day and night. Fifteen days and nights make one fortnight. A fortnight is either dark or bright. Two fortnights make one month. The twelve months, such as <term>Magha</term> and others, are divided into six seasons: Winter, Spring, Summer, the Rainy Season, Autumn, and <term>Hemanta</term> <note>pre-winter or early winter</note>, each consisting of two months.

The two months known as <term>Tapas</term> and <term>Tapasya</term> (<term>Magha</term> and <term>Phalguna</term>) constitute the season of winter. Spring consists of the two months called <term>Madhu</term> and <term>Madhava</term> (<term>Chaitra</term> and <term>Vaishaka</term>). Summer is marked by the two months known as <term>Shuchi</term> and <term>Shukra</term> (<term>Jaistha</term> and <term>Ashadha</term>). The Rainy Season is marked by the two months called <term>Nabhas</term> and <term>Nabhasya</term> (<term>Shravana</term> and <term>Bhadra</term>). The two months known as <term>Isha</term> and <term>Urja</term> (<term>Ashvina</term> and <term>Kartika</term>) constitute what is called the season of Autumn. <term>Hemanta</term> is marked by the two months called <term>Sahas</term> and <term>Sahasya</term> (<term>Agrahayana</term> and <term>Pousha</term>). These six seasons are respectively characterized by cold, heat, rain, and other such qualities.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the division of time, specifically describing the two solstices (Ayanams), their seasonal successions, and their physiological effects on living beings.</meta>

## Chap. VI. ] SUTRASTHANAM.

The two <term>Ayanams</term> <note>solstices</note> are introduced by the sun and the moon changing their respective courses in the heavens—passing over the tropics of Cancer and Capricorn—as the measurers of time. The rainy season, autumn, and <term>Hemanta</term> <note>early winter</note> follow one another in succession when the sun is over the Tropic of Capricorn during the Winter Solstice (<term>Dakshináyanam</term>), and the moon gains in strength during this part of the year. <term>Rasas</term> <note>serum or sap</note> possessing acidic, salty, and sweet tastes grow strong and become dominant when the sun is over the Tropic of Capricorn (<term>Dakshináyanam</term>), and all beings gain more strength and energy.

Winter, spring, and summer mark the passing of the sun over the Summer Solstice (<term>Uttaráyanam</term>). The sun grows stronger in heat and light, and the saps (<term>rasas</term>) of bitter, pungent, and sour tastes increase in intensity, and all animals gradually begin to lose strength and energy.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:**
The moon imparts moisture and humidity to the earth, which is soaked up by the sun in its daily course, while the wind, in conjunction with the sun and the moon, contributes toward the preservation of animal life. The successive change of the two solstices marks one year.

Five such complete years count as a <term>Yuga</term>. The subdivisions of eternal time, from the smallest <term>Nimesha</term> <note>the time taken to blink</note> to a complete <term>Yuga</term>, are constantly revolving like a wheel.

<summary>This page describes the two solstices, Dakshináyanam and Uttaráyanam, explaining how the sun and moon influence the seasons, the dominance of specific tastes, and the physical strength of living beings. It also defines the measurement of a year and a five-year Yuga within the eternal cycle of time.</summary>

<keywords>Ayanams, Hemanta, Dakshináyanam, Rasas, Uttaráyanam, Yuga, Nimesha, solstices, Ayurveda seasons, biological strength</keywords>

# THE SUṢHRUTA SAMHITÁ. [ Chap. VI.

and this constant or perpetual revolution is called the wheel or cycle of time (<term>Kála-Chakra</term>) by some authorities.

The six seasons, such as the rainy season, etc., have been mentioned again in this chapter for the purpose of fully describing the accumulation, excitation (aggravation), and balancing of the bodily humours, such as wind, etc. According to some, the rainy season consists of the two months known as <term>Bhádra</term> and <term>Áshvina</term>; Autumn consists of the two months of <term>Kártika</term> and <term>Márgashirshya</term>; <term>Hemanta</term> <note>early winter</note> consists of the two months of <term>Pousha</term> and <term>Mágha</term>; spring consists of the two months of <term>Phálguna</term> and <term>Chaitra</term>; summer consists of <term>Vaishákha</term> and <term>Jaistha</term>; and <term>Právrit</term> <note>the early rainy season</note> consists of <term>Áshádha</term> and <term>Shrávana</term>.

<term>Oshadhis</term> (medicinal plants and cereals) sprout during the rains and have weakened properties. Water becomes muddy or turbid, and the earth is covered with fresh deposits of washed-off or silted mud. The sky becomes overcast with clouds, and the wind, charged with an excess of humidity, dulls the appetite and the systems of living beings. Hence, the food of these beings—which principally consists of tender and newly grown vegetables of weak potency, and is considerably impaired by the turbid water consumed as drink during the season—proves acidic in its digestive reaction and generates excessive bile in the human system. In autumn, the sky becomes cloudless, and the mud is dried

Chap. VI. ] SUTRASTHANAM.

up, and the bile that originated and accumulated during the rains is liquefied by the rays of the sun and gives rise to bilious diseases.*

Plants and vegetables (<term>Oshadhis</term>) that grow or sprout during the rainy season mature over time and ripen in their virtues and potency in the season of <term>Hemanta</term>. Water becomes clear, cool, and heavy in this season. The sun's rays become feeble and mild, and the winds, moistened with frost and snow, make the human system feel slightly numb and heavy. Hence, water and vegetables consumed in <term>Hemanta</term> lose their properties of acidic reaction after being assimilated <unclear>in</unclear> the human system, but they cause an accumulation of phlegm in the body due to their heaviness, sliminess, and cool, oily character. In spring, the phlegm accumulated in the body is liquefied and brings about diseases caused by a deranged state of that bodily humour.†

These plants and vegetables, in turn, lose their sap, moisture, and nutritive elements in summer, becoming dry and extremely light. In the same manner, water becomes parching <note>producing a state of dryness in the organism—<term>Ruksha</term></note> in its quality, and considerably loses its natural coolness and

---

\* This should be regarded as the excited, aggravated, or agitated state of bile (<term>Pitta</term>) in the terminology of <term>Ayurveda</term>.

† This is called the excited or agitated state of phlegm (<term>Kafa</term>).

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how seasonal changes affect the bodily humours, specifically focusing on the accumulation of wind during the summer and its excitation during the rainy season.</meta>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. Chap. VI.

nutritive properties. The sun's rays dry up the natural moisture of the human system. Consequently, when water and vegetables are consumed in large quantities during the summer, they give rise to an accumulation of wind in the system due to their lightness, dryness, or their expansive and dehydrating properties. Subsequently, the wind that accumulated during the summer is agitated by the rains and cold winds in the early part of the rainy season (<term>Právrit</term>), when the ground is flooded with water; this leads to diseases associated with a deranged state of the bodily wind.*

The fundamental bodily humours, such as wind and bile, which increase and accumulate during the rainy season, <term>Hemanta</term>, and summer, should be checked as soon as they become aggravated (manifest symptoms) in autumn, spring, or the early part of the rainy season (<term>Právrit</term>).

Diseases originating from a deranged state of bile, phlegm, and wind are respectively improved in <term>Hemanta</term>, summer, and autumn by natural causes <note>such as variations in atmospheric or ground temperature, rainfall, etc.</note>. So far, we have discussed the accumulation, excitation, and pacification or relief of the deranged bodily humours.

## Chap. VI. | SUTRASTHANAM.

...characterize the different parts of a full day and night. <note>In other words</note>, traits specific to springtime show themselves in the morning; noon is marked by all the characteristics of summer; the evening by those of the rainy season; midnight by those of autumn; and the hours before dawn by those of <term>Hemanta</term>. Similarly, just like the seasons of the year, the different parts of the day and night are marked by variations in heat, cold, and so on. <note>In other words</note>, the unbalanced <term>bodily humours</term>, such as <term>wind</term> and <term>bile</term>, naturally and spontaneously accumulate, worsen, or subside during different parts of the day, just as they do in the different seasons of the year <note>as represented by the parts of the day and night mentioned above</note>.

Water and vegetables keep their natural properties when the seasons follow their natural course and do not show unusual features. During these times, they tend to increase the appetite, vitality, strength, and power of the human system. Unnatural or irregular seasons are merely the consequences of the sins committed by a whole community and signal the workings of a harmful destiny. A season that shows unnatural or irregular features affects or reverses the natural properties of the water and vegetables specific to it. When these are consumed or eaten, they cause terrible epidemics in the country. The best protection is to avoid using such contaminated water and vegetables when an epidemic breaks out in the land.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how environmental and supernatural factors, beyond just seasonal shifts, can lead to widespread disease and depopulation.</meta>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [Chap. VI.

Sometimes a town or a city is depopulated by a curse, anger, sin, or by a monster or a demoness summoned by a spell or incantation. Sometimes the pollen of poisonous flowers or grasses, carried by the winds, invades a town or a village and produces a kind of epidemic cough, asthma, catarrh <note>nasal congestion</note>, or fever, regardless of an individual's constitutional traits or the disturbance of bodily humors. Towns and villages are known to have been depopulated through harmful astral influences, or because houses, wives*, beds, seats, carriages, riding animals, gems, and precious stones took on inauspicious characteristics.

**Preventive measures:** In such cases, moving to a healthy or unaffected area, performing rites of pacification and atonement, (wearing protective gems and drugs), reciting <term>mantras</term>, pouring clarified butter into the sacrificial fire, making offerings to the gods, celebrating sacrificial ceremonies, bowing with palms pressed together to the gods, practicing penance, self-control, and charity, showing kindness, receiving spiritual initiation, obeying one's elders and teachers, being devoted to the gods and the <term>Brámhanas</term>, and observing similar rules of conduct may prove beneficial to the affected community.

# Chap. VI. ] SUTRASTHANAM. 53

**The characteristic features of the seasons which do not exhibit unnatural traits (Metrical texts):**

Cold winds from the north blow in the season of **Hemanta**. The directions of the sky are enveloped in smoke and take on a dusky appearance. The sun is hidden in the frost, and lakes and pools are frozen or lie covered with flakes or thin layers of ice. Crows, rhinoceroses, buffaloes, lambs, and elephants become excited and lively during this part of the year; and the <term>Lodhra</term>, <term>Priyangu</term>, and <term>Punnaga</term> trees begin to blossom.

Winter exhibits the same features as mentioned above, only with a greater degree of intensity; and the directions of the sky are disturbed by strong gales of wind and showers of rain.

In spring, when the summits of Mount Malaya are stained red with the moist footprints of the brides of the <term>Siddhas</term> and the <term>Vidyadharas</term>, and are perfumed by contact with the sweet-scented sandal forests, the lively south wind is roused from its lair and fans joy to young women burning with desire. It kindles the flame of love and calms the amorous anger of lovers by turning their thoughts to themes of love. The directions of the sky are clear and look joyful. The woods are decorated with the full-blown flowers of the <term>Kinshuka</term>, lotus,

<meta>This page continues the description of the seasons, transitioning from the flowers of spring to the heat of summer and the arrival of the rainy season.</meta>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. VI. 54

<term>Vakula</term>, mango, and <term>Ashoka</term> trees blossom. Bees hum, and the notes of the cuckoo are heard echoing through the sky. The south wind fans this king of the seasons, and the forests are draped with garlands of tender, sprouting leaves in his honor.

The sun's rays become stronger and more intense in summer. Unhealthful winds blow from the southeast. The earth is heated; the rivers run narrow and shallow in their beds; the directions of the sky glare with a blazing light; <term>Chakraváka</term> birds and their mates roam about in search of cool ponds and reservoirs of water; herds of deer are tormented and overwhelmed by thirst; trees, plants, and vines are scorched by the intense heat, and withered leaves drop from the trees—leaves which alone make it possible to identify their parent trees.

In the early part of the rainy season (<term>Právrit</term>), clusters of scattered clouds, glittering with lightning and driven by gales from the west, come thundering over and envelop the sky. The earth is dressed in green with a lush growth of grain, enlivened here and there by the dark crimson of cochineal insects (<term>Indragopa</term>), and the <term>Kadamva</term>, <term>Nipa</term>, <term>Kutaja</term>, and <term>Ketaki</term> trees begin to flower.

During the rainy season, the rivers overflow their banks, toppling the trees that grow along them. Ponds and lakes are decorated with full-blooming <term>Kumud</term>

# Chap. VI. | SUTRASTHÁNAM. [ Page 55

and <term>Nilotpala</term> flowers. The earth is covered with lush vegetation. All distinction between dry lands and reservoirs of water becomes impossible, and the sun and the planets are shrouded in dark clouds that pour down torrents of rain but do not thunder.

In autumn, the sun's rays take on a mellow golden tint. Masses of white clouds are seen sailing across the deep dark blue of the sky. Ponds are adorned with full-blooming lotus flowers, stirred by the wings of diving swans. The high ground becomes dry, while the lowlands still remain muddy. The level plains are covered with shrubs and undergrowth, and plants and trees such as <term>Vána</term>, <term>Saptáhva</term>, <term>Vandhuka</term>, <term>Kásha</term>, and <term>Asana</term> flower in abundance.

The bodily humours, such as wind and others, are disturbed and aggravated by the opposition, excess, or variations in the characteristic features of the seasons. Therefore, it is wise to check the deranged phlegm in spring, to overcome the deranged bile in autumn, and to subdue the deranged bodily wind during the rainy season, before they develop into any obvious or visible physical illness.

Thus ends the sixth chapter of the *Sutrasthánam* in the *Sushruta Samhitá*, which discusses the characteristic features of the seasons and their influence on health and medicines.

### CHAPTER VII.

We shall now discuss the chapter that deals with **Surgical Appliances, Their Uses, and Construction (Yantra-Vidhimadhyayam).**

Surgical instruments total one hundred and one * in all, of which the hand is the most important, since all of them rely on the hand as their primary aid, and none of them can be handled without it; furthermore, all surgical operations primarily require its cooperation. Any foreign or external substance that becomes lodged in the human system and becomes painful to both the body and the mind is called a **Shalyam**; and surgical instruments are the means of extracting it from the seat or place where it is embedded.

(Surgical appliances may be divided into six different groups or types, such as the **Svastika**, the **Sandansha**, the **Tala**, the **Nadi Yantras**, and the **Shalakas**, besides those that are called the minor or accessory appliances (**Upa-yantras**).

The **Svastika** instruments (forceps), in turn, are divided into twenty-four subclasses; the **Sandansha** instruments (tongs) into two; the **Tala Yantras**

<note>* According to certain authorities, "one hundred" is used here indefinitely to signify a large number.</note>

# Chap. VII.] SUTRASTHAṆAM

These instruments are all made of iron, which may be substituted with any other similar or suitable substance if iron is unavailable.

The mouths of these appliances are usually made to resemble those of birds and beasts; therefore, they should be shaped like the mouths of specific animals, or otherwise designed according to the advice of old and experienced physicians (surgeons), the directions laid down in the Shástras (medical books of recognized authority), the requirements of the case, or the shape and structure of other appliances used on similar occasions.

**Metrical texts:** Appliances should be made neither too large nor too small, and their mouths or edges should be sharp and keen. They should be constructed with a special focus on strength and stability, and they should be provided with convenient handles.

Appliances of the Svastika class should measure eighteen fingers in length; their mouths should be made to resemble those of lions, tigers, wolves, hyenas, bears, cats, jackals, deer, Ervárukas (a species of deer), crows, cormorants,

Instruments of this type should be used to extract any thorn or foreign matter which may have entered the bones.

**Sandanshas** (tongs) are divided into two classes: those joined with a bolt and those without. They should be made sixteen fingers in length and should be used to withdraw any thorn-like substance from beneath the skin, flesh, veins, or nerves.

The **Tála Yantras**, which measure twelve fingers in length, may be divided into two classes: the single Tála and the double Tála. The former resemble fish scales in shape, while the latter, according to certain authorities, are made to resemble the entire mouth of a fish of the Bhetuli species. These Yantras are used to extract splinters from inside the nose, ears, and other external channels or passages of the body.

These are the twelve defects of a surgical instrument.

The Kanka-mukhas are the best of all other types of instruments, because they can be inserted and taken out without the least difficulty, are capable of drawing out a <term>Shalyam</term> with the greatest ease, and are applicable to all parts of the human body (whether they are an artery or a bone joint).

# CHAPTER VIII.

Now we shall discuss the chapter that treats instruments used in connection with a surgical operation (**Shastravacharaniyamadhyayam**).

These instruments are twenty in number, such as: the *Mandalágram*, the *Karapatram*, the *Vriddhipatram*, the *Nakhashastram*, the *Mudriká*, the *Utpalapatram*, the *Arddhadháram*, the *Suchi*, the *Kushapatram*, the *Átemukham*, the *Sharárimukham*, the *Antarmukham*, the *Trikurchakam*, the *Kutháriká*, the *Vrihimukham*, the *Ará*, the *Vetasapatrakam*, the *Vadisha*, the *Dantashanku*, and the *Eshani*.<note>The Mandalágram measures six fingers in length and has a round or circular face. The Karapatram is the same as a modern saw. The term Vriddhipatram signifies a razor. A Vriddhipatram measures seven fingers in length, with the handle alone measuring five fingers. The Nakhasastram is the same as a modern nail-clipper, with the blade of the instrument measuring one finger in width. The Utpalapatram resembles a lotus leaf in shape. The Arddhadháram (lancet) measures eight fingers in length; it is one finger wide at the middle and two fingers wide at the blade. The Suchi is the same as a modern needle. The Kushapatram is named for its resemblance to the blade of Kusha grass. An Átemukham resembles the beak of a bird of the *Áte* species. The blade of an Átemukham measures two fingers in length, and the handle measures five fingers, totaling seven fingers in length. The Sharárimukham (scissors) is named for the resemblance of its blades to the beaks of a *Sharári* bird; it looks somewhat like a modern blacksmith’s clipper, with a total length of twelve fingers. The Antarmukham is semicircular in shape and has a toothed edge like that of a hand-saw. The Trikurchakam (trocar) has three separate blades. The space between the two blades attached to a handle (which measures five fingers in length) is equal to the width of a Vrihi seed; its total length is eight fingers.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter VIII, listing twenty specific surgical instruments and providing detailed descriptions of their dimensions and forms. The text compares these ancient tools to natural objects, birds, and modern implements like saws and needles.</summary>

### Chap. VIII. ] SUTRAS THANAM.
<sig>9</sig>

Of the instruments mentioned above, the *Mandalágram* and the *Karapatram* should be used for incising and scraping. The *Vriddhipatram*, the *Nakhasastram*, the *Mudriká*, the *Utpalapatram*, and the *Arddhadháram* should be employed for incising (*Chhedanam*) and excising (*Bhedanam*); and the *Kushapatram*, the *Shuchi*, the *Átemukham*, the *Sharárimukham*, the *Trikurchakam*, and the *Antarmukham* should be used for draining or secreting (*Visrávanam*). The *Kutháriká*, the *Vrihimukham*, the *Ará*, the *Vetasapatram*, and the *Suchi* (needle) should be used for puncturing. The *Vadisha* and the *Danta-Shanku* should be used to extract solid bodies. The *Eshani* probe or director is used for probing or searching the course or direction of pus (in a suppurated part), and the *Suchi* (needle) should be used for suturing. Thus, we have explained the eight different functions of the instruments in connection with surgical operations.

The *Kutháriká* (a small, blunt axe) measures seven and a half fingers in the handle; the blade is half a finger in width and is blunted like the tooth of a cow. The *Vrihimukham* measures six fingers in its entire length, and its tip is like that of a *Vrihi* seed, with the edge cut into small, thorn-like projections. The *Ará* resembles a cobbler's awl and measures ten fingers in its entire length; the blade is as wide as a sesame seed and has the girth of a *Durva* grass stem. The *Vetasapatram* (knife) resembles the leaf of a *Vetasa* plant. The blade is four fingers in length, one finger in width, and is keenly edged, with the handle also measuring four fingers in length. The *Vadisha* is shaped like a modern fishing hook. The *Danta-shanku* (pincers for extracting teeth) somewhat resembles the *Vrihimukham* in shape. The tip of an *Eshani* (probe) is like that of a *Gandupáda* (earthworm).

<summary>This page categorizes various surgical instruments into eight functional groups, such as incising, puncturing, and suturing, while providing specific anatomical descriptions and measurements for several tools.</summary>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [Chap. VIII.

Now we shall discuss the mode of handling the instruments mentioned above. The <term>Vriddhipatram</term> and other instruments for excising (<term>Bhedánam</term>) should be held at a point between the blade and the handle. During scraping, the <term>Vriddhipatram</term> and the <term>Mandalágram</term> should be handled with the palm of the hand slightly turned up. The instruments for draining should be held at the base of their blades at the time of use; however, in the case of a king, an old man, a timid or delicate person, a child, a woman, and especially in the case of a royal prince, the <term>Trikurchakam</term> should be used whenever any draining or exudating operation is necessary. The handle of a <term>Vrihi-mukham</term> should be kept hidden within the palm of the hand, and the blade should be held with the thumb and the index finger (<term>Pradeshini</term>). The <term>Kuthárika</term> should first be supported on the left hand and then struck with the thumb and third finger of the right. The <term>Ará</term>, the <term>Karapatram</term>, and the <term>Eshani</term> should be held at their bases. The rest of the surgical instruments should be grasped according to requirements.

The instruments mentioned above are shaped like the objects their very names imply, as has already been described. The <term>Nakhashastram</term> and the <term>Eshani</term> measure eight fingers in length. The <term>Suchi</term> (needle)

The top ends of the <term>Vadisha</term> and the <term>Danta-Shankhu</term> (dental pincers) are slightly bent down, and their faces are made to resemble sharp thorns or the newly sprouted leaves of a barley plant. The top end of an <term>Eshani</term> closely resembles the mouth of an earthworm. The rest of the instruments are mostly made to measure six fingers in length.

### **Commendable features in a Surgical instrument:**

—Instruments that are fitted with easy-to-grip handles and are made of good and pure iron, well-shaped, sharp, and are set with edges that are not jagged and end in well-formed points or tips, should be considered the best of their kind.

Curvature, bluntness (<term>Kuntha</term>—literally: incapable of cutting hair), unequal sharpness of the edge, rough edges, excessive thickness, excessive thinness, excessive length, and excessive shortness are the defective traits in a surgical instrument. Those possessing the opposite features should be used. However, a <term>Karapatram</term> set with a very rough (toothed) edge may be used for the purpose of sawing bones.

A surgical instrument meant for excision (<term>Bhedanam</term>) should be set with an edge as thin as that of a <term>Musura</term>

An instrument used either in connection with draining secretions or piercing (<term>Vyadhanam</term>) should be set with an edge as fine as a human hair, while an instrument of incision should have an edge half as thin as that of the former.

Surgical instruments should be tempered with one of three substances: alkali, water, or oil. Instruments used in cutting an arrow, a bone, or any foreign matter (<term>Shalyam</term>) embedded in the human body should be tempered with alkali, whereas those that are used in cutting, cleaving, and lopping off the flesh from an affected part should be tempered with water. Instruments used in opening (<term>Vyadhanam</term>) a vein (<term>Shira</term>) or in cutting open a nerve (<term>Snáyu</term>) should be tempered with oil, and should be sharpened upon a species of stone slab resembling <term>Masha</term> pulse in color; their sharpened edges should be protected by being placed in a sheath made of <term>Shálmali</term> wood.

### **Authoritative verses on the subject:**

An instrument that is well-ground, well-shaped, fitted with a convenient handle, capable of (laterally) cutting a hair in two, and made according to the measures laid down in the <term>Shastras</term>, should alone be used in a surgical operation.

**The inferior or substitutive instruments (the <term>Anu-Shastras</term>):—** The skin of bamboos, crystals, bits of glass, <term>Kuruvindas</term> (a type of crystal), leeches, fire, alkali, nails, the leaves of trees known as <term>Goji</term>, <term>Shephaliká</term>, and <term>Shákapatra</term>, the tender sprouts of corn, hair, and the fingers should be included within the category of the minor instruments of surgery, which may be used in certain instances as substitutes for the principal and usual ones.

**Metrical texts:—** The four articles—strips of bamboo skin, crystals, bits of glass, and the rock known as <term>Kuruvinda</term>—should be used by an intelligent physician in incising or excising (<term>Bhedanam</term>) operations when the patient is found to have a dread of the knife, is too young to be operated upon with it, or where the proper instrument cannot be obtained. The fingernails should be used in operations of incising, excising, or extracting (in substitution for the instruments prescribed to be used for the purpose) when such a course appears feasible. The processes of applying alkalis, leeches, and cauterization will be dealt with later on. In diseases affecting the eyelids or the cavity of the mouth, operations for the purposes of secreting or evacuating accumulated pus or phlegm may be performed with the leaves of <term>Shákapatra</term>, <term>Shephaliká</term>, or <term>Gojis</term>. # CHAPTER IX.

->Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats the practical instructions in surgical operations (**Yogya-Sutra**).<-

The teacher should ensure that his student practices surgery even if he has already thoroughly mastered the various branches of medical science or has studied them in their entirety. In all acts related to surgical operations, such as making incisions and the injection of oil, the student should be fully instructed regarding the pathways or areas (<term>Karma-patha</term>) through which the operations or applications are to be performed. A student who is otherwise well-read but lacks practical experience is not competent to perform the medical or surgical treatment of a disease. 

The technique of making specific types of incisions should be taught by making cuts in the body of a <term>Pushpaphala</term> (a type of gourd), <term>Alávu</term> (a bottle gourd), watermelon, cucumber, or <term>Erváruka</term>. The technique of making cuts in either an upward or downward direction should be taught in a similar way. The technique of making excisions should be practically demonstrated by making openings in the body of a full water-bag, the bladder of a dead animal, or the side of a leather pouch filled with slime or water. The technique of scraping should be taught on a piece of skin where the hair has been left intact. The technique of venesection

<summary>This chapter introduces the practical training (Yogya-Sutra) necessary for surgeons, emphasizing that theoretical knowledge must be supplemented by hands-on practice using fruits, water-bags, and animal skins. It details specific methods for practicing incisions, excisions, and scraping to prepare students for human surgery.</summary>

<keywords>Yogya-Sutra, surgical training, medical education, Karma-patha, incisions, excisions, practical surgery, Pushpaphala, Alávu, Erváruka</keywords>

(<term>Vedhya</term>) should be taught on the veins of a dead animal, or with the help of a lotus stem. The art of probing and stuffing should be taught on wood eaten by worms (<term>Ghuna</term>), or on the reed of a bamboo, or on the mouth of a dried bottle gourd (<term>Alávu</term>). The art of extracting should be taught by withdrawing seeds from the kernel of a <term>Vimbi</term>, <term>Vilva</term>, or Jack fruit, as well as by extracting teeth from the jaws of a dead animal. The act of secreting or evacuating should be taught on the surface of a <term>Shálmali</term> plank covered with a coat of beeswax, and suturing on pieces of cloth, skin, or hide. Similarly, the art of bandaging or ligaturing should be practically learned by tying bandages around the specific limbs and members of a full-sized doll made of stuffed linen. The art of joining a severed earlobe (<term>Karna-sandhi</term>) should be practically demonstrated on a soft severed muscle or on flesh, or with the stem of a lotus lily. The art of cauterizing, or applying alkaline preparations (caustics) should be demonstrated on a piece of soft flesh; and lastly, the art of inserting syringes and injecting enemas into the region of the bladder or into an ulcerated channel, should be taught by asking the pupil to insert a tube into a lateral opening of a pitcher full of water, or into the mouth of a bottle gourd (<term>Alávu</term>).

## CHAPTER X.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats the essential qualifications of a physician before he formally enters his profession (**Vishikhā-nupraveshaniya-madhyāyam).**

A physician, having thoroughly studied the science of medicine, and fully reflected on and verified the truths he has learned through both observation and practice, and having reached a level of clear knowledge that would enable him to make a clear explanation of the science (whenever necessary), should begin his medical career (start practicing) with the permission of the king of his country. He should be clean in his habits and well-shaved, and should not allow his nails to grow long. He should wear white clothes, put on a pair of shoes, carry a staff and an umbrella in his hands, and walk about with a mild and kind look as a friend to all living creatures, ready to help everyone, and frank and friendly in his speech and behavior, never allowing the full control of his reason or intellectual powers to be disturbed or interfered with in any way.

A physician, having met with a messenger of good omen, or having been encouraged on his journey by the songs of auspicious birds or sights, should go to the house of his patient. <note>Then, having entered the</note>

It also mentions the significance of favorable omens when a physician is called to visit a patient.</summary>

### Chap. X. ] SUTRASTHANAM.

sickroom], the physician should observe the body of his patient, touch it with his own hands, and inquire about their complaint. Several authorities hold that these three—inspection, touch, and questioning—largely constitute the means of ascertaining the nature of a disease. But that is not correct, because the five sense organs—hearing, sight, etc.—and oral inquiry contribute significantly to a better diagnosis.

Diseases which are to be diagnosed with the help of the organ of hearing will be fully treated later in the Chapter on <term>Vrana-Srava</term> (secretions from an ulcer). The wind (<term>Vayu</term>), making the blood bubble, forces it up with a distinctly audible sound and thus affects the sense of hearing. But this will be dealt with later in the aforementioned chapter. The heat and coldness of the body, or the glossiness, roughness, hardness, or softness of the skin of the affected part—as in fever or in an edematous swelling of the body—are perceptible by the sense of touch. Fullness or wasting of the body (*cachexia*), the state and signs of vitality, strength, complexion, etc., are perceived by the sense of sight. Secretions or discharges (from the inflamed mucous membrane of the urethra) in <term>Prameha</term> etc., should be tested with the organ of taste.<note>The sweet or any other taste of the discharges should be inferred from whether or not they are swarmed by hosts of ants or flies, etc.</note> The characteristic smell emitted

Meanwhile, facts such as the time or season of the first appearance of the disease, the patient's caste, and the things or measures that tend to bring about a clear improvement of the disease or prove comfortable to the patient (<term>Sátmyam</term>), as well as the cause of the disease, the worsening of pain, the strength of the patient, and his state of digestion and appetite, the passing of stool, urine, and gas (or their stoppage), and the progression of the disease over time, should be specifically determined by directly questioning the patient on those subjects. Although the aforementioned five sense organs, like the three fundamental vital humours, help us to make a correct diagnosis of a disease, the symptoms locally perceived by these senses should still not be ignored when determining its specific nature.

**Authoritative verse on the subject:** A disease wrongly observed, incorrectly described, or wrongly diagnosed is sure to mislead a physician.

Diseases affecting a Bráhmana well-versed in the Vedas, or a king, or a woman, or an infant, or an old man, or a timid person, or a man in the royal service, or a cunning man, or a man who pretends to possess a knowledge of the science of medicine, or a man who conceals his disease, or a man of an excessively irritable temperament, or a man who has no control over his senses, or a man in extremely impoverished circumstances or without anyone to take care of him, are likely to become incurable even though they appear common or curable at the beginning. The physician who practices his art with regard for these facts acquires piety, wealth, fame, and all the objects he desires in life.

# CHAPTER XI.

Now we shall discuss the chapter that deals with the preparation of alkalis or potential cauteries (**<term>Kshára-páka-vidhi-madhyáyam</term>**).

In cases that require incision, excision, and scraping, alkalis or alkaline preparations are of greater importance than surgical instruments and appliances (both primary and secondary), as they possess the power to subdue the three deranged bodily humours (wind, bile, and phlegm).

The etymological meaning of the term <term>Kshára</term> (alkalis) is based on their property of corroding (the skin or the flesh of an affected part of the body), or on their specific quality of destroying the skin and flesh where such an effect is desired. Since various substances are used in the composition of <term>Kshára</term> (alkalis), they are endowed with the virtue of subduing the three deranged bodily humours. Due to their white color, <term>Ksháras</term> should be categorized as cooling substances (<term>Saumya</term>).

But since many drugs or substances of a hot or fiery nature (<term>Ágneya</term>) enter into their composition, <term>Ksháras</term> (alkalis) are endowed with the properties of blistering, burning, causing suppuration (<term>Páchana</term>), opening, etc., without

-

<summary>This chapter introduces the importance and preparation of alkalis (Kshára) in surgery, noting their superiority over instruments for certain procedures. It explains that alkalis possess both cooling and fiery properties, allowing them to treat various bodily humours and conditions.</summary>

<keywords>Kshára, alkalis, potential cauteries, Kshára-páka-vidhi-madhyáyam, humours, Saumya, Ágneya, Páchana, Ayurvedic surgery</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XI regarding the dual nature (cooling and fiery) of alkalis and their specific therapeutic applications in surgery and internal medicine.</meta>

involving any contradiction to their generic cooling nature (<term>Saumya</term>). Hence, they are included in the list of substances that are both cooling and fiery (<term>Saumya</term> and <term>Ágneya</term>) in their virtues. They are pungent in taste, have a heating potency, and are irritant, digestive, corrosive, absorbent, and liquefying. They improve unhealthy sores and granulation, and act as agents that stop bleeding and cause paralysis. They exercise a destructive action on animal tissues. They are antitoxic and anthelmintic <note>acting against parasitic worms</note>, and they possess the property of curing mucous accumulations in the intestines. They tend to reduce fat and phlegm, and they have the virtue of destroying skin diseases. In large doses, alkalis have the effect of destroying a man’s virility.

<term>Kshára</term> (caustics) may be grouped under two distinct heads (according to their mode of administration): <term>Pratisáraniya</term> (for external application) and <term>Pánia</term> (alkaline potions). Alkaline preparations should be used externally in skin diseases such as <term>Kitima</term>, <term>Dadru</term>, <term>Kilás</term>, and <term>Mandala</term>, as well as for anal fistulas, tumors, bad ulcers (<term>Dushta Vrana</term>), sinuses, skin-tags (<term>Charma-kila</term>), freckles (<term>Tilkálaka</term>), <term>Nacchya</term>, <term>Vyanga</term>, <term>Mashaka</term>, external abscesses, and hemorrhoids. In cases of worms and poisoning, as well as in the seven forms of diseases that affect the oral cavity—such as <term>Upajihva</term>, <term>Upakusha</term>, <term>Danta-Vaidarbha</term>, and the three types of <term>Rohini</term>—external applications of alkalis act as substitutes <note>for surgical instruments</note>.

<summary>This page details the pharmacological properties of alkalis (Kshára), noting their ability to digest, corrode, and heal various tissues while warning of their impact on virility. Alkalis or alkaline potions will prove positively harmful to a patient suffering from fever or hemoptysis <note>coughing up blood</note>, to a person of bilious temperament, to an infant, or to an elderly person; and they will cause similar harm in a weak person, or in a patient suffering from vertigo, insensibility, fainting, and <term>Timira</term> (darkness of vision). These preparations of <term>Alkalis</term> should be made in one and the same way by filtering; and we reserve the full description of this process for another occasion.

Alkalis for external application are prepared in three different potencies: the mild, moderate, and strong (extremely irritant). A physician wishing to prepare such an alkali should first purify his body and mind and observe a fast on a day in autumn marked by auspicious astral combinations. <meta>This page continues the instructions for treating alkaline burns and explains the Ayurvedic pharmacological theory of why acidic substances neutralize alkaline caustics through the interaction of tastes.</meta>

->Chap. XI.] SUTRASTHANAM. 85<-

...of the shreds of <term>Amla-Kānjika</term>, sesamum, and <term>Madhukam</term> taken in equal parts and pasted together should be applied to the part burnt with an alkali, in the event that the latter failed to produce the desired effect because the disease was deeply seated. <term>Madhukam</term> and the <term>Kalka</term> paste of sesamum mixed with clarified butter would cause such an incidental ulcer to heal.

Now you may ask the question: how can an acid substance, which is fiery in its nature and heating in its potency, help to subdue the effects of an alkali that possesses similar qualities and properties, instead of increasing them as might naturally be expected? Well, my child, the question can be fairly answered by stating that substances of all tastes enter into the composition of an alkali, except for the acid one. The pungent (<term>Katu</term>) taste is the primary taste of an alkali, while the salty (<term>Lavana</term>) taste forms its minor or accessory flavor (<term>Anurasa</term>). Now, when this salty taste is combined with an acid one, it gives up its extremely sharp or irritating property and is thus transformed into a quality of sweetness or a soothing virtue. Therefore, an acid taste tends to allay the burning resulting from an application of alkali (potential caustic) in the same way that water tends to put out fire.

An operation of perfect cauterization with an alkaline application brings about an improvement in the disease, or the disease is entirely subdued, accompanied by

**Authoritative verse on the subject:** An alkali administered by an ignorant physician is to be feared more than poison, fire, strikes from a weapon, thunderbolts, or death itself; however, in the hand of an intelligent physician, it is powerful enough to quickly subdue all serious diseases for which its use is recommended.

## CHAPTER XII.

->Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats cauterization and the rules to be observed in its use (**<term>Agni-Karma-Vidhimadhyayam</term>**).<-

Thermal cautery (fire) is superior to an alkali in terms of its healing properties. A disease treated with fire is cured permanently and does not recur; furthermore, diseases that ordinarily baffle the skill of a surgeon or physician, and never prove responsive to medicinal or surgical remedies, are found to yield to fire (cauterization).

The following drugs, articles, and substances should be understood as accessories to the act of cauterization: *viz.*, <term>Pippali</term>, goat excrement, the tooth of a cow (<term>Godanta</term>), <term>Shara</term>, a rod, the surgical instrument known as the <term>Jamvavaustha</term>, articles made of copper or silver, honey, treacle, oil, or any other oily substance. Out of these, <term>Pippali</term>, the <term>Godanta</term>, <term>Shara</term>, and the rod should be heated until red hot and used for cauterizing the affected part in a disease restricted only to the skin. Similarly, the surgical instrument known as the <term>Jamvavaustha</term>, as well as the appliances made of copper or silver, should be used for a disease seated in the flesh. Honey, treacle, and oil should be boiled and employed in cauterizing the disease

Cauterization is permissible in all seasons of the year except summer and autumn; however, no such distinction should be observed in cases of impending danger. In those instances, it should be practiced with the help of appliances of a contrary cooling nature <note>such as wet sheets, cooling drinks, and cooling plasters, etc.</note>.

In all diseases and in all seasons of the year, the patient should be fed a diet of slimy (mucilaginous) food before the cautery is actually applied; however, the patient should be kept on an empty stomach before the act in cases involving <term>Mudagarbha</term> (malpresentation of the fetus), fistula in ano, hemorrhoids, or a disease affecting the cavity of the mouth.

According to certain authorities, the processes of cauterization may be grouped into two categories, depending on whether the skin or the flesh is cauterized. The present work does not forbid the cauterization of any nerve, vein, bone, or bone-joint (as stated before). A burning of the skin is accompanied by a peculiar bursting or cracking sound. The skin becomes contracted and emits a foul smell. Similarly, in a case where the flesh is burned, the affected part assumes a dove-grey or blackish-brown color, marked by pain and a little swelling, and the resulting ulcer becomes dry and contracted. The modes of cauterization vary according to the site of the disease and are four in total, namely: the Ring, the Dot, the Lateral or Slanting lines, and the Rubbing modes.

**Authoritative verse on the subject:**
A physician, after carefully considering the site of the disease and judiciously determining the patient's strength and the locations of the <term>Marmas</term> <note>the vital parts of the body</note>, should resort to cauterization, keeping in mind the nature of the malady and the current season of the year.

After being properly cauterized, the area should be rubbed with an ointment made of honey and clarified butter. A person of a bilious temperament, or one with stagnant blood trapped in any part of the body, or one with loose bowels, should be considered unfit for cauterization. This also applies to anyone with a foreign object <note>such as a thorn or a splinter still lodged in the body</note>, the weak, the elderly, infants, the timid, those suffering from many ulcers, and patients with diseases where <term>diaphoretic</term> <note>sweat-inducing</note> treatments are prohibited.

Now we shall describe the characteristic symptoms of various types of burns other than those performed for surgical purposes. Fire feeds on both fatty and hard fuels <note>such as oil and logs of wood, etc.</note>. Hot or boiling oil has the property of permeating or entering the smallest nerves and veins, and

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XII.

hence, it is capable of burning the skin, etc. Accordingly, an ulcer resulting from such a burn (scald) is characterized by extreme pain, etc.

Burns may be grouped under four distinct categories, namely: the <term>Plushtam</term>, the <term>Dur-Dagdham</term>, the <term>Samyag-Dagdham</term>, and the <term>Ati-Dagdham</term>. A burn characterized by the discoloration of its location and extreme burning, and marked by the absence of any vesicle or blister, is called <term>Plushtam</term>, from the root “plusha,” meaning to burn. A burn which is characterized by the eruption of large vesicles or blisters, assumes a red color, and is characterized by excessive burning and a kind of drawing pain—and which suppurates <note>discharges pus</note> and takes a long time to heal—is called <term>Dur-Dagdham</term> (bad burn or scald). A burn which is not deep (superficial), assumes the color of a ripe <term>Tála</term> fruit, does not appear raised or elevated, and develops the preceding symptoms, is called <term>Samyag-Dagdham</term> (the fully burnt one). A burn in which the flesh hangs down and where the veins, nerves, and bones are destroyed—accompanied by fever, burning, thirst, fainting, and similar disturbances—and which leads to a permanent disfigurement of the body, delaying the healing of the resulting ulcer (which leaves a discolored cicatrix <note>scar tissue</note> even after healing), is called <term>Ati-Dagdham</term> (the over-burnt one). A physician should try to heal any of these four types of burns using the measures already established before.

## Chap. XII. ] SUTRASTHANAM

**Authoritative verses on the subject.**—A person's blood is agitated and heated by fire, and the blood heated in this way tends to excite or cause the bile (<term>Pittam</term>) to rise. And since fire and bile are similar in their taste, essence, effect, potency, and originating factors, the effects of <term>Pittam</term> (such as a burning sensation, etc.) are naturally aggravated and increased through contact with fire. Blisters or vesicles appear in rapid succession and mark the location of the burn; fever, thirst, and other symptoms then follow.

Now I shall describe the course of medical treatment to be used for curing burns. Hot and dry fomentations <note>warm medicinal applications</note>, as well as warm plasters, should be applied to a burn of the <term>Plushtam</term> type, and a course of hot food and drink should likewise be prescribed for the patient. The blood becomes thin when the body is induced to sweat <note>diaphorised</note> by means of warm fomentations, whereas water, because of its natural cooling properties, tends to thicken the blood. Therefore, warm fomentations or applications have curative properties for a burn of the type mentioned above, while water or cold applications produce the opposite effect.*

Both warm and cold measures are to be used for a burn of the <term>Dur-Daghdha</term> type, the

<note>* By stopping the radiation of the trapped heat and thereby encouraging the rise of the local temperature and the increase of the burning sensation.</note>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. | Chap. XII

medicinal remedies consisting of cold applications and ointments of clarified butter.*

A plaster composed of <term>Tugākshiri</term>, <term>Plaksha</term>, <term>Chandana</term>, <term>Gairika</term>, and <term>Amritam</term> (<term>Guduchi</term>), mixed together with clarified butter, should be applied over a burn of the <term>Samyag-Dagdha</term> type, or the flesh of domestic, aquatic, or amphibious animals should be made into a paste and applied over the affected part. A burn of this type, marked by an intense burning sensation, should be medically treated in the same manner as a case of bilious abscess (<term>Pitta-vidradhi</term>).

In the case of a burn of the <term>Ati-Dagdha</term> (over-burnt) type, the loose or dangling skin and flesh should be removed, and cold applications should be applied to the ulcer. Then the affected part should be dusted with powdered <term>Shali</term> rice, or a plaster composed of the powdered bark of <term>Tinduki</term> and clarified butter mixed together should be applied over its surface.† The affected part should be covered with the leaves of <term>Guduchi</term>, or of lotus, or other aquatic plants, and all measures and medicinal

---

<note>\* Cold applications and cooling measures should be used in the case of a deep and excessive burn, while the opposite should be considered the correct remedy in the case of a slight and superficial one.</note>

Now we shall describe the symptoms that appear in a person whose nostrils and larynx are choked with smoke. Breathing becomes labored and rapid, and the abdomen is distended, accompanied by constant sneezing and coughing. The eyes look red and feel as if they are burning. The patient breathes out smoke and cannot detect any smell other than that of smoke. The sense of hearing is significantly affected; the sense of taste becomes dull; fever, thirst, and a burning sensation follow; and the patient collapses, completely unconscious.

### CHAPTER XIII.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats leeches and how and which of them to use **(Jalauka-vacharaṇīyamadhyāyam).**

Leeches should be applied where the patient is elderly, mentally impaired, a woman, an infant, a person of an extremely timid disposition, or a person of a delicate constitution, and as such is not fit to be surgically operated upon, since this mode of bleeding is the gentlest that can possibly be devised. Blood vitiated by the disturbed wind (<term>Váyu</term>), bile (<term>Pittam</term>), and phlegm (<term>Kapham</term>) should be respectively sucked through a horn, by leeches, and a gourd appliance (<term>Alávu-Yantra</term>), or with whichever of them is available at the time, regardless of the cause of such vitiation, whenever such bleeding or sucking is found to be imperatively necessary.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:**

A cow horn is described in the <term>Shastras</term> as having a hot or heat-making potency, and as possessing a slightly cooling (<term>Snigdha</term>) or soothing (<term>Madhura</term>) property. Accordingly, it should be used in sucking blood vitiated through the action of the disturbed bodily wind. Leeches, which are born in water, possess <term>Madhura</term> (sweet or soothing) properties, and hence they

**Mode of application:** The part from which the blood is to be sucked should first be scarified or slightly cut in two or three places. Then, the mouth or open end of the horn—covered with a thin piece of muslin tied around its edges—should be placed over it. The blood is then sucked by mouth through the aperture at its tip or top end, or by using a gourd appliance equipped with a lighted lamp placed inside it.

The term <term>Jalauká</term> (leeches) may be etymologically interpreted to mean creatures whose life (<term>Áyu</term>) or longevity exists in, or depends upon, water. Alternatively, the derivative meaning of the term <term>Jalauka</term> (leeches) is based on the fact that they dwell ("Oka"—dwelling place) in water (<term>Jalam</term>). Leeches may be divided into twelve distinct species, of which six are venomous and six are non-venomous. The six venomous species are named <term>Krishná</term>, <term>Karvurá</term>, <term>Alagardá</term>, <term>Indráyudhá</term>, <term>Sámudriká</term>, and <term>Gochandaná</term>. Leeches that are provided with narrow mouths and are marked by a bifurcating line at the bottom like the scrotum of a bull are called <term>Gochandanás</term>.

A person bitten by any of the venomous leeches mentioned above has an irresistible urge to scratch the site of the bite, which is marked by significant swelling. Fever, accompanied by burning sensations, retching, drowsiness, and delirium, sets in, and ultimately the patient loses all consciousness. The remedy consists of the administration of an anti-toxic medicine known as <term>Mahágada</term>, used as snuffs, potions, and ointments, etc. A bite by an <term>Indráyudha</term> usually proves fatal. Venomous leeches, as well as cures for their bites, have thus been described.

The non-venomous species include <term>Kapilás</term>, <term>Pingalás</term>, <term>Shankhamukhís</term>, <term>Musikás</term>, <term>Pundarimukhís</term>, and <term>Sarávikás</term>. Venomous leeches originate in the decomposed urine and fecal matter of toads and venomous fish in pools of stagnant and turbid water. **Authoritative verse on the subject.**—The non-venomous leeches swim about in sweet-scented waters, live on non-poisonous weeds, lie on the leaves of flowering water plants instead of on the damp and muddy beds of pools, and suck blood from the affected part of a human body without causing any discomfort.

Leeches should be caught with a piece of wet leather, or by some similar article, and then put into a large, new pitcher filled with the water and mud or slime of a pool. Powdered <term>zoophytes</term> and powder of dried meat and aquatic bulbs should be thrown into the pitcher for their food, and blades of grass and leaves of water-plants should be put into it for them to lie upon. The water and the food should be changed every second or third day, and the pitchers should be changed each week (the leeches should be transferred to a new pitcher at the end of every seven consecutive days).

**The authoritative verse on the subject:**—Leeches that are venomous, thick about the middle, elongated, slow-moving, appear fatigued, do not readily attach to the part they are applied to, and are capable of sucking only a small quantity of blood, should be looked upon as not belonging to the proper or recommended type.

# Chapter XIII. | SUTRASTHANAM.

Then, after seating or laying down the patient suffering from a disease that requires the application of leeches, the site of bleeding, if not already ulcerated, should be roughened by dusting it with a mixture of loose earth and pulverized cowdung. Then the leeches should be taken out of their containers and sprinkled with water saturated with mustard seed and turmeric paste. They should then be kept for a moment in a basin full of water; after they have regained their natural energy and freshness, they should be applied to the affected part. Their bodies should be covered with a piece of thin, wet linen or a piece of white cotton. The affected part should be sprinkled with drops of milk or blood, or slight incisions should be made into it if they refuse to attach to the desired spot. Other fresh leeches should be applied if the previous measures prove ineffective. A successful attachment to the affected part can be inferred when the mouths of the leeches assume a horseshoe shape and their necks become raised and arched after attaching to the site of the disease. While sucking, the leeches should be covered with a piece of wet linen and constantly sprinkled with cold water.

Leeches refusing to fall off even after the desired effect has been produced, or sticking to the affected part because of their fondness for the smell of blood, should be sprinkled with the dust of powdered <term>Saindhava</term> (rock salt).

After falling off, the leeches should be dusted with rice powder and their mouths should be lubricated with a mixture of oil and common salt. Then they should be caught by the tail end with the thumb and the forefinger of the left hand, and their backs should be gently rubbed with the same fingers of the right hand from the tail upward to the mouth, with the goal of making them vomit or eject the full quantity of blood they had sucked from the site of the disease. The process should be continued until they show the clearest symptoms of disgorging. Leeches that have vomited the entire quantity of blood sucked, as described above, will move about briskly in search of food if placed in water, while the opposite should be inferred if they lie dull and inert. These should be made to disgorge again. Leeches not made to emit the entire quantity of the sucked

An ulcer resulting from the application of leeches should be rubbed with honey, washed with sprays of cold water, or bound with an astringent (<term>kasháya</term>), sweet, and cooling plaster, depending on the quantity of blood removed from the area.*

<note>* In the case of full and proper bleeding (<term>Yoga</term>), the ulcer should be rubbed with clarified butter technically known as <term>Shatadhautam</term> (literally: "hundred times washed") <term>Ghritam</term> (clarified butter), or a piece of cotton soaked in the same substance should be applied as a compress over the area. Similarly, in a case marked by the absence of any bleeding at all (<term>Mithyá-Yoga</term>), a sour, sweet, and cooling plaster should be applied over the ulcer.</note>

### CHAPTER XIV.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats blood (**Shonita-Varnaniya-madhyāyam**).

The food of a human being, which is usually composed of the five fundamental material principles, can be classified under four different categories <note>such as drinks, edibles, etc.</note>. It has six different tastes, or possesses two potencies <note>cooling or heat-producing</note>, or consists of eightfold properties <note>namely hot, cool, dry, expansive, slimy, mild, sharp, etc.</note> and a variety of other active or efficacious virtues. The food is fully digested with the help of the internal heat and ultimately assimilated into the system, giving rise to lymph chyle (<term>Rasa</term>) which is extremely thin or refined in its consistency and which forms the essence of the assimilated food.

The lymph chyle (<term>Rasa</term>), though running through the whole organism, has its primary seat in the heart, from where it flows through the twenty-four vessels which branch off from the heart to the remotest parts and extremities of the body. Of the mentioned twenty-four vessels, ten course upward, ten course downward, and four have a lateral direction. The <term>Rasa</term> or the

---

<note>It is free from all sorts of impurities such as fecal matter, etc., and permeates the smallest vessels and capillaries.</note>

<summary>This chapter introduces the study of blood by explaining how food is digested and transformed into lymph chyle (Rasa). It describes how this essence of food originates in the heart and is distributed throughout the body via twenty-four primary vessels.</summary>

<keywords>Shonita-Varnaniya-madhyāyam, blood, Rasa, lymph chyle, digestion, five fundamental principles, heart, circulatory vessels</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Rasa (lymph chyle) and its transformation into blood, completing the sentences started on the previous page regarding its circulation and physiological properties.</meta>

# Chap. XIV.] SÚTRASTHÁNAM.

lymph chyle, flowing out from the heart in this way, constantly soothes, maintains, and irrigates the body through transudation. It further contributes to its growth and supports life due to the dynamic effects of causes that lie beyond human understanding. The nature and path of this lymph chyle, which runs through the entire system, can be inferred from the growth, thinning, or other changed conditions of the body.

Now it may be asked whether the <term>Rasa</term>—which permeates the entire body and limbs, and which, by flowing through different chambers (viscera) of the body, is in constant contact with excreta and other morbid humors—possesses a cooling (<term>Saumya</term>) or heat-producing (<term>Ágneya</term>) potency?

The question may be answered by stating that, since the <term>Rasa</term> or lymph chyle is a fluid and possesses lubricating, vitalizing, moistening, and nutritive <gloss>literally: supporting</gloss> properties, it must be included in the class of <term>Saumya</term> (cooling) substances. Although it is a <term>Saumya</term> fluid, the <term>Rasa</term> obtains its characteristic pigment (<term>Rágam</term>) as it passes through the spleen and liver.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:** The <term>Rasa</term>, or lymph chyle, becomes colored through the effect of the body's healthy, normal dyeing heat and thus obtains the name of blood. In women, the <term>Rasa</term> is transformed into the menstrual flow, which begins at the age of twelve and ceases at fifty.

<summary>This page explains how lymph chyle (Rasa) supports life and body growth, and how it transforms into blood as it acquires pigment in the liver and spleen. It also identifies Rasa as the source of menstrual flow in women.</summary>

<keywords>Rasa, lymph chyle, blood formation, Saumya, Ágneya, Rágam, spleen, liver, menstruation, Sútrasthánam</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transformation of Rasa (chyle) into other bodily tissues, specifically focusing on the properties of blood and the sequential development of the body's fundamental principles.</meta>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XIV.

Menstrual blood, although it originates from <term>Rasa</term> which has a cooling potency, is fiery or heat-making (<term>Ágneya</term>) in its character. The fertilized or impregnated ovum (<term>Garbha</term>) possesses both cooling and heat-making properties because it shares the nature of both the menstrual blood (ovum) and semen, which respectively possess the two preceding virtues. Therefore, several authorities hold blood to be identical to the life-blood or the vital principle of a living organism; as such, it is the product of the five fundamental material principles (<term>Pánchabhautikam</term>).

**Metrical texts:** In blood, properties such as a raw or fleshy smell, fluidity, redness, lightness, and mobility—which respectively characterize the fundamental principles (of earth, water, fire, air, and sky)—are to be found, thus representing those specific elements in its composition.

The chyle produces blood. From blood, flesh is formed. From flesh originates fat, which gives rise to bones. From bones originates marrow, which, in its turn, generates semen.

The <term>Rasa</term> that originates from digested or assimilated food and drink primarily strengthens all the fundamental principles of the body.

The <term>Purusha</term>, or self-conscious personality, is chyle—
