### Chap. XIV. | SUTRASTHÁNAM

is born in its origin, and hence an intelligent person should carefully preserve his bodily <term>Rasa</term> (lymph chyle) by adopting a proper regimen of diet and conduct.

The term <term>Rasa</term> is derived from the root "Ras," meaning to go, and the substance is so called from the fact of its continually flowing through and permeating every vital principle of a living organism.

The <term>Rasa</term> is successively transformed into each of the six remaining fundamental principles of the body, and continues in the shape of each for the period of three thousand and fifteen <term>kalás</term> (five days according to our modern computation). Thus the <term>Rasa</term> is converted into semen, or into the menstrual blood (ovum) in women, in the course of a month.*

---

* The successive development of the fundamental or root principles of the body follows a distinct order. The essence of the assimilated food matter under the heat of digestion goes towards the formation of chyle, and is ultimately transformed into it, its excreted and waste residue being passed out of the organism in the shape of stool, etc. The chyle thus produced is called the immature <term>Rasa</term>, or the <term>Rasa</term> in its early stage. Subsequently it enters into the bodily principle of <term>Rasa</term>, becomes matured by the native heat of the latter, and is resolved into three factors—or in other words, its excreted matter is transformed into phlegm, its thick or condensed portion is transformed into and assimilated in the matured <term>Rasa</term> of the body, whereas its subtle essence is transformed into blood. The blood, thus newly generated, is merged into the fundamental organic principle of blood; and there, by the heat of the latter, it is again resolved into three factors: namely, its excreted portion is transformed into bile, its thick or condensed portion is transformed or assimilated into the fundamental organic principle of blood, and its subtle essence is transformed into flesh. The flesh, thus newly formed, is merged into the fundamental organic principle of flesh, and there, by the native heat of the latter, it is resolved into three

The text details how metabolic heat refines chyle into blood and flesh while producing waste products like phlegm and bile.</summary>

THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XIV.

### Authoritative verse on its computation:
In the present work, as well as in other works of recognized authority, a month is calculated to consist of eighteen thousand and ninety <term>Kalás</term>.

The said <term>Rasa</term> courses through the whole body in invisible currents of zigzag shape, like the waves of sound, or in an upward direction like flames of fire, or in a downward direction like rivulets of water.

factors, *namely*, its excreted portion goes toward the formation of such waste as is found to be deposited in the corners of the eyes and inside the folds of the prepuce, or about the region of the glans penis; its thick or condensed portion is transformed into the organic principle of flesh, and its subtle essence is transformed into fat. The fat, thus newly generated, enters into the organic principle of that name, and there, by the natural heat of the latter, is resolved into three factors: *namely*, its excreted portion is discharged through the pores of the skin in drops of perspiration, its condensed portion is assimilated into the organic principle of fat, and its subtle portion is transformed into bone. Again the bone, in its nascent stage, enters into the organic principle of bone, and there, by the inherent heat of that principle, is resolved into three factors: *namely*, its excreted portion goes toward the formation of hair, mustaches, etc., its thick or condensed portion is assimilated into the organic principle of bone, and its subtle portion is transformed into marrow. The marrow, in its nascent state, enters into the organic principle of that name; and there matured under the natural heat of that principle, it is resolved into three factors: *namely*, its excreted portion contributes toward the formation of gelatinous matter deposited in the corners of the eyes, and the oily secretions of the skin, its condensed portion is assimilated into the organic principle of marrow, and its subtle portion is transformed into semen. The semen again, in its nascent stage, enters into the organic principle of that name and there, matured under its natural heat, is resolved into two factors: *namely*, thick and thin. The thick portion is assimilated into the organic principle of semen, the thin one being transformed into (albumen). Semen, like gold a thousand times purified, casts off no dregs. Hence certain authorities hold albumen (protoplasmic matter) to be the eighth or the culminating principle of the body.

<summary>This page describes the movement of Rasa through the body and the sequential transformation of bodily principles from blood to flesh, fat, bone, marrow, and finally semen and albumen. It also defines the measurement of a month as eighteen thousand and ninety Kalás.</summary>
<keywords>Rasa transformation, Kalás, Ayurvedic physiology, bone formation, marrow, semen, albumen, protoplasmic matter, Sushruta Samhita</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the transformation of bodily fluids, specifically focusing on the final conversion of Rasa into semen and its presence throughout the different stages of life.</meta>

Chap. XIV.] SŪTRASTHANAM.

Now it may be asked, since the <term>Rasa</term> is naturally transformed into semen over the course of a month, what is the use of administering medicine that has a stimulating effect upon the reproductive organs (<term>Vájikaranam</term>)? The answer is that such medicines, through their own specific potencies and virtues, help the rapid conversion of <term>Rasa</term> into semen and its profuse emission [on the desired occasion], just as purgatives aid in the thorough evacuation of the bowels.

Again, it may be asked: why is semen not found in an infant? Since the fragrance in a flower bud is imperceptible to the sense of smell, you might as well ask whether there is any fragrance in it or not. But what does not exist in a thing cannot be brought forth in the subsequent course of its development. Just as the fragrance in a flower bud lies latent in its early stage of growth but becomes evident only with the growth of its reproductive organs, so semen or <term>menstrual blood</term> <note>The original text uses "catamenial blood."</note> lies in a potential state in a male or a female child, and appears with the growth of beards and mustaches, or with the enlargement of the breasts, uterus, and vaginal canal, and the appearance of pubic hair.

The same <term>Rasa</term>, originating from assimilated food, serves only to maintain vitality in the elderly and those who have naturally declined due to the exhausted state of the inner vitalizing principle common to old age.

<summary>This page explains that while Rasa naturally becomes semen over a month, specific medicines can accelerate this process, and it uses the analogy of a flower bud to explain why reproductive fluids are latent in children until puberty.</summary>
<keywords>Rasa, Vájikaranam, semen, puberty, menstrual blood, reproductive health, Sushruta Samhita, aging, vitality</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of bodily principles, moving from the transformation of Rasa into semen to the role of the primary Dhátus and the specific characteristics of blood when affected by different bodily humours.</meta>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAḾHITĀ. [Chap. XIV.

The above-mentioned principles (of <term>Rasa</term>, blood, etc.) are called the root principles (<term>Dhátus</term>), because they maintain the integrity of the human organism and protect it against rapid decay. And since the strength or weakness of these bodily principles depends entirely upon the richness or poverty of the blood, we shall now discuss the various conditions of the blood.

Blood that is vitiated by an imbalance of the bodily wind (<term>Váyu</term>) becomes thin, frothy, transparent, fast-moving, and expansive; it takes on a vermilion or black color and loses its sliminess. On the other hand, blood vitiated by an imbalance of the bile (<term>Pittam</term>) takes on a blue, yellow, green, or brown color, emits a fishy smell, becomes thin in its consistency, and is shunned by flies and ants. Similarly, blood vitiated by an imbalance of phlegm (<term>Kapham</term>) becomes cold, glossy, and thick, taking on a color like that of the washings of <term>Gairika</term> <note>Red ochre.</note> or that of a flesh tendon; it takes longer to secrete or flow down and is marked by an increase in its sliminess. Blood that is vitiated by a combined imbalance of all three bodily humours exhibits the characteristics of each and takes on a color like that of <term>Kánjika</term> (sour gruel) while emitting a foul smell. Similarly, blood vitiated through the joint action of any two of the aforementioned bodily humours is characterized by features peculiar to each of them.

<summary>This page defines the Dhátus as the root principles of the body and provides a detailed diagnostic guide for identifying blood imbalances based on the three humours: Váyu, Pittam, and Kapham.</summary>
<keywords>Dhátus, blood, Váyu, Pittam, Kapham, Gairika, Kánjika, bodily humours, Ayurveda, pathology</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the properties of blood, transitioning from the signs of vitiated blood to the characteristics of healthy blood and the contraindications and methods for blood-letting.</meta>

### Chap. XIV. | SUTRASTHANAM.

Healthy blood in its natural state has a bright red color like that of an <term>Indragopa</term> (cochineal) insect, and it is neither too thin nor too transparent.

**Cases where blood-letting is prohibited:**—A person suffering from an edematous swelling extending all over the body should be considered unfit for bleeding. A patient should not be bled if they have a swelling (intumescence) caused by an excessive use of acidic food while in a weak and feeble state, or if they suffer from jaundice, hemorrhoids, or abdominal dropsy. Likewise, a pregnant woman or a person suffering from pulmonary consumption (<term>Shosha</term>) should not undergo blood-letting.

Blood-letting performed with a surgical instrument can be divided into two distinct categories: scarification (<term>Prachchhánam</term>) and venesection (<term>Sirá-Vyadhanam</term>). In these cases, the knife or instrument (<term>Shastram</term>) should be applied quickly and directly to ensure the incision is straight, narrow, contained, and of a consistent, shallow depth (reaching only the surface layer of the flesh and blood). Care must be taken not to injure the local veins, nerves, joints, or other vital parts.

Bleeding performed on a cloudy day or done with a

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITÁ. [ Chap. XIV

A wrong incision, or one performed with full exposure to cold and wind, or on a patient who has not previously undergone diaphoretic treatment (sweating), or on a patient with an empty stomach, is attended with little or no outflow of blood because of the thickened condition of the blood.

**Authoritative verse on the subject:**—Blood-letting surgically performed on a fatigued or exhausted subject, or on a person in a faint, or anyone who is poisoned or intoxicated, or on a person suffering from extreme constipation of the bowels accompanied by the suppression of flatus (<term>Váyu</term>) and urine, or on a person of a timid disposition, or on one overcome with sleep, is marked by the absence of any outflow of blood.

The vitiated blood, failing to find an outlet, gives rise to itching, swelling, redness, burning, suppuration, and pain in the part to which it is confined. On the contrary, blood-letting performed on the body of a person who has been excessively diaphorised (sweated) or heated, or by an ignorant or inexperienced surgeon, or with an injudiciously deep incision, is attended with hemorrhage, which may be followed by such dreadful results as <term>Shirobhitápa</term> (violent headache), blindness or loss of sight (<term>Timira</term>), <term>Adhimantham</term> (ophthalmia), the loss of the vital principles of the body (<term>Dhatu-Kshaya</term>), convulsions, paralysis (<term>Ekánga Vikára</term>), hemiplegia (<term>Pakshágháta</term>), thirst, a burning sensation, hiccups, cough, asthma, jaundice, and even death.

A complete and successful act of blood-letting is followed by a feeling of lightness and the relief of pain in the affected part, by a reduction of the disease, and a general sense of cheerfulness.

A person accustomed to blood-letting enjoys a kind of immunity from all types of skin diseases, <term>sarcomata</term> (fleshy tumors), <term>aneurism</term>, <term>œdema</term> (swelling), and diseases brought about by a corrupted condition of the blood, such as ovarian tumors, carbuncles, erysipelas, etc.

Blood is the origin of the body. It is blood that maintains vitality. Blood is life. Therefore, it should be preserved with the greatest care.

### CHAPTER XV.

Now we shall describe the chapter that treats the increase and decrease of the humoral constituents of the body and waste products (**Dosha-Dhātu-Mala-Kshaya-Vṛiddhi-Vijnāniya-madhyayam**).

Since the human body is composed of humors (<term>Doshas</term>), waste products (<term>Mala</term>), and the fundamental principles (<term>Dhātus</term>) such as blood, marrow, etc., hear me discuss the features that are unique to each of them.

**The Váyu.**—The imparting of motion to the body (**Praspandanam**), the carrying of sensations from the respective sense organs (**Udvahanam**), the passing down of food to its proper receptacles (**Puranam**), the separation of waste products from the assimilated food matter (**Viveka**), and the retention and evacuation of urine, semen, etc. (**Dharanam**) should be attributed to the functions of the five types<note>They are called Prána, Udána, Samána, Vyána, and Apána.</note> of <term>Váyu</term> (nerve force) which support the body.

**The Pittam.**—Pigmentation or coloring (**Rágakrit**), the digestion of food and metabolism of tissues (**Paktikrit**), the vitalization and nutrition of the protoplasmic cells (**Ojakrit**), the origination and preser—

**The Shleshmá.**—The function of the five kinds<note>They are known as **Shleshmaka**, **Kledaka**, **Vodhaka**, **Tarpaka**, and **Avalamvaka**.</note> of <term>Shleshmá</term> is to lubricate the interior of the joints (**Sandhi-Samshleshanam**), to contribute to the luster of the body (**Snehanam**), to aid in the formation of healthy granulation in sores (**Ropanam**), to add to the size of the body (**Puranam**), to build fresh tissues (**Vrimhanam**), to impart a pleasant or soothing sensation to the body (**Tarpanam**), to increase its strength (**Valakrit**), and to give firmness to the limbs (**Sthairyakrit**), thereby contributing to the welfare of the body by supplying it with its watery element.

to contribute to the increased formation of blood. The blood, in its turn, increases the healthy glow of the complexion, leads to the increased formation of flesh and muscles, and maintains vitality in the organism. The flesh contributes toward the thickness or fullness of the limbs and leads to the formation of fatty matter in the system. The fat gives rise to the sheen (the formation of oily or albuminous matter) of the body and primarily contributes to the firmness and growth of the bones. The bones, in turn, support the body and contribute to the formation of marrow. The marrow contributes to the formation and increase of semen, fills the internal cavities of the bones, and serves as the chief source of strength, sexual desire, and cheerfulness. The semen gives rise to valor and courage, makes a man romantically inclined toward the female sex, increases his strength and libido, is the sole fertilizing principle in the male organism, and possesses the quality of being quickly discharged.

The excreta, or the fecal matter of a man, is essential for the preservation of the body. It contains the wind and digestion (being primarily connected with the movements of the bodily <term>Váyu</term> and the sensation of hunger). The urine fills the bladder and has the property of washing or draining off the waste or refuse matter of the organism; whereas perspiration tends to moisten the skin.

The <term>Ártavam</term> (menstrual blood) is endowed with the same properties as its arterial namesake and is one of the essential factors in a woman that makes impregnation possible. The fetus or impregnated matter (<term>Garbha</term>) serves to make evident the features characteristic of pregnancy. The breast milk in its turn tends to bring about an expansion of the breasts (of a woman) and maintains the life of her child (by supplying it with the necessary and nutritive elements of food). These <term>Váyu</term>, etc., should be properly preserved in their normal condition.

Now we shall describe the symptoms that accompany the loss or wasting of any of the previously mentioned principles of the body.*

The loss of the bodily <term>Váyu</term> (nerve force) is followed by a state of languor, shortness of speech, uneasiness or a lack of cheerfulness, and loss of consciousness. The loss of (<term>Pittam</term>) is marked by a dullness of complexion, a decrease in bodily heat, and an impaired state of the internal fire (digestive heat). The loss of phlegm (<term>Kapham</term>) is marked by dryness, a sensation of internal burning, a feeling of emptiness in the stomach and other

---

<note>Such a loss or noticeable deterioration of any of them should be attributed to the use of excessive cleansing or cathartic (<term>Samshodhanam</term>) and pacifying (<term>Samshamanam</term>) measures, or to the suppression of the body's natural urges, or to a course of violent or over-tiring physical exercise, or to sexual excesses, or to the use of unwholesome and unsuitable food, or to grief, etc.</note>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XV.

cavities or chambers of the body, looseness of the joints (a feeling as if the joints were all broken), thirst, weakness, and insomnia. In such cases, the medical treatment should consist of remedial agents that are capable of directly contributing to the growth or formation of the humor so lost or deteriorated.

Similarly, the **loss of lymph chyle** is marked by pain around the region of the heart, Angina Pectoris, with palpitation of the heart, a sensation of emptiness or a "gone feeling" in the viscus, and thirst. The **loss of blood** is accompanied by such symptoms as roughness of the skin and a craving for acid food or drink. The patient longs to be in a cool place and asks for cool things, and the veins become loose and flabby. The **loss of flesh** is marked by emaciation <margin>i</margin> of the buttocks, cheeks, lips, thighs, breasts, armpits,<note>The armpits look thin, narrow, and contracted.</note> <margin>220</margin> neck, and the calves of the legs. The arteries seem loose and flabby, and the body seems to be dry and inert, accompanied by an aching or gnawing pain in its limbs. The **loss of fat** is followed by such symptoms as the enlargement of the spleen, a sense of emptiness in the joints, a peculiar dryness of the skin, and a craving for cold and emollient meat. The **degeneration of the bones** is marked by an aching pain in the bones and joints, a wasting of the teeth and gums, and a general dryness of the body. Similarly,

# CHAP. XV. ] SUTRASTHANAM.

The **loss or waste of marrow** is characterized by the production of a smaller quantity of semen, aching pain in the bones, and a breaking pain in the bone joints which have become depleted of marrow. The **loss or waste of semen** is marked by pain in the penis and the testicles, and by an incapacity for sexual intercourse. In such cases, the emission of semen happens only rarely and is then perceptibly deficient in its quantity; the emitted matter consists of a small quantity of semen marked with shreds of blood. The medical treatment under the preceding circumstances should consist of remedies with such medicinal virtues as are found to directly and immediately contribute to the formation of the bodily principle (that has been wasted or lost).

The **loss, absence, suppression, or scanty formation of fecal matter** is accompanied by a sensation of pain in the sides and the region of the heart, and the upward movement of trapped wind or flatus, accompanied by a rumbling sound around the region of the liver and the intestines. Similarly, the **loss (absence or scanty formation)** of urine is marked by an aching pain in the bladder, causing it to dribble or to come out in thin and scanty jets. Here, as in the preceding instances, the remedial agents should consist of drugs which directly contribute to the formation of urine. Similarly, the **waste, absence, or scanty formation** of perspiration is followed by such symptoms as numbness around the pores of the hair, and

In the case of loss or reduction of the menstrual flow, the menses do not appear at the scheduled time or are scanty. The vagina feels congested and painful. Medical treatment in such cases consists of adopting purifying or cleansing measures and administering drugs with heat-producing (*Ágneya*) potency or properties.

The loss or deficiency of breast milk is characterized by a shrunken condition of the breasts and the suppression or scanty secretion of the fluid. The medical treatment in such cases involves administering drugs that generate <term>Kapha</term>.

The atrophy or wasting of the fetus in the womb (during the period of pregnancy) is marked by an absence of movement in the uterus and the walls of the abdomen not being distended. The treatment consists of the application of <term>Kshira Vastis</term> (medicated milk enemas) in the eighth month of pregnancy and prescribing a course of softening (emollient) foods for the mother. <note>Several editions recommend invigorating diets, eggs, etc.</note>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XV.

### SUTRASTHANAM.

Now we shall describe the symptoms that mark the excess (excessive accumulation in the body) of any of the fundamental humors, principles, and excrements of the body.

The quantities of these humors, principles, and secretions are abnormally increased through the use of substances that primarily contribute to their formation in the organism.<note>Several editions read this as an additional text.</note>

An excess of **<term>Váyu</term>** in the body is marked by symptoms such as roughness of the skin,<note>Several editions read this as "roughness of speech."</note> emaciation of the body, darkness of complexion (literally: blackness of hue), a slight tremor or trembling of the limbs, a longing for heat or for hot things, insomnia, thickness or increased consistency of the fecal matter, and a decrease in bodily strength. (Similarly, an abnormal) increase of **<term>Pittam</term>** is characterized by a sallow complexion or a yellowish color of the skin, a general burning sensation in the body as well as insomnia, a craving for cold contact and cooling things, a reduction in strength, weakness of the sense organs, fits of fainting, and yellowness of the conjunctivae, stool, and urine.

An excess of **<term>Kapham</term>** in the body is marked by symptoms such as whiteness, coldness, and numbness of the body, heaviness of the limbs, and a

The sides of the abdomen are increased in size, the body emits a foul smell, and the person is afflicted with cough and shortness of breath (dyspnea). **An excessive formation of bone** (abnormal ossification) is accompanied by such symptoms as the growth of additional teeth and the abnormal development of any of the bone structures. **An excessive formation of marrow** gives rise to a heaviness of the eyes and the limbs of the body.

130 THE SUSHRÚTA SAMHITÁ. [ Chap. XV.

increase in any of the fundamental principles of the body should be checked and reduced to its normal quantity.

Now we shall describe the characteristic features of the strength-giving principles of the body, as well as the symptoms that mark their loss or waste. The quintessence of all the fundamental principles of the body, starting with lymph chyle and ending with semen, is called **<term>Ojas</term>**, which is identical to what is termed "**vital power**." This view of the unity of vitality with protoplasmic albumen has been adopted in the present work. <note>The Sanskrit term "**<term>Ojas</term>**" has a variety of meanings. Primarily, it means protoplasmic matter as found in cells (<term>Vindus</term>). Secondarily, it means albumen, as we shall describe later in the chapters on the etiology and therapeutics of <term>Prameha</term>. Several authorities hold a contrary view, stating that **<term>Ojas</term>** (albumen) forms only one of the essentials of vitality and that the two are by no means identical.</note>

This **<term>Ojas</term>** (albumen) or strength-giving principle serves to impart a firm integrity to the flesh (and the muscles), exercises unbounded control over all acts of vitality, improves the voice and complexion, and helps both the external (operative) and the internal (intellectual) sense organs in duly performing their natural functions.

### **Authoritative verses on the subject:**—**<term>Ojas</term>** (albumen), being of a white color, belongs to the class of **<term>Somátmakam</term>** (cooling) substances.

<summary>This page defines Ojas as the essential vital power of the body, derived from all fundamental principles, and describes its role in maintaining physical integrity, sensory function, and overall vitality.</summary>
<keywords>Ojas, vital power, albumen, protoplasm, Somátmakam, Sushruta Samhita, physiology, lymph chyle</keywords>

# Chap. XV. ] SUTRASTHÁNAM.

It is cooling, oily, and stable (<term>Sthira</term>), contributes to the formation and growth of flesh, maintains its integrity or holds it firm, and is mobile or capable of moving from one place to another within the body. <note>Several editions read <term>Rasam</term>, meaning it possesses a sweet taste.</note> It is further soft and shiny, possesses the most effective power, and should be regarded as the most important seat of vitality. The entire body, with all its limbs and parts, is permeated with <term>Ojas</term>, and a loss or decrease in its natural quantity leads to the gradual wasting away and ultimate breakdown of the body.

A physical blow, a persistent wasting disease, anger, grief, worries and anxieties, fatigue, and hunger are the causes attributed to the wasting or disappearance of this strength-giving principle (albumen) of the body. Due to these causes, the body's albumen is depleted through the channels that carry the different fundamental principles of the body. Albumen is transformed into strength, which radiates from the heart.

An impaired or corrupted albumen (<term>Ojas</term>) is characterized, first, by its displacement from its proper location (<term>Visransha</term>); second, by a change or modification of its natural qualities when in contact with impaired humors or disordered organs (<term>Vyápad</term>); and third, by wasting away (<term>Kshaya</term>).

To the second of the foregoing properties (the change or modification of its natural quality through contact with impaired bodily humors, etc.) should be attributed symptoms such as numbness and heaviness of the limbs, dropsy due to the action of the impaired bodily <term>Váyu</term>, a discolored or changed complexion, a feeling of malaise, drowsiness, and sleepiness. The third property of the impaired albumen (loss or wasting) brings on fits of fainting, loss of flesh, stupor, delirium, and ultimately death.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:**—An impaired state of albumen is marked by the three aforementioned properties: dislodgment from its proper location (<term>Visransha</term>); a change of its natural qualities through contamination (<term>Vyápad</term>); and wasting (<term>Kshaya</term>). The first of these properties (<term>Visransha</term>) is characterized by looseness of the joints, an inert state of the body, a sense of fatigue, a displacement of the impaired humors from their natural locations, and a suppression of bodily and intellectual functions. The loss or waste of *<term>Ojah</term>* (albumen) is marked by such symptoms as fainting fits, emaciation of the body, bewilderment and distraction of the mind, delirium, loss of consciousness, and ultimately death.

The medical treatment in cases of the dislodgment or flowing out (external secretion) of albumen from its natural seat (*<term>Visransha</term>*), as well as in the event of it becoming contaminated by the impaired principles of the body, should consist of improving its quantity through elixirs and remedies possessed of rejuvenating properties, which tend to increase the quantity of such fluid (albumen) in the body. A patient who has lost all consciousness due to an excessive loss or waste of albumen should be given up by a physician (as incurable).

The oily or albuminous matter found within the components of the other fundamental principles (*<term>Dhátu</term>*) of the body (as metabolized by the internal heat and regularly transformed into the succeeding ones) should be grouped under the category of fiery or thermogenetic (*<term>Ágneya</term>*) substances. **Metrical texts:—** A person suffering from the wasting of any of the constituent humours, fundamental principles, or waste products of the body, as well as one suffering from the loss of <term>Ojah</term> (vital essence), naturally craves the drink and food that tend to contribute directly to the formation of the matter (or bodily principle) so lost or wasted. Conversely, the particular food or drink longed for by a person suffering from a loss or depletion of any of the aforementioned fluids or principles should be looked upon as possessing curative properties in that particular case. Such a person, if devoid of consciousness and stripped of his physical and intellectual functions through a deranged state of the bodily <term>Váyu</term> (nerve-force) and extremely weak and enfeebled due to the loss of the vital fluid, should be regarded as beyond cure.

**Etiology of Obesity:—** Obesity or the loss of flesh (<term>Kársha</term>) should be attributed to changes in the condition of the lymph chyle. The lymph chyle derived from the digested food of a person who is accustomed to a diet that tends to increase the quantity of the bodily <term>Kapham</term>, or who is in the habit of pampering his belly even when a previous meal has not been thoroughly digested, or who is addicted to the habit of sleeping during the day, or leading a sedentary life, or is averse to taking

An obese or excessively corpulent person is likely to be afflicted with diseases such as urethral discharges, eruptions, boils, carbuncles, fever, and fistula in ano <note>a chronic inflammatory condition near the anus</note>, or with any of the diseases caused by a deranged state of the bodily <term>Váyu</term>; such attacks are invariably found to end in death. Any disease in such a person is likely to develop into a violent and dangerous type because the internal channels are obstructed with deposits of fat.

# Chap. XV. ] ŚUTRASTHĀNAM.

Accordingly, medicated compositions consisting of drugs and substances such as <term>Shilájatu</term>, <term>Guggulu</term>, <term>Go-Mutram</term> <note>cow urine</note>, <term>Triphalá</term>, <term>Loharaja</term>, <term>Rasánjanam</term>, <term>Madhu</term> <note>honey</note>, <term>Yava</term> <note>barley</note>, <term>Mudga</term>, <term>Koradusha</term>, <term>Shyámaka</term>, and <term>Uddálaka</term>—which possess anti-fat properties—should be prescribed. Other treatments include remedial agents that have the power to cleanse the internal channels, as well as enemas of liquefying solutions technically known as <term>Lekhana Vastis</term> and physical exercise.

**The Causes of Kárshyam <note>Emaciation</note>:—**The loss of flesh or a gradual emaciation of the body should be attributed to eating food containing substances that largely or excessively aggravate the bodily <term>Váyu</term>. Other causes include over-fatiguing physical exercise, sexual excesses, excessive studying, fear, grief, or anxiety, staying up late, unsatisfied hunger, insufficient food, and astringent foods that tend to dry up the lymph chyle. The chyle, dried out in this way, moves through the organism but fails to provide the necessary nutrition because it is insufficiently charged with it, thus causing the body to grow extremely thin.

A patient suffering from extreme emaciation cannot endure the harshness of the weather or variations in environmental heat; they become indifferent to all movement and only imperfectly perform the

## Chap. XV. | <term>SUTRASTHANAM</term>

balanced. A man with such a body is capable of all kinds of work and movement. He can reasonably endure the harshness of the weather and the intensity of hunger and thirst, and he will gain strength and energy. Care should always be taken to maintain such a well-proportioned body of moderate size.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:**—Excessively obese and excessively lean persons are equally to be condemned. A body which is neither too stout nor too thin, but finds the middle ground regarding plumpness, is best. A lean frame should be preferred over a stout one. The disturbed or aggravated bodily humours dry up the <term>fundamental principles</term> of the body, such as <term>lymph chyle</term> and others, just as a well-lit fire will evaporate the water in a basin placed over it. Since the temperament, constitution, size, and <term>fundamental principles</term> of the body vary in different individuals (and since the body, in turn, undergoes gradual transformations such as infancy, youth, and old age, and changes its state every moment), it is absolutely impossible to determine the exact quantity of the imbalanced humours, waste products, and <term>fundamental principles</term> (of <term>lymph chyle</term>, blood, semen, albumen, etc.) that may be found in the human organism. Hence, it is necessary for a physician to determine their state of equilibrium (their continuation in a normal state and

<vocab><term>Sutrasthanam</term>, bodily humours, <term>lymph chyle</term>, equilibrium, physician, obese, temperament, constitution</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the physical advantages of a balanced body and notes that a lean constitution is preferable to an obese one. It also explains that because the body's elements and humours are constantly changing, a physician must focus on maintaining their equilibrium rather than finding fixed quantities.</summary>

Any local veins accidentally injured by an ignorant or incompetent surgeon may result in symptoms described under the headings of **Kālikā**, **Marmarikā**, and **Lohitikā**.

**Kālikā** is characterized by fever, a burning pain in the affected area, and swelling. **Marmarikā** causes pain and nodular (knotty) formations around the affected region, accompanied by inflammatory fever; while in the last named type (**Lohitikā**), symptoms such as Manyá-Stambha (stiffness of the tendons at the nape of the neck), Apatának (a type of tetanus), Shirograha (headache), and Karna-shula (earache) appear; these should be treated with the medicinal remedies prescribed under their respective sections. The lint should be quickly removed from a pierced hole that shows extreme pain and swelling, whether this is due to the use of a blunt, crooked, or stunted needle, or because it was plugged with too deep or excessively large lint, or because it was disturbed by aggravated bodily humours (**Doshas**), or because it was made

Among these, the process known as **Nemi-sandhánaka** should be used in cases where each of the bifurcated lobes of the ears is found to be thick, extended, and equal in size. The process known as **Útpala-Bhedyaka** should be used in cases where the severed lobes of the ears are found to be round, extended, and equal in dimensions. The process **Valluraka** should be resorted to in cases where the severed lobes of the ears are found to be short, circular, and equal in size. The process known as **Ásangima** should be adopted in cases where the front surface of one of these severed appendages has a more elongated shape than the other. The process known as **Ganda-Karna** consists of slicing off a patch of healthy flesh from one of the regions of the cheeks and adhering it to one of the severed lobes of the ears which is more elongated on its front side than the other (plastic operations). In the case of extremely short lobes, the flesh should be cut off from both cheeks and adhered to them, the process being known as **Áháryaya**. The lobes of the ears which have been completely severed from their roots are called **Pithopamas**. The process known as **Nirvedhima** should be resorted to in such cases by piercing the two **Putrikás** <note>tragus and anti-tragus</note> of the ears.

The process known as **Vyáyojima** should be made use of in cases where one of the bifurcated

The process known as <term>**Kapáta-Sandhika**</term> consists of creating a joining, on the posterior side, between one of the split lobes and another which is lengthened on the anterior side of the ear. The joining is so called from the fact of its resembling the closing of the two leaves of a door (<term>**Kapátam**</term>). The process known as the <term>**Ardha-Kapáta-Sandhika**</term> consists of creating a joining on the anterior side between the shorter of the two parts of a split earlobe and the part lengthened on the posterior side, like a half-closed door.

The ten previously mentioned processes of joining may be successfully performed, and their shapes can be easily imagined from the meanings of their respective names.

The remaining five types, such as the <term>**Samkhiptam**</term> etc., are seldom successful and are therefore called impracticable (<term>**Asádhayas**</term>). The process <term>**Samkhiptam**</term> is used in cases where the auricle (<term>**Shashkuli**</term>) has withered and one of the split lobes is raised, while the other is reduced and shortened. The process of <term>**Hina-karna**</term> should be adopted in cases where the supporting rim of the lobe (pinna) has been entirely lost and its outer sides and the cheeks are sunken and devoid of flesh. For three consecutive days, the wound should be anointed with unboiled oil, and cotton soaked in the same substance should be placed over it; this dressing is to be changed every third day until the wound has healed.

<meta>This page continues the surgical instructions for earlobe reconstruction, specifically detailing the complications that arise if the wound is closed while the blood or bodily humors are still unbalanced.</meta>

<header>Chap. XVI.] SUTRASTHANAM. 149</header>

An incidental ulcer should not be healed as long as the local blood (the blood within the ulcer) is not fully purified, or as long as there is hemorrhage from the affected area, or the local blood remains weak. An ulcer joined while even a small amount of <term>Vayu</term>-vitiated blood remains inside will spontaneously burst or break open again. It will again be accompanied by pain, burning, redness, and suppuration if it is closed while a small amount of <term>Pitta</term>-disturbed blood is trapped inside. If joined while even a small amount of <term>Kapha</term>-contaminated blood remains in its cavity, an ulcer is marked by itching and numbness. An ulcer joined while active hemorrhage continues from its inside is marked by a brown or blackish-yellow swelling. An ulcer joined at a time when the local blood—though otherwise good or pure—has been thinned or weakened through excessive bleeding, is followed by a corresponding wasting (thinness) of the joined part. The earlobe thus joined should be gradually pulled down and elongated after the ulcer has completely healed, its associated symptoms have subsided, and the <term>cicatrix</term> has taken on the color of the surrounding skin. Otherwise, the joined part may be characterized by pain, swelling, inflammation, burning, and suppuration, or the union may separate again. A joined earlobe, un-

One should not attempt to elongate an earlobe immediately after the adhesion of its two severed parts, because the center of the adhesion, still being raw, might cause them to separate again. Thus, an earlobe in this condition should be gradually elongated only when it shows hair growth on its surface, the hole or perforation has assumed a circular appearance, and the adhesion has become firmly established, well-dried, painless, uniform, and level throughout its entire length.

The ways to achieve an adhesion of the two severed parts of an earlobe are countless; and a skilled and experienced surgeon should determine the shape and nature of each according to the needs of a particular case.*

\* ***Additional Text :—*** O <term>Sushruta</term>, I will again address the diseases that affect the earlobe under the conditions described above. The disturbed bodily <term>Váyu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, and <term>Kapham</term>, either together or separately, cause several types of diseases that affect the earlobe. Disturbed <term>Váyu</term> produces numbness, an erysipelas-like swelling, and an ulcer around the affected earlobe, while an erysipelas-like ulcer in that area accompanied by swelling, burning, suppuration, and so on, should be attributed to the action of disturbed <term>Pittam</term>. Heaviness, numbness, and swelling of the earlobe accompanied by constant itching in the affected area indicate the action of disturbed <term>Kapham</term>. The medical treatment in these cases consists of reducing the specific disturbed humor by means of diaphoresis (inducing sweat), lubrication, <term>Parishekas</term> (medicated plasters), or bloodletting, as the case may be. These measures should be applied moderately, and nutritive and invigorating food should be pres-

## **Rhinoplastic operations: Now I shall deal with the process of affixing an artificial nose.**

...prescribed for the patient. The physician who is well-acquainted with the actions of the deranged bodily humors as described above, should be considered the only one entitled to take in hand a case which falls under one of the preceding types.

Now I shall list the names of the several diseases which affect a severed earlobe and describe the symptoms which each of them develops in succession. They are known as <term>Utpátaka</term>, <term>Utputuka</term>, <term>Shyáva</term>, <term>Bhrisam-kandujáta</term>, <term>Avamantha</term>, <term>Sakanduka</term>, <term>Akundaka</term>, <term>Granthika</term>, <term>Jámvala</term>, <term>Srávi</term>, and <term>Dáhavána</term>. Now listen as I discourse on the nature of the medicinal treatment to be adopted in each of them.

**Remedies:**—A plaster composed of the drugs known as <term>Apámárga</term>, <term>Sarjarasa</term>, <term>Patala</term> bark, and <term>Lakucha</term> bark pasted together, or a medicated oil prepared and boiled with the preceding substances, should be applied in a case of the <term>Utpátaka</term> type, whereas a case of the <term>Utputuka</term> type would prove responsive to a medicinal plaster consisting of <term>Shampáka</term>, <term>Shigru</term>, <term>Putika</term>, the fat and marrow of a <term>Godhá</term> <note>a large lizard</note>, and the milk and bile of a female deer, buffalo, or sow, pasted together; or to a medicated ointment composed of the above-said substances duly boiled with oil. Similarly, a medicinal plaster composed of the drugs known as <term>Gauri</term>, <term>Sugandhá</term>, <term>Shyámá</term>, <term>Anantá</term>, and <term>Tanduliyakam</term>, or an oil prepared and boiled with the extract of the preceding drugs, would prove beneficial in a case of the <term>Shyáva</term> type of the disease. In a case of the <term>Vrisham-Sakundakum</term> type, the affected part should be rubbed or lubricated with an ointment or medicated oil prepared with the boiled extract of <term>Páthá</term>, <term>Rasánjanam</term>, <term>Kshoudram</term>, and warm <term>Kánjikám</term> <note>sour fermented gruel</note>, or a plaster composed of the same drugs and substances should be applied over the diseased area.

In a case of ulceration, the ulcerated earlobe should be rubbed with the oil prepared and boiled with the drugs known as <term>Madhukam</term> and <term>Kshira-kákoli</term>, or with those which form the group known as the <term>Jivakádi-Varga</term>; while in a case where <term>Vringhanam</term> <note>nourishing or bulk-promoting</note> measures are to be adopted, lard prepared from the fat of a <term>Godhá</term>, boar, or snake might be used with advantage. In the <term>Avamanthaka</term> type, the diseased earlobe should be washed and covered with a plaster composed of the drugs known as <term>Prapaundarikam</term>, <term>Madhukam</term>, <term>Samangá</term>, and <term>Dhavam</term>, or rubbed with oil prepared and boiled with the same drugs. Similarly, a case of <term>Kandu-juta</term> (accompanied by itching) would yield to a plaster composed of the drugs known as <term>Sahadevá</term>, <term>Vishvadevá</term>, and <term>Saindhava</term> salt pasted with goat's milk, or to the medicated oil boiled and prepared with the same drugs and substances.

First, a leaf of a creeper, long and broad enough to fully cover the entire severed or clipped-off part, should be gathered. A patch of living flesh, equal in dimension to that leaf, should then be sliced off (from the bottom upward) from the region of the cheek. After scarifying it with a knife, it should be swiftly adhered to the severed nose. Then, the calm physician should steadily tie it up with a bandage that is aesthetically pleasing and perfectly suited for its purpose (<term>Sádhu Vandha</term>). The physician should ensure that the adhesion of the severed parts has been fully achieved and then insert two small pipes into the nostrils to facilitate breathing and to prevent the adhered flesh from sagging. After that, the adhered part should be dusted with a powder made of <term>Pattanga</term>, <term>Yashti-madhukam</term>, and <term>Rasânjana</term> pulverized together; and

In a case of the <term>Granthika</term> type (characterized by the formation of knotty growths on its inside), the knotty growths or glandular formations should first be removed, and the affected area should be bled with a surgical instrument and dusted with powdered <term>Saindhava</term> salt. Likewise, in a case of the <term>Jámvala</term> type, blood-letting should be performed by scarifying the site of the disease, which should then be washed with a spray of milk. The ulcer should be healed after the thorough purification of its internal morbid contents. A case of the <term>Srávi</term> (secreting) type will readily respond to a medicinal plaster composed of the drugs known as <term>Madhuparni</term> and <term>Madhukain</term>, or of <term>Madhukam</term> mixed into a paste with honey, or to a medicinal oil prepared and boiled with these same drugs and substances. A case of the <term>Dahyamána</term> (burning) type should be treated with a plaster composed of the drugs known as the five <term>Kalkas</term> and <term>Madhukam</term> pasted together and mixed with clarified butter <note>ghee</note>, or with a pasted compound of the drugs that form the group of the <term>Jivakadi Varga</term>, with a quantity of clarified butter added to it.

### CHAPTER XVII.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which deals with the mode of distinguishing between suppurating and non-suppurating swellings. **A'ma-pakkishaniya-madhyāyam.**

Diseases such as **Granthi** (aneurysm), **Vidradhi** (abscess), and **Alaji** (inflammation of the edge of the cornea), etc., are ushered in by a preliminary swelling which subsequently develops symptoms peculiar to each of them. These diseases differ in their symptoms and outward shape. A swelling which may appear at any part of the body, and is round, elevated, even, or uneven in its surface, is called a **Shotha** (swelling). It restricts itself to the skin and flesh of its locality and is characterized by the individual or combined action of the deranged bodily humours. The **Shothas** (swellings) can be divided into six different types according to whether they are caused by the action of the deranged **Váyu**, **Pittam**, **Kapham**, or blood, or are due to the combined action of the three fundamental humours of the body, or are of traumatic origin.

Now we shall describe the symptoms which mark the respective actions of the humours in a swelling. A swelling due to the action of the deranged **Váyu** assumes a reddish or blackish hue and is shifting in its character. It feels rough and soft to the touch,

<summary>This page introduces Chapter XVII, defining "Shotha" (swelling) and classifying its six types based on the three humours, blood, combined humours, or trauma. It begins to detail the specific symptoms of a swelling caused by deranged Váyu.</summary>

<keywords>Shotha, Váyu, Pittam, Kapham, Granthi, Vidradhi, Alaji, suppuration, inflammation, bodily humours, Ayurveda</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of swellings (Shotha) from Chapter XVII, detailing the specific symptoms associated with each of the bodily humours and the signs of impending suppuration.</meta>

->THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XVII.<-

and is marked by a sort of aching pain (peculiar to the deranged <term>Váyu</term>) which vanishes at intervals.

A swelling, due to the action of the deranged <term>Pittam</term>, assumes a yellowish hue. It is soft and fluctuates under pressure, and is marked by an accumulation of blood in its body. It swiftly shifts from one part of the body to another, accompanied by a burning, sucking pain. A swelling, brought about through the deranged condition of the <term>Kapham</term>, assumes a gray or whitish color. The skin becomes glossy and cold, and the swelling very slowly changes its original site, if it shifts at all, accompanied by pain and itching. A swelling engendered through the concerted action of the three bodily humours successively manifests the symptoms and assumes the colors respectively peculiar to each of them. The symptoms which mark a swelling due to the action of the vitiated blood are identical with those which are exhibited in a swelling of the <term>Pittaja</term> type with the exception of the blackness of the part (and an increase of heat). A swelling due to an external blow (traumatic) manifests symptoms peculiar to the <term>Pittaja</term> and blood-origined types.

A swelling, which does not yield to internal and external remedies on account of an excessive accumulation of the deranged local humours, or through the insufficient or contrary effects of the remedial agents, shows signs of suppuration.

<summary>This page describes the specific physical symptoms and colors of swellings caused by Pittam, Kapham, combined humours, tainted blood, and trauma. It concludes by noting that swellings that do not respond to treatment will begin to suppurate.</summary>

<keywords>Váyu, Pittam, Kapham, Pittaja, humours, suppuration, Shotha, inflammation, Ayurvedic medicine, Sushruta Samhita</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of swellings (Shotha) from Chapter XVII of the Sutrasthanam, transitioning from the symptoms based on bodily humors to the clinical progression through the three stages of inflammation and suppuration.</meta>

-># Chap. XVII.] SUTRASTHANAM.<-

Now hear me describe the symptoms that respectively mark an **unsuppurated**, **suppurating**, or **suppurated** swelling. The unsuppurated or immature stage continues as long as the skin of the swelling retains its natural color, characterized by a little pain and heat on the inside, and coldness, hardness, and a slight elevation on its surface.

The suppurating stage causes a sensation of pricking pain in the affected area. The swelling feels as if it is being pricked with needles, bitten, or swarmed over by a host of ants, or as if it were being cut with a knife, pierced with a spear, beaten with a club, pressed with the hand, scraped around with fingers, or burnt with fire or an alkali. The patient complains of a kind of sucking, burning pain in the swelling, which may be fixed or shifting. The patient, as if stung by a scorpion, finds no comfort in any place or position. The color of the local skin changes and the swelling continues to increase like an inflated leather bag; fever, thirst, a burning sensation, and an aversion to food, etc., gradually develop.

The suppurated stage is marked by a lessening of the local pain and a yellowish color of the skin over the swelling, which cracks and appears too large, thus creating folds in the skin. The swelling fluctuates under pressure and shows perceptible signs of shrinking. Moreover, it yields to pressure and

<summary>This page details the three clinical stages of a swelling: the immature unsuppurated stage, the painful and active suppurating stage, and the final suppurated stage where pus has formed. It describes the specific physical sensations, skin changes, and systemic symptoms like fever and thirst that accompany this progression.</summary>

<keywords>Sutrasthanam, inflammation, suppuration, unsuppurated, suppurating, swelling symptoms, medical stages, integument</keywords>

reaches its former height when the pressure is removed. The pus or the suppurated matter changes its place or shifts from one part of the swelling to another under pressure, like water in a bloated leather bag. The distressing symptoms gradually subside; the patient again shows a desire for food and feels a constant inclination to scratch the affected part, which is characterized by a sort of aching pain. 

Sometimes, as in cases of traumatic swelling or in those brought about by a deranged condition of the <term>Kapham</term>, the suppurating process is restricted to the deeper tissues of the affected part and therefore fails to exhibit its characteristic symptoms—a fact which often misleads a physician (surgeon) regarding the true state <note>literally: whether suppurated or not</note> of the accompanying swelling. However, the knowledge that a process of suppuration occurring in the deeper tissues of an affected part is accompanied by an alleviation of the pain and a swelling that becomes as compact as stone and cold to the touch, while the local skin resumes its natural color, would unquestionably prevent any fear of an error in judgment.

# Chap. XVII. ] SUTRASTHÁNAM. 159

there can be no pain without the intervention of the deranged <term>Váyu</term>; and no suppuration can set in without the action of the deranged <term>Pittam</term>; nor can there be pus without the action of the deranged <term>Kapham</term>. It is evident that a suppurated swelling is marked by the combined and simultaneous action of the three deranged humours of the body.

According to certain authorities, the deranged <term>Pittam</term> gains the preponderance over the local <term>Váyu</term> and <term>Kapham</term>, and transforms the blood into pus through its own overwhelming energy.

The incision or opening of a swelling in its inflammatory or unsuppurated (*literally: immature, unripe*) stage is attended by the destruction of the local flesh, ligament, bone, vein, or joint, and is usually followed by excessive hemorrhage. The resulting wound becomes extremely painful. Many distressing symptoms begin to manifest themselves in succession, and cavities are formed inside the wound which may lapse into a case of *<term>Kshata-Vidradhi</term>* (a type of ulcerated abscess).

On the other hand, a fully suppurated swelling, left unopened for a long time out of fear or ignorance by the attending physician, is attended by symptoms which are fraught with dreadful consequences. The accumulated pus, unable to find an outlet, is infiltrated and attacks the deeper tissues of the affected part, and forms large cavities or sinuses in their inside, thus converting

<summary>This page explains that suppuration requires the combined action of Váyu, Pittam, and Kapham, and warns that opening a swelling too early or too late causes severe tissue damage and complications.</summary>

**Authoritative verses on the subject:** The physician (surgeon) who opens an unsuppurated or unripe swelling out of ignorance, as well as the man who neglects a fully suppurated one, should be regarded as the lowest <term>Chandála</term> for his wrong or incorrect diagnosis. The patient should be provided with a meal before the surgical operation, or strong wine should be given to him if he is known to be in the habit of taking it. The effect of a good meal under these circumstances will be to maintain the strength of the patient and to guard against fainting during the operation, while the effect of wine will be to make him unconscious of the pain. The rule regarding the feeding and anesthetizing (giving wine) of the patient should be strictly followed, since the internal vital principle of a man is invigorated by the strength of his body, which is the product of lymph-chyle—the essence of food and the quintessence of the five material principles. A swelling, no matter whether limited or extensive, spontaneously progresses toward suppuration if it is not treated medicinally or is left to nature. The base of such a swelling continues to extend. It becomes unevenly suppurated and reaches an irregular elevation, thus affecting the deeper tissues of the part and quickly turning into an incurable type. A swelling which does not respond to the application of medicated plasters

<meta>This page concludes the seventeenth chapter, continuing the discussion on the physical characteristics of suppurating swellings and the surgical treatments required for abscesses.</meta>

->### Chap. XVII. ] SUTRASTHANAM.<-

or to corrective or blood-letting measures, it speedily and uniformly suppurates. It is marked by a small and restricted base and a circular or conical elevation. Just as a blazing fire fed by gusts of favorable wind soon consumes a withered forest, so the trapped pus, in the absence of any outlet, attacks and eats away the healthy flesh, veins, and nerves of the body.

Surgical procedures in connection with an abscess (**Shotha**) may be divided into seven kinds, such as: 1. reduction (**Vimlápanam**) of the swelling by massage; 2. **Avashechanam** (bleeding or the application of leeches); 3. **Upanáham** (poulticing); 4. **Pátanam** (opening or incision); 5. **Shodhanam** (purification of the internal morbid matter of an incised boil with corrective medicines); 6. **Ropanam** (healing); and 7. **Vaikritápaham** (restoring the natural color of the skin to the scar).

Thus ends the seventeenth chapter of the Sutrasthanam in the Sushruta Samhita, which treats how to distinguish between suppurating and non-suppurating swellings.

## CHAPTER XVIII.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats the dressings and bandages of ulcers (**Vranælepana-Vandha-Vidhi-madhyayam**).

A medicinal plaster should be regarded as the general and most important remedy in all cases of inflammatory swelling. We shall presently discuss the nature of plasters to be used in each specific form of disease. A bandage plays a more important part than a medicinal plaster regarding its healing and curative efficacy, because it materially contributes to the purification and healing of an ulcer and keeps the joints steady. A medicinal plaster should be applied from the bottom upward or in a direction contrary to that of the local hair (**Pratiloma**). It should never be applied so as to run down with the local hair, since a plaster applied as directed above will firmly stick to the surface of the affected part and naturally seep through the follicles of the hair and the external openings of the sweat glands (sudoriferous ducts), thus permeating the organism with its own natural potency and virtue.

A medicinal plaster should be removed or replaced by a fresh one as soon as it has become dry, except in cases where the purpose of its application

<summary>This chapter introduces the importance of medicinal plasters and bandages in treating inflammatory swellings and ulcers. It emphasizes applying plasters against the grain of the hair to ensure the medicine properly permeates the skin's pores and sweat glands.</summary>
<keywords>ulcers, medicinal plaster, bandages, Vranælepana-Vandha-Vidhi-madhyayam, Pratiloma, sudoriferous ducts, inflammatory swelling, wound healing</keywords>

would be to draw the pus to a definite head (*Pidayitavya Vrana*).

-> . <-

A dried medicinal plaster will prove useless or ineffective, and may act as a caustic or corrosive agent. A medicinal plaster can be grouped under any of the three subcategories of **Pralepa, Pradeha**, and **Álepana** (according to its thickness or consistency), etc.

**Medicinal plasters:** —A medicinal plaster of the **Pralepa** class is applied thin and cold, and is made to possess an absorbing (*Vishoshi*) or non-absorbing (*Avishoshi*<note>As in the case of a Pidayitavya ulcer, described before, where the withdrawing or gathering of pus to a definite head is desired.</note>) property according to the nature of the desired effect.

On the other hand, a medicinal plaster of the **Pradeha** class is applied either thick or thin, warm or cold, and acts as a non-absorbent.

A medicinal plaster of the **Álepana** class stands midway between a Pralepa and a Pradeha.

Of these, a plaster of the **Pralepana** class is effective in calming or restoring the disturbed blood and Pittam to their normal condition. A plaster of the **Pradeha** class calms the disturbed Váyu and Kapham and tends to bring about the union, purification, and healing (of an ulcer), causing the

<summary>This page defines the three types of medicinal plasters—Pralepa, Pradeha, and Álepana—based on their consistency, temperature, and absorption. It explains how these applications are used to balance the bodily humors (Pittam, Váyu, and Kapham) and facilitate the healing of ulcers.</summary>

A medicinal plaster (**<term>Álepanam</term>**) applied over an ulcer is called by the alternative names **<term>Kalka</term>** or **<term>Niruddha-Álepanam</term>** (arrestive or astringent plaster). The function of such an **<term>Álepanam</term>** consists in stopping a local hemorrhage, softening the ulcer, removing sloughing or decaying flesh from its cavity, preventing the formation of pus inside it, and correcting the morbid matter or deranged humors (which delay its closing and healing).

**Metrical Texts :—** A medicinal plaster of the **<term>Álepanam</term>** class would be beneficial for a swelling characterized by the absence of suppuration, because it subdues the symptoms of each of the deranged bodily humors: namely, the burning sensation (peculiar to deranged **<term>Pittam</term>**), itching (incidental to the deranged state of **<term>Kapham</term>**), and the aching pain (which marks the disorder of the bodily **<term>Váyu</term>**). Its action consists mainly in cleansing the skin, the flesh, and the blood of all disease-causing conditions, removing the burning sensation, and alleviating piercing pain and itching.

A physician (surgeon) should use an **<term>Álepanam</term>** for diseases (related to ulcers) appearing around the anus or any other vital part (**<term>Marmas</term>**) of the body, with the goal of

In diseases caused by a deranged condition of the <term>Váyu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, or <term>Kapham</term>, medicinal plasters should be respectively mixed with a quantity of clarified butter measuring a sixth, a quarter, and an eighth part of their respective quantities.

It has been said that the thickness of an <term>Álepanam</term> should not be made to exceed that of the newly-skinned hide of a buffalo. Under no condition should a medicinal plaster be applied at night, because such a measure would prevent the escape or radiation of heat from the swelling due to the plaster's own inherent moisture, and thus cause the condition to worsen.

**Metrical Texts:** —In diseases that are suitable for the application of medicinal plasters of the <term>Pradeha</term> type, as well as in swellings resulting from the corrupted condition of the blood and the <term>Pittam</term>, or in those that are of external origin, or are due to the effect of a poison or a blow, the plaster should be applied cold during the day. A plaster should not be applied without removing the previous one, nor over one applied the day before, as this would increase the local heat and worsen the pain and burning sensation due to its increased thickness. A medicinal plaster that has been previously used should not be moistened and applied again; it should be held

### Articles for bandaging:
Now we shall list the names of the items required for bandaging ulcers. They are as follows:

<term>Kshauma</term> (cloth woven from the fibers of the <term>Atasi</term> plant), <term>Ávika</term> (blankets made of sheep's wool), <term>Dukula</term> (fine silk), <term>Kausheya</term> (silk), <term>Patrorna</term> (a type of cloth made from the fibers of <term>Nága</term> trees, which grow in the provinces of <term>Paundra</term> and <term>Magadha</term>), <term>Chinapatta</term> (Chinese cloth), <term>Antarvalkala</term> (the inner bark or fibers of a tree), <term>Charma</term> (skin or leather), <term>Alávu Shakala</term> (the skin of a gourd), <term>Lata-vidala</term> (shredded <term>Shyámá</term> creepers), string or cord, the cream of milk, <term>Tula-phalam</term> (cotton seeds) and iron. These materials should be used based on the requirements of each case and the time or season of the year.<note>For a swelling or ulcer caused by deranged <term>Vayu</term> and <term>Kapham</term>, the bandage should be a piece of thick cloth; however, in summer, it should consist of thin linen. # Chap. XVIII. | SUTRASTHANA M.

**Bandages:** — The fourteen different forms of bandage are named as the **Kosha** (a sheath or scabbard), the **Dáma** (a cord or chaplet), the **Svastika** (cross), the **Anuvellita** (a twist), the **Pratoli** (a winding street or road), the **Mandala** (ring), the **Sthagiká** (a betel-box), the **Yamaka** (double or twin), the **Khattá** (a bedstead), the **China** (a streamer), the **Vivandha** (noose), the **Vitána** (canopy), the **Gophaná** (cow-horn), and the **Panchángi** (five-limbed). Their shapes can be easily inferred from the meanings of their names.

**Applications:** — Of these, the **Kosha**, or the sheath-shaped bandage, should be tied around the thumb and the phalanges of the fingers; the **Dáma**, or chaplet-shaped bandage, around the narrow or unbent parts of the body; the **Svastiká**, or cross-shaped bandage, around the joints, around the articulations or the <term>Marmas</term> known as the <term>Kurchakas</term> (navicular ligaments), around the eyebrows, around the ears, and around the region of the breast. Similarly, the bandage known as the **Anuvellita** should be used when the site of the affection is situated at the extremities (hands and legs). A bandage of the **Pratoli** class should be tied around the neck or the penis; the **Mandalam** ring—

should be stopped by binding the part with milk-cream, while the affected part in a case of <term>Ardita</term> (facial paralysis) as well as a broken tooth should be bound with strings of iron, gold, or silver. Warts, etc., should be bandaged with <term>Ela</term> (cardamom skins), while dried gourd-skins should be used in bandaging ulcers on the head (scalp).

<summary>This page details fourteen types of surgical bandages named after their shapes and provides specific instructions on which bandage to use for different body parts and medical conditions.</summary>

In short, a bandage of any particular shape should be tied around the part of the body to which it would be found to be most suited. Now we shall deal with the <term>Yantranas</term> (fastenings of bandages) which can be divided into three different classes based on whether they are fastened above, below, or obliquely around an ulcer.

**Kavalikä (Tow) :—** Any soft stuffing or tow (such as the leaves or the bark of trees with medicinal virtues) between the medicine applied over an ulcer and the bandaging linen is called the <term>Kavaliká</term> (medicated tow). and unfolded or smooth linen over it, and then firmly tie the bandage in a manner so as not to leave any knot over the site of the ulcer, or cause any discomfort to the patient.

**Introduction of lint :** A <term>Visheshiká</term> (lint) saturated with honey, clarified butter, and a medicinal paste should be inserted into the ulcer. Care should be taken not to insert the lint when it is extremely dry or overly oily (over-soaked in a lubricating or oily medicinal preparation), because an over-lubricated lint would cause an excessive formation of slimy mucus in the ulcer, whereas its dry substitute would cause friction and the subsequent breaking of the edges of the ulcer, as if it were misplaced or wrongly inserted.

A bandage should be tied in any of the three ways—<term>Gádha</term>, <term>Sama</term>, and <term>Shithila</term> fastenings—according to the shape and location of the ulcer.* A tight bandage (<term>Gádha-Vandha</term>) should be tied around the buttocks, the sides, the armpits, the inguinal regions, the breast, or the head. * **Additional text :** — A bandage tightly tied around an ulcerated or affected part of the body without causing any pain or discomfort to the patient is called a <term>Gádha-Vandha</term>, while the one which is loosely bound is called <term>Shithila</term>, the one neither too tight nor too loose being called a <term>Sama-Vandha</term>.

> “You shall exercise your own discretion, and vary or adopt the preceding rules of bandaging according to the requirements of each case.”

A medicated lint fails to have any effectiveness but instead tends to increase the local pain and swelling when a bandage, which was intended to be loosely bound or bound with moderate and uniform steadiness (<term>Sama-Vandha</term>), is replaced by a tight or deeply fastened one (<term>Gádha-Vandha</term>). A loose bandage, unwisely used in a case where a tight or a moderately firm bandage should have been used, would cause the medicine to fall off the lint and lead to friction and laceration of the edges of the ulcer. Similarly, a moderately firm and steady bandage (<term>Sama-Vandha</term>) fastened in a case where a light or loose bandage should have been used would fail to produce any effect. A proper bandage leads to the reduction of pain and the softening of the edges of the ulcer, thus bringing about a purification of the local blood.

### Evils of non-bandaging :—
An ulcer, left uncovered and untied with a suitable bandage, is soon attacked by gnats and flies. It is moistened by sweat and cold wind, etc., and is at risk of being irritated by the deposit of many foreign materials.

**Metrical Texts:** A smashed, lacerated, fractured, dislocated, or displaced bone—or a vein or ligament similarly jeopardized—may be quickly healed or set right with the help of a surgical bandage. **Cases where bandaging is prohibited:** Ulcers should not be bandaged at all if they are due to the deranged condition of the blood or <term>Pittam</term>, or to the effects of a blow or any ingested poison. This includes ulcers characterized by a sucking, burning pain, redness, or suppuration, as well as those resulting from burns or the application of actual or potential cauteries marked by sloughing or a phagedenic character.

**Metrical Texts:** An ulcer caused by a scald on a person with leprosy, or a carbuncle in a diabetic patient (*Pidaká*), as well as fleshy condylomata caused by the bite of a venomous rat or any other poisonous ulcer, should not be bandaged at all. The same rule should be observed—

Bandages may be tied from above, below, or from the sides of a diseased area. Now I shall fully describe the process of bandaging an ulcer.* First, the <term>Kavaliká</term> (tow) should be thickly laid over the site of the ulcer, and after that, a piece of soft, smooth linen should be placed upon it, and the bandage should be tied loosely or tightly according to the directions previously established.*

The lint and the (inserted) medicine should not be over-lubricated and must not be excessively oily, inasmuch as such lint or medicine would lead to the formation of excessive and abnormal slimy mucus in the ulcer. On the other hand, extremely dry lint would cause friction and tearing of the edges of the ulcer, like one that is wrongly or improperly inserted.

### CHAPTER XIX.

Now we shall discuss the chapter that treats the care and nursing of a patient with an ulcer, etc. (**Vranito-pāsanīya-madhyāyam**).

First of all, a suitable room should be sought and selected for a patient suffering from an ulcer. It should be roomy and spacious, and located in a favorable site.

**Metrical Text:**—Diseases that are physical, mental, or traumatic in their origin can never attack a person who lives in a clean and spacious room, protected from excessive heat and strong gusts of wind.

The bed should be made clean, large, and comfortable, with the head of the bedstead turned toward the east, and provided with some kind of weapon.

**Metrical Texts:**—In a spacious and well-made bed, an ulcer patient can move around and stretch his limbs with the greatest comfort. The reason for the head being turned toward the east is so that the patient may easily pay respects to the (demons and) celestial spirits who inhabit that quarter of the sky. Thus, the patient shall lie in a comfortable posture, attended to by his pleasant-speaking friends and relatives.

<summary>This chapter describes the ideal recovery environment for a patient with an ulcer, emphasizing a clean, spacious, and protected room. It also details the proper arrangement of the bed, including its eastward orientation for spiritual reasons and the presence of supportive companions.</summary>
<keywords>ulcer care, nursing, Vranito-pāsanīya-madhyāyam, patient environment, celestial spirits, hygiene, wound recovery</keywords>

**Metrical Text:—**The friends and relatives of a patient should alleviate the pain of his ulcer with pleasant and interesting topics, and by reassuring him with the prospect of a speedy recovery. A patient with an ulcer should not sleep during the day, as it tends to aggravate the pain, swelling, and redness of the ulcer, increases its discharge, and causes itching and heaviness in the limbs.

The patient must carefully protect the ulcer when moving any of his limbs, such as when standing up, sitting down, turning on his sides, moving about, or speaking in a loud voice.

**Metrical Text:—**Even if a patient with an ulcer feels strong and capable, he should avoid standing or sitting for too long, as well as excessive walking and daytime sleep.<gloss>*</gloss> Doing these things to excess, or even a long confinement to bed, would aggravate the bodily <term>Váyu</term>, thereby causing pain in the ulcer.

He should studiously avoid the company, touch, and even conversation with women with whom he can legitimately have sexual intercourse.

Hence, he shall be kept in a chamber furnished with flowers, garlands, weapons, fried paddy, and lamps kept continuously burning. His friends and relations should entertain him with pleasant and loving topics to drive away the feeling of sleepiness with the prospect of a speedy cure.

## CHAPTER XX.

->Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats of<-
->the beneficial and harmful effects of regimen, etc.<-
->(<term>Hitāhitiya-madhyāyam</term>).<-

According to certain eminent medical authorities, an article or a substance that is beneficial in disorders of the bodily <term>Vāyu</term> may prove positively harmful in a <term>Pittaja</term> affection; therefore, it is impossible to name an article or substance that is absolutely or universally wholesome regardless of the nature and type of a disease, and of the deranged bodily humours involved in it.

But we cannot agree with the previous hypothesis, since by nature or combination, things (substances) are, or become endowed with properties which prove absolutely beneficial or unconditionally harmful, or exert a mixed influence (both beneficial and harmful) according to the difference in the nature and type of the disease in which they are used. Substances such as clarified butter, water, milk, and boiled rice, etc., may be described as absolutely beneficial owing to their compatibility with, or natural suitability for, the human organism.

Similarly, substances such as fire, alkali, and poison, may be designated as unconditionally harmful by virtue

<summary>This chapter introduces the debate on whether substances are universally beneficial or harmful, with the author arguing that items like milk and clarified butter are naturally wholesome for the human body.</summary>
<keywords>regimen, Vāyu, Pittaja, Hitāhitiya-madhyāyam, clarified butter, humours, wholesome substances, Ayurvedic medicine</keywords>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the inherent qualities of substances, specifically explaining how harmless items can become toxic through improper combinations and listing foods that are generally safe for everyone.</meta>

THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XX.

of their burning, suppurating <note>literally: boiling</note>, and fatal effect upon organic bodies in general. A substance which is harmless by nature may prove just as injurious as any active poison through an unwise or incompatible combination; whereas a substance or an article which proves beneficial in a derangement of the <term>Vayu</term> may prove otherwise in a disorder of the <term>Pittam</term>.

Articles or substances which may be safely included within the food stuffs of all human beings are the members of the group (*Varga*) known as the red *Sháli*, the *Shastika*, the *Kánguka*, the *Mukundaka*, the *Pánduka*, the *Pitaka*, the *Pramodaka*, the *Kálaka*, the *Ashanaka*, the *Pushpaka*, the *Karddamaka*, the *Shakunáhrita*, the *Sugandhaka*, the *Kalama*, the *Nivára*, the *Kodrava*, the *Uddálaka*, the *Shyámáka*, the *Godhuma*, and the *Venu*, etc., as well as the flesh of the *Ena*, the *Harina* <note>copper-colored deer</note>, the *Kuranga*, the *Mriga*, the *Mriga-mátriká*, the *Shvadanstrá*, the *Karála*, the *Krakara*, the *Kapota* <note>pigeon</note>, the *Láva*, the *Tittiri*, the *Kapinjala*, the *Varttira*, and the *Varttika*, and similar beasts and birds. The varieties of pulse which form the articles of human food are known as the *Mudga*, the *Vana-Mudga*, the *Makushtha*, the *Kaláya*, the *Masura*, the *Mangalya*, the *Chanaka*, the *Harenu*, the *Ádhaki*, and the *Satina*.
