Similarly, the practice of self-control, living in a room protected from strong gusts of wind, sleeping only at night, drinking tepid water, and moderate physical exercise should be regarded as absolutely conducive to the preservation of health.

We have already listed the names of substances which are absolutely beneficial or unconditionally harmful to human health. Things which are both wholesome and harmful are those which, for example, may prove beneficial in a disorder of the bodily <term>Váyu</term> <note>the air or nervous system humor</note> though harmful in a <term>Pittaja</term> <note>bile-related</note> condition. <header>THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XX.</header>

Since conditions vary infinitely in different types of diseases—and even the same conditions do not occur in every instance of the same type*—physicians generally prescribe a diet of their own selection, determined by its general effect on health, in preference to one that has been strictly laid down in books of medicine.

If asked to prescribe either milk or poison to a healthy person, a physician would naturally prescribe the former, and thereby prove the absolute wholesomeness of milk and the unconditional harmfulness of poison. Thus, Sushruta, the correctness of the maxim is verified: that things such as water, etc., are absolutely and unconditionally wholesome or otherwise, by virtue of their respective natural properties.

### **Incompatible preparations of food:**

Now we shall list the names of substances that become unwholesome through incompatible preparations. The flesh of a pigeon fried in mustard oil should not be eaten. The flesh of a <term>Kapinjala</term>, <term>Mayura</term> (peacock), <term>Láva</term>, <term>Tittira</term>, and <term>Godhá</term>, if boiled with castor oil and cooked over a fire of castor plant twigs, should not be eaten. Clarified butter that has been kept in a vessel of Indian bell metal for ten consecutive days should be rejected as unwholesome. Honey should not be used in combination with any article or substance heated by fire, nor should it be consumed in the seasons of spring and autumn. The leafy vegetables known as <term>Kákamáchi</term>, if boiled in a bowl in which fish or ginger had been previously boiled or prepared, should be rejected as positively harmful.

Similarly, the leafy vegetables known as <term>Upodiká</term> should not be eaten if they have been boiled with a ground paste of sesame. The flesh of a heron prepared with hog’s lard should not be consumed with the pulp of the coconut fruit. The flesh of a <term>Bhása</term> bird, roasted on a spit over a charcoal fire, should not be eaten.

**Objectionable proportions:** Now we shall list the names of substances that become unwholesome when mixed in harmful proportions. Two oily substances (such as oil and clarified butter), or honey and any of the oily substances, mixed in equal proportions, should not be consumed; nor should rain water be drunk immediately after taking honey and clarified butter.

**Incompatible tastes, potencies, and chemical actions:** Now we shall describe the substances listed in pairs that possess different tastes and prove incompatible with each other through their respective tastes, potencies, and chemical actions (<term>Vipāka</term>). Sweet and sour tastes, or sweet and salty tastes, should be considered incompatible with each other regarding their potencies and inherent properties. Sweet and acrid tastes are incompatible with each other in all three of the aforementioned respects.

Similarly, sweet and bitter, or sweet and astringent things should be considered incompatible with each other regarding their tastes and chemical action. Sour and salty things are incompatible with each other regarding their flavors. Sour and acrid things are incompatible regarding flavor and chemical action. Sour and bitter, or sour and astringent things, are incompatible with each other, both regarding their respective flavors, potencies, and digestive or chemical transformations.

Salty and pungent things are incompatible with each other regarding their respective flavor (<term>Rasa</term>) and digestive (chemical) transformation.

Pungent and bitter tastes are incompatible with each other regarding flavor and digestive transformation, whereas substances with pungent and astringent or bitter and astringent tastes are incompatible with one another regarding their respective potencies, flavors, and digestive (chemical) action or transformation.

**Degrees of incompatibility:** —Substances that are incompatible with, or antagonistic to, the system through a difference of degree or intensity, as well as things which bring about an extreme dryness of the body, or those which are extremely oily in their composition or are characterized by extreme cold or warmth, should be strictly rejected.

By consuming substances that are incompatible with each other regarding their tastes, potencies, and digestive transformations, a greedy and immoderate person becomes afflicted with disease and weakness of the sense organs, and ultimately meets their end.

Anything which, when taken, aggravates or agitates the bodily <term>humours</term> without causing the assimilated food (waste matter) to be evacuated from the bowels, or which possesses a taste contrary to or different from what is necessary for vitalization, should be regarded as the primary source of all bodily disorders.

Diseases brought about by food or drink composed of incompatible substances can be treated with purgatives, emetics, or pacifying (correcting the deranged <term>humours</term>) medicines; and such a diet, even when found unavoidable, should be preceded by the use of drugs or substances potent enough to neutralize its harmful effect.<note>This couplet also occurs in the Charaka Samhita.</note>

small quantities, as well as in people of youthful vigor and strong appetite, or in those who have become invigorated by the use of oily and protein-rich food and healthful physical exercise.<note>Different Reading: In a child or in a man of voracious appetite.</note>

### **The effects of the winds:**—Now we shall describe the effects of the winds on the body (as they blow from the different quarters of the heavens).

### **The East wind:**—The East wind, which is cool and sweet in its potency, is heavy and charged with salt; it aggravates the blood and <term>Pittam</term> and causes an acid digestive reaction. It especially aggravates the disease in a patient suffering from a wound or an ulcer, or from the effect of any poison, and affects people of <term>Shleshmála</term> temperament. It is highly beneficial to fatigued people, as well as to those of a <term>Vátalá</term> (nervous) temperament, or who are afflicted with any sort of <term>Kaphaja</term> disease; though it increases the slimy secretion in their ulcers if there are any.

### **The South wind:**—The South wind is light, sweet (producing the same soothing effect on the organism as something with a sweet taste) and is followed by an astringent after-taste (<term>Anurasa</term>), being antacid in its reaction. It is the best of winds; it gives vigor to the eyes, increases strength, and soothes the blood and the <term>Pittam</term> without aggravating the bodily <term>Váyu</term>.

<summary>This page discusses how the East wind aggravates blood, Pittam, and certain diseases, while the South wind is described as the most beneficial wind for increasing strength and soothing the humours.</summary>

**The West wind:—** The West wind is pure, non-slimy, dry, rough to the senses, and sharp. It absorbs the albumin or oily principles of the body. It dries up fat and <term>Kapham</term>, creates a parched condition in the body when exposed to it, and quickly diminishes a person's strength.

**The North wind:—** The North wind is cold, crisp, and mild, with a sweet taste ending in an astringent one. It does not in any way aggravate or agitate the disordered bodily humours. In healthy subjects, it increases strength and the flowing secretions from the various orifices of the body (such as the nostrils, etc.). It proves extremely beneficial to patients suffering from <term>consumption</term>, <term>cachexia</term>, and the effects of poison.

### CHAPTER XXI.

Now we shall discuss the chapter that investigates the nature of bodily humours as the causes of ulcers (**Vrana-prashna-madhyayam**).

The <term>Váyu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, and <term>Shleshmá</term> should be considered the primary and most essential factors in the constitution of the human organism. These fundamental and vital humours, occupying the lower, middle, and upper parts of the body respectively, maintain its integrity. The human body is supported by the three fundamental humours in the same way a dwelling house is propped up by three supporting poles or stays; for this reason, the body is called the "three-supported one" (**Tristhunam**) by certain authorities. A deranged condition of these three fundamental humours may cause its dissolution or death, while the vitality of the organism depends on them remaining in a normal state. These three humours, in combination with a fourth—the principle of blood—determine the origin, preservation, and dissolution of the living organism and permeate it with their respective properties until the moment of death.

**Authoritative verse on the subject:**—There can be no organism without <term>Váyu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, <term>Kapham</term>, and blood, which are necessary to

<vocab>Váyu, Pittam, Shleshmá, Vrana-prashna-madhyayam, Tristhunam, Kapham, bodily humours, ulcers</vocab>

<summary>This chapter introduces the three fundamental humours—Váyu, Pittam, and Shleshmá—along with blood, as the essential pillars that support the human body's life and integrity. It explains that the balance of these humours determines health, while their derangement leads to disease or death.</summary>

<keywords>Váyu, Pittam, Shleshmá, Tristhunam, bodily humours, blood, human organism, ulcers, Sushruta Samhita</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the etymology of the three humours and details their specific anatomical locations within the body, following the introduction of their fundamental role in the human organism.</meta>

Chap. XXI.] SUTRASTHÁNAM.

constantly maintain its integrity. The terms <term>Váta</term> (<term>Váyu</term>), <term>Pittam</term>, and <term>Shleshmá</term> (<term>Kapham</term>) are respectively derived from the roots '*Vá*' (to move or smell), '*Tapa*' (to burn or to heat), and '*Shlisha*' (to embrace), with the suffix '*Ta*' added to them.<note>From this, it should be inferred that motion and smell are the natural attributes of the vital <term>Váyu</term>; heat and burning are those of <term>Pittam</term>; and union and integration are those of <term>Kapham</term>.</note>

## Seats of the bodily humours:—
Now we shall describe the locations of the aforementioned vital humours. The <term>Váyu</term> may be briefly described as being located in the regions of the pelvis (<term>Shroni</term>) and the rectum (<term>Guda</term>). The <term>Pittam</term> has its seat in the region between the stomach (<term>Ámáshaya</term>) and the intestines (<term>Pakváshaya</term>)—which is above the pelvis and the rectum and below the navel—while the <term>Kapham</term> is situated within the cavity of the stomach (<term>Ámáshaya</term>).

Now we shall divide the locations of each of the vital humours into five parts:— The five locations of the <term>Váyu</term> will be described under the heading of <term>Váta-Vyádhis</term> (nervous diseases), while those of <term>Pittam</term> are the liver and the spleen, the heart, the pupils of the eyes, the skin, and the intestines (<term>Pakváshaya</term>). The <term>Kapham</term> is located in the regions of the chest, the throat, the head, the joints, and the stomach (<term>Ámáshaya</term>). These regions are the seats of the vital humours in their normal state.

THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITÁ. [Chap. XXI.

**Metrical Texts:—**The vital humours (<term>Váyu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, and <term>Kapham</term>) maintain the integrity of the living organism by creating, assimilating, and diffusing strength in the same way as the moon, the sun, and the winds maintain the integrity of the terrestrial globe.*

**The Pittam:—**Now it may be asked whether the <term>Pittam</term> is identical to the elemental fire, or if it is something other than that? The question may be answered by stating that the <term>Pittam</term> is the same as fire. Since symptoms such as a burning sensation, digestion (boiling), and all other characteristics of fire can never manifest themselves in the human body without the intervention of <term>Pittam</term>, <term>Pittam</term> is therefore called internal fire.†

Consequently, a weakened action of <term>Pittam</term> is remedied by the administration of drugs and substances which are similar to the elemental fire in their attributes, while an abnormal or excessive action (secretion) of <term>Pittam</term> is subdued by cooling measures, just as an over-kindled fire is subdued by moisture. There is no other fire (heat-making factor) in the organism than <term>Pittam</term>.

---

<note>* The moon bathes the earth and imparts to it the vitalizing principle with her own ambrosial light. The sun draws off the moisture by virtue of his own attractive force, and the <term>Váyu</term> distributes the heat and moisture over its surface.</note>

<note>† The analogy is based on the heating (and metabolic) actions of <term>Pittam</term> and does not extend to its liquid secretion (bile). But since the former attributes permeate it in its entirety, it is designated the **Internal fire.**</note>

Chap. XXI. ] SUTRASTHANAM.

**The Pachakagni:** — By the decree of fate or necessity (an unfathomable natural cause), the <term>Pittam</term>, located in the region between the stomach (<term>Amashaya</term>) and the intestines (<term>Pakvashaya</term>), helps the digestion of the four kinds of food (such as drinks and edibles, etc.) consumed by a living subject. It also clears away the residue or impure, disease-causing matter in the form of urine and excrement after the process is complete. Even while located there, it maintains the temperature in its other distant locations (the skin, etc.) by virtue of its inherent heat-giving quality. Therefore, this <term>Pittam</term> is called the **Pachakagni** (digestive fire or heat) in a living organism.

**The Ranjakagni:** — The function of the <term>Pittam</term> which has its seats in the liver and the spleen consists of imparting its characteristic pigment (<term>Ragakrit</term>) to the lymph-chyle; it is therefore known as **Ranjakagni** (literally: dyeing fire or pigment bile).

**The Sadhakagni:** — The <term>Pittam</term> located in the heart is named the **Sadhakagni** (performing or operating heat or fire) because its action is to bring about the fulfillment or realization of one's desires.

**The Alochakagni:** — The <term>Pittam</term> which is located in the pupils of the eyes is called the **Alochakagni** (the <term>Pittam</term> or fire of sight), as its function is to

**The Bhrájakágni:** — The <term>Pittam</term> which has its seat in the skin is called the **Bhrájakágni** (illuminating or irradiating heat), because it absorbs the substances used in the form of ointments, lubricants, etc., and irradiates the glow of one's natural complexion.

**Metrical texts:** — The <term>Pittam</term> is a keen, sharp, and warm liquid. It is blue in its normal state, or yellowish when in a deranged condition. It emits a kind of fleshy smell and possesses a pungent taste, which is transformed into an acid one when it is imbalanced or corrupted.

**Seats of Shleshma (<term>Kapham</term>):** — Now we shall describe the locations of <term>Kapham</term>. The stomach (<term>Amashaya</term>), which is the seat of <term>Kapham</term>, holds the same position in relation to <term>Pittam</term> as the moon holds in relation to the sun. Since the stomach (<term>Amashaya</term>) is situated above the gallbladder or pancreas (<term>Pittashaya</term>), and is endowed with a cooling property contrary to the primary heating virtue of <term>Pittam</term>, and since the heat emitted by the receptacle of <term>Pittam</term> naturally radiates in an upward direction, the four kinds of food brought into the stomach (<term>Amashaya</term>) are cooked and transformed into a soft, placid mass (<term>chyme</term>), like rice boiled in a bowl full of water placed over a

## Humours and their aggravations :—

Now we shall list the causes which disturb and aggravate the deranged humours. The bodily <term>Váyu</term> is aggravated by factors (such as conduct, practices, and diet) like wrestling with an opponent of superior strength, violent gymnastic exercises, sexual excess, excessive study, a headlong plunge into water or a leap from an extreme height, running, a violent blow, leaping over a ditch, a bounding gait, swimming, staying up late, carrying heavy loads, excessive riding, walking a long distance, and eating food composed of pungent, astringent, bitter, light, or drying articles, or substances of cool potency. Diets consisting of dried potherbs, <term>Vallura</term>, <term>Varaka</term>, <term>Uddálaka</term>, <term>Karadusha</term>, <term>Shyámáka</term>, <term>Nivára</term>, <term>Mudga</term>, <term>Masura</term>, <term>Ádháki</term>, <term>Harenu</term>, <term>Kaláya</term>, and <term>Nishpâva</term> tend to aggravate the bodily <term>Váyu</term>.

**Metrical Text :**—The bodily <term>Váyu</term> is naturally aggravated on a cold, cloudy, or windy day; in winter; during the rainy season; in the morning and evening; and especially at the completion of digestion.

**Symptoms of aggravated Pittam :**—The <term>Pittam</term> is aggravated by anger, grief, fear, fatigue, fasting, the acid transformation (reaction) of digested food, deficient gastric digestion, unnatural sexual indulgence, and eating food consisting of pungent, acid, or salty, sharp, heating, or light substances, as well as those whose digestion is followed by reactionary acidity. It is aggravated by the use of sesame oil or sesame paste. <term>Kulattha</term>, <term>Sarshapa</term>, <term>Átashi</term>, the pot-herbs known as <term>Haritaka</term>, fish, and the flesh of a <term>Godhá</term>, a goat, or a sheep (mutton) may lead to its aggravation if consumed unwisely.

Similarly, the use of curd, whey, <term>Kurchiká</term> (thickened milk), <term>Sauviraka</term>, different kinds of wine, <term>Amla-phala</term> (sour fruits), or <term>Katvara</term> (curd mixed with oil), and excessive exposure to the sun may be followed by the same consequences.

### **Expansion of the deranged humors :**

Now we shall describe the expansion (<term>Prasaram</term>) of the deranged humors. The deranged humors, aggravated by the causes mentioned above, expand and overflow the limits of their respective locations in the same manner as cakes, soaked in any ferment or enzyme and left standing overnight, ferment and rise through the acquisition of new and unseen attributes. The <term>Váyu</term>, which possesses locomotion or extreme mobility, should be looked upon as the cause of their expansion or overflowing. The <term>Váyu</term>, though an inanimate thing, in reality possesses the quality of "**Rajas**" (creative or cohesive energy), and the quality of the Rajas is the only essential or motive principle in the universe.

As a vast and mighty expanse of water, which has been divided into two expanses by a dam or barrier, will sweep away the latter and unite again to form one sheet of water; so the deranged humors, sometimes singly, sometimes in combination with two or all of their species, or in unison with blood, expand and overrun the organism in all directions. As for example, the <term>Váyu</term>, the <term>Pittam</term>, the <term>Kapham</term> and the blood are singly expanded, whereas the bi-hu-

**Metrical Texts:-** The aggravated, or the abnormally irritated deranged humors, whether permeating the whole or half of the system or restricted to any particular part or member of the body, give rise to disease in the place of their incarceration, like rain clouds pouring down in the quarter of the sky where they are formed. The deranged humors, when not excessively (only slightly) aggravated, lie inoperative, coating the internal passages (<term>Márgas</term>) of the body, and thus bring about a fresh disease if subsequently agitated by any disturbing causes.

The aggravated and expanded humors, thus firmly settled in the different parts of the body, show the premonitory symptoms of diseases which will be fully dealt with under their respective headings. The appearance of these premonitory symptoms should be considered the fourth opportunity for medical treatment.

### **Disease—Its Development:—**

Now we shall deal with the full development or manifestation of a disease. The full manifestation of a disease, such as a swelling, tumor, aneurysm (<term>Granthi</term>), <term>Vidradhi</term> (abscess), erysipelas (<term>Visarpa</term>), fever, or dysentery, signifies the complete development of the characteristic symptoms; this should be regarded as the fifth opportunity for medical treatment.

The sixth opportunity for medical aid should be considered to have arisen when a swelling (abscess, tumor, etc.) bursts and exhibits the characteristic symptoms of an open ulcer. A persistent lingering or continuation of a fever or dysentery, etc., should be considered as marking one of its particular stages, which may turn into an incurable type if neglected or not sufficiently cared for at the start.

The physician, who fully knows about the accumula-

The deranged humors, if checked or subdued in their accumulation stage, fail to show any further or subsequent development; however, if left untreated, they gain strength and intensity as they continue to develop. The humors, deranged either individually, in pairs, or in a triple combination regarding one or two of their properties, move forward, follow, and blend with humors that are similarly deranged in terms of their qualities and number of combinations.

Medical treatment in a case where two or all three of the deranged humors are involved consists of overcoming the strongest one in the combination, but in such a way as not to provoke or aggravate the minor or weaker humors in the group—especially in a case of <term>Sannipáta</term>.*

<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>Chap. XXI. ] SUTRASTHANAM.</header>

A gathering of imbalanced humors, affecting and appearing in a specific part of the body, is called a boil or an ulcer (<term>Vrana</term>). This term is derived from the root “<term>Vri</term>,” meaning “to cover,” and it is named so because it covers a specific part of the body, or because it leaves a scar (<term>cicatrix</term>) that remains for the patient's entire lifetime.

->rise to an ulcer.<-

# CHAPTER XXII.

->Now we shall discuss the chapter which<-
->treats the secretions from boils or ulcers of<-
->different types. (**Vranasrāva-Vijnāniaya-**<-
->**madhyāyam**).<-

A boil or an ulcer generally has its seat in one of the eight following components or principles of the body: the bone, the skin, the flesh, the veins, the ligaments, the joints, the viscera, and the <term>Marmas</term> (vital parts of the body). A boil or an ulcer of any type may crop up or appear in any one of the above-mentioned locations.

A boil or an ulcer that is confined only to the skin readily yields to medical treatment, while the remaining types, as well as those that spontaneously suppurate and burst, are hard to cure. A boil or an ulcer usually assumes a shape that is either diffused, rectangular, spheroidal, or triangular; while those that are irregular or indefinite in shape (or have forms other than the preceding ones) should be looked upon as belonging to types that can be cured only with the utmost difficulty. Any <term>Vrana</term> (burst or incised abscess) in a patient who observes a strict regimen, and who, from the outset, is placed under the medical treatment of an experienced physician (surgeon), will be speedily healed; while an ulcer affecting a person of

<summary>This page introduces Chapter XXII, detailing the eight anatomical locations where boils and ulcers occur and describing their various shapes. It explains that skin-deep ulcers are easier to treat than those affecting deeper tissues or those that burst spontaneously.</summary>
<keywords>Vranasrāva-Vijnāniaya-madhyāyam, ulcers, boils, Marmas, anatomy, Vrana, surgery, prognosis</keywords>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ulcers from page 212, focusing on the progression into malignant types (Dushta-Vranas) and their specific physical symptoms and humoured-based classification.</meta>

...irregular habits and treated by a quack or an ignorant physician, will develop into a malignant type. This can be healed only with the greatest difficulty because it is aggravated by the deranged bodily humours involved.

**Symptoms of Dushta-Vranas:**—Malignant ulcers (Dushta Vranas) are recognized by the following signs:—They are either too narrow or too wide-mouthed. They feel either extremely hard or soft to the touch and appear either raised and elevated, or depressed. They are black, red, yellow, or white in color, and are characterized by extremes of temperature. Exhibiting strange and unusual features, they are crisscrossed with networks of veins, ligaments, and other structures, and are filled with putrid, sloughing flesh and foul-smelling pus. Indefinite and irregular in shape, they exude a sort of dirty, foul-smelling pus, which runs into fissures and cavities, following an oblique or upward course. They have a corpse-like look and smell and are characterized by extreme pain and a burning sensation, accompanied by swelling, redness, itching, and suppuration. Pustules crop up around these ulcers, which secrete a large amount of tainted blood and remain unhealed for an exceptionally long time.

These ulcers may be divided into six classes <note>according to whether they are each caused by the deranged bodily humours (<term>Vayu</term>, <term>Pittam</term>, and <term>Kapham</term>), or are</note>

It begins to classify these ulcers based on the imbalance of the three bodily humours: Vayu, Pittam, and Kapham.</summary>

**Secretions from ulcers:**

Now we shall describe the characteristic secretions from all types of ulcers. Secretions from bruised or torn skin, as well as from an ulcer confined only to the skin—whether bursting naturally or opened surgically—are thin and watery in their consistency. They are characterized by a raw (fleshy) smell and a yellowish color. An ulcer affecting the flesh exudes a slimy, thick, and white secretion like clarified butter. A large amount of blood flows out of a recently cut vein, while a secondary ulcer, in its stage of suppuration, discharges an abundant secretion like water flowing out of a pipe; this discharge is also detached, thin, stringy (ropy), and slimy in character, with a brown or frosted hue. An ulcer confined only to a ligament secretes a sort of cold and thick discharge, like coughed-up mucus, though sometimes marked with streaks of blood.

A bone that is injured, fractured, or suddenly cracked by internal causes (imbalance of the bodily humours) loses its internal marrow and appears as if washed (losing its natural shine). It takes on the color of an oyster shell, while the secretions from an ulcer located in

An ulcer located in any of the bone joints does not release any discharge under pressure, but it secretes a slimy, hanging, frothy, and blood-streaked pus when the affected limb or part is moved—such as when flexed, extended, raised, or lowered during activities like running (moving about), sitting, standing upright, or during defecation.

An ulcer located in the abdominal cavity (<term>Koshtha</term>) releases a discharge mixed with urine, fecal matter, pus, blood, and a thin or watery (serous) fluid. The secretions from an ulcer affecting any vital part of the body do not need to be described separately, as such a part naturally consists of the organic components like skin, flesh, and so on. Therefore, an ulcer invading a vital part will necessarily release a discharge characteristic of whichever of these bodily components (skin, flesh, etc.) has been affected.

Deranged <term>Vāyu</term> causes the secretions from an ulcer located in any of the seven aforementioned components—skin, flesh, veins, ligaments, bones, joints, and the abdomen—to be respectively coarse and rough to the touch, brown, grey, frosty, or white like the cream of curd; it may also be colored like alkaline wash water, or like the washings of meat or rice husks. They have a lard-like hue, a color like that of rice paste, a color like that of water tinged with sesame, a color like that of the internal juice or water of a coconut, or a color like that of hog's lard, according to whether the flesh, a vein, a ligament, a bone, or a joint is attacked. On the other hand, through the combined action of all three deranged humors of the body (<term>Sannipáta</term>), those secretions become colored like the water tinged with the soaking of sesame seeds, the internal sap or water of a coconut, the juice of the <term>Erváruka</term>, the transparent surface layer of rice gruel, the washings of the <term>Aruka</term> fruit, water tinged with the fruits of the <term>Priyángu</term>, or like the liver or the <term>Mudga</term> pulse.

### **Authoritative verses on the subject :**

—An ulcer located in the abdominal cavity that produces a discharge resembling the color of rice husks, as well as one located in the blood-viscera (the spleen or liver—<term>Raktáshayam</term>) that produces a discharge like alkaline water, should be considered incurable. Similarly, an ulcer located in the stomach cavity (<term>Amáshaya</term>), or in the <term>Trika</term> region (the articulation of the collarbone with the notch above the breastbone) that produces a thin, watery discharge colored like the washings of <term>Kaláya</term> pulse, should be considered to be of the same type (incurable). A physician should only undertake the treatment of a patient with an ulcer after having examined the nature of the discharges mentioned above.

### **Pain and its character :**

Now we shall describe all the different kinds of pain that are experienced in the several types of <term>Vrana</term> (ulcers) described previously.

### **Vataja pain :**

—Pains that feel like pricking, piercing, thrashing, cutting, expanding, gnawing, churning, shooting, tingling, burning, breaking, bursting, pinching, uprooting, uplifting, quivering, various types of aching, shifting, stuffing, numbing, hardening, contracting, and pains of a spasmodic nature are usually felt in ulcers. A pain that appears or disappears without any apparent cause, or is varied and shifting

**Pittaja pain :—** A sensation of burning is felt in the ulcer, accompanied by a sort of sucking pain. A feeling of inhaling heat or vapor, and a burning sensation running through the whole body, should be regarded as the result of the deranged <term>Pittam</term>. At the same time, the body feels as if it had been strewn with bits of glowing charcoal. The heat (or the temperature of the affected area) shows a steady rise, and a pain like that caused by the application of alkaline water (a caustic solution) is experienced in the ulcer.

**Raktaja pain :—** The pain and other specific features of an ulcer due to the vitiated condition of the blood are identical to those developed by an ulcer of the <term>Pittaja</term> type.

**Kaphaja pain :—** An ulcer characterized by numbness, heaviness, coldness, itching, and a slight pain in the affected part—which feels as if it has been plastered over with a paste and is insensitive to touch—should be attributed to the action of the deranged <term>Kapham</term>.

**Colors of Vranas:**—Now we shall describe the colors assumed by the various types of ulcers. An ulcer due to the action of the deranged <term>Váyu</term> is rough and black, red, or ash-colored, or is the color of a bone or a pigeon. An ulcer caused by the action of the deranged blood or <term>Pittam</term> is colored blue, yellow, greenish-brown, black, reddish-tawny, or flame-colored. An ulcer due to the action of the deranged <term>Kapham</term> is white, gray, and glossy. An ulcer due to the combined action of the three deranged bodily humours may assume any color peculiar to them.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:**—Not only in cases of <term>Vrana</term>, but in all inflammatory swellings of whatever type, the physician should carefully observe the nature of the local pain and the color of the epidermis.

### CHAPTER XXIII.

Now we shall discuss the chapter that deals with the Prognosis of an ulcer (**<term>Kritẏakritẏa-Vidhi-madhẏaẏam</term>**).

A boil or an ulcer appearing in a patient who is young, muscular (in frame), strong, or who possesses indomitable courage and fortitude, proves readily responsive to healing measures and applications; how much more so when one appears in a patient in whom all four of these conditions exist simultaneously.

An ulcer in a young patient is healed quickly because of the fresh and vigorous vitalizing principles of the body; whereas one that appears in a person of strong and muscular build reaches a quick and successful conclusion because of the inability of the surgical instrument to cut deep into the hard and tough muscles of the affected part and to reach down to, or in any way destroy, the underlying veins and nerves, etc. A strong and vigorous patient can easily endure a considerable amount of burning pain, etc., and does not feel distressed by a strict dietary regimen. A person of great endurance and fortitude can sustain the fatigue and anxiety of even the most painful surgical operation. Accordingly, a boil or an ulcer appearing in a patient of the description mentioned above is easily and quickly healed;

<summary>This chapter explains the prognosis of ulcers, noting that patients who are young, strong, muscular, and courageous are more likely to heal quickly and endure surgical treatments. It describes how physical vitality and mental fortitude contribute to successful recovery and the protection of underlying nerves and veins.</summary>
<keywords>ulcer, Prognosis, Kritẏakritẏa-Vidhi-madhẏaẏam, healing, surgical operation, patient constitution, fortitude, muscularity</keywords>

--- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the prognosis of ulcers from the previous page, contrasting the healing process of healthy patients with those who are elderly or weak, and categorizing ulcers by their anatomical location.</meta>

->Chap. XXIII. ] SUTRASTHANAM.<-

In contrast, an ulcer which affects an old, emaciated, or timid person, or one of little strength and endurance, takes a long time to heal.

Boils or ulcers that appear in the regions of the buttocks (**<term>Sphik</term>**), or around the anus, the genital organs, the back, forehead, cheek, or lips, or in the region of the external ears, or on the testes, the abdomen, the cavity of the mouth, the nape of the neck, or above the collarbones, are easily healed. Those that are located in the eyes, the gums, the nostrils, the outer corner of the eye, the cavity of the ears, the abdomen, the navel, or near any suture of the body, the hips, ribs, armpits, chest, breasts, sides, or the joints, are healed only with the greatest difficulty. This also applies to those that discharge frothy blood or pus with a gurgling sound, or contain any foreign matter embedded inside them.

Similarly, an abscess or an ulcer appearing in the lower region of the body and pointing upward, or one appearing at the edge of the scalp (**<term>Románta</term>**), or near the end of a fingernail, or in any of the vulnerable parts of the body, as well as one affecting either of the thigh bones (femurs), should be regarded as equally hard to cure. Likewise, an abscess or an ulcer affecting a bone of the pelvis (**<term>Shronikanda-Acetabulum</term>**), as well as a fistula

**Authoritative verse on the subject:** An ulcer (<term>Vrana</term>) appearing in a person with leprosy (<term>Kushthi</term>), or in a person suffering from diabetes (<term>Madhu-meha</term>), or from <term>Shosha</term> (literally: pulmonary consumption), or from the effects of poison, as well as one appearing within a pre-existing ulcer, should be looked upon as curable only with the greatest difficulty.

**Palliative (<term>Yāpya</term>) ulcers:** An ulcer occurring as a result of, and affecting the site of, any of the following diseases—namely, <term>Avapāthikā</term> (paraphimosis), <term>Niruddha-Prakāsh</term> (phimosis), <term>Sanniruddha-guda</term> (constriction of the anus), <term>Jathara</term> (abdominal dropsy), or <term>Granthi</term> (glandular inflammation)—and characterized by the growth of parasites inside it, as well as an ulcer appearing in the abdominal cavity, or affecting the mucous linings of the intestines, or caused by the corrosive secretions of a nasal catarrh (<term>Pratishyāya</term>), and infested with parasites, should be considered as only allowing for palliative treatment. Similarly, palliation is the only remedy for an ulcer that appears in a patient suffering from any morbid secretion from the urethra (<term>Prameha</term>) or from any form of skin disease, if the ulcer contains worms inside it.

**Metrical texts :--** In a patient neglecting a disease at its preliminary stage, (or otherwise not observing a strict regimen) even a curable malady may speedily develop into one which admits only of palliative measures, while a disease of the last named type is soon transformed into an incurable one. An incurable disease under these circumstances speedily finds a fatal termination. A patient laid up with a disease, which only admits of a palliative treatment, lives so long as the course of the medical treatment is continued, and will die almost simultaneously with its discontinuance. Just as a prop or a pillar can prevent the collapse of a tumbling edifice, so palliative measures, judiciously applied by a skillful physician, may keep off the inevitable in a disease which knows no radical cure.

**Authoritative verses on the subject:** A traumatic ulcer, which exudes a secretion of fat, marrow, or brain matter, may be responsive to medical treatment, whereas a humoral ulcer under the same circumstances will prove incurable.

An ulcer appearing at any part of the body other than a vital one (<term>Marma</term>), and which is found to invade its successive elements even without affecting any vein, bone, joint, etc., should be regarded as incurable.

<page-num>226</page-num>

Just as an old tree that has spread its roots deep into the soil cannot be uprooted, a disease that has gained strength and maturity over time and has gradually invaded the different essential principles of the body cannot be eradicated. A disease that was neglected at the start and has become a lingering or persistent type by invading the successive elements of the body—thereby gaining strength and intensity—baffles medicines (even those of tested and marked efficacy), just as malignant astral combinations tend to nullify potent incantations.

## Symptoms of cleansed healthy ulcers:
An ulcer that does not belong to any of the types mentioned above may prove easily responsive to the curative effects of medicines. In other words, an ulcer of recent origin is easily uprooted like a tender, young sapling. An ulcer that is unaffected by any of the three deranged bodily humours, assumes a dark brown hue along its edges, and is characterized by the absence of any pain, pustular eruptions, or secretions, and which is of an even or equal elevation throughout its length, should be regarded as cleansed (aseptic or healthy) and divested of all morbid matter or principles (**<term>Shuddha-Vrana</term>**).

## Symptoms of Healing Ulcers:
An ulcer that is dove-colored (yellowish dusky) and is

**Symptoms of Healed Ulcers:** An ulcer with its edges firmly adhered and characterized by the absence of any pain and swelling, and not appearing knotty or glandular to the touch, and that has left a scar (<term>cicatrix</term>) of the same color as the surrounding skin, should be considered as perfectly healed.

Causes such as emotional excitement—including excessive grief and outbursts of joy, anger, or fear—as well as an external blow, excessive physical exercise, an abnormal excitation of any of the disturbed <term>humours</term>, or impaired digestion, may tend to reopen an ulcer that has recently closed and healed. Accordingly, such acts and conditions should be avoided by an ulcer patient.

## CHAPTER XXIV. *

Now we shall discuss the chapter which deals with the classification of diseases according to their specific nature (**Vyādhi-Samuddheshiya-madhyāyam**).

Diseases may be grouped under two broad subdivisions: surgical and medical—the latter being those that respond to the administration of purgatives, emetics, oils, <term>diaphoretics</term>, and ointments.

The use or administration of medicated oils and ointments, and so on, is not prohibited in a surgical disease, while a case which is exclusively medical in its character does not allow for the adoption of any surgical remedy. Only a general outline of the nature and symptoms of all diseases has been briefly laid down in the present work. This work includes within its scope subject matter which has been fully dealt with in other books (which have only a general bearing upon all the several branches of the science of medicine).

It has been stated before that anything that afflicts either the body or the living personality (the self), or both, is called a disease. This pain or affliction

It may be either physical in its character (<term>Ádhyátmikam</term>), or due to any disturbance in the physical environment of a person (<term>Ádhibhautikam</term>), or to the acts of God (<term>Ádhidaivikam</term>), and so on. This three-fold pain may be ultimately transformed into any of the seven kinds of diseases, such as: <term>Ádi-vala-pravritta</term>, <term>Janma-vala-pravritta</term>, <term>Dosha-vala-pravritta</term>, <term>Sangháta-vala-pravritta</term>, <term>Kála-vala-pravritta</term>, <term>Daiva-vala-pravritta</term>, and <term>Svabháva-vala-pravritta</term>.

**Ádi-vala-pravritta:** The disease termed <term>Ádi-vala-pravritta</term> is attributed to any inherent defect in the semen or the ovum of one’s parent, which forms one of the original and primary factors of “being.” This includes leprosy (<term>Kushtham</term>), hemorrhoids, phthisis, and so on. This type may be divided into two subdivisions, depending on whether the disease is generated by the deranged paternal or maternal factor at the time of conception.

**Janma-vala-pravritta:** The congenital or <term>Janma-vala-pravritta</term> type usually follows such causes as improper conduct on the part of the mother during the period of gestation, and so on. It encompasses such defects or maladies as (congenital) blindness, deafness, muteness, a nasal voice, and such monstrous aberrations of nature as congenital cretinism,

**Kāla-vala-pravritta:**—The Periodical type (<term>Kāla-vala-pravritta</term>) includes diseases that are brought about by variations in atmospheric heat or humidity with the change of the seasons. This type can be grouped under two different sub-headings, depending on whether the seasons that introduce these changes exhibit natural or abnormal features.

**Daiva-vala-pravritta:**—The Providential (<term>Daiva-vala-pravritta</term>) type includes diseases that are the manifestation of curses, divine wrath, or displeasure, or are brought about through the mystic power of charms and spells, as described in the Atharva-Veda. This type may be divided into two minor divisions: those due to acts of God, such as when a person is struck by lightning, etc., or those due to the malignant influences of demons and monsters. These may be further grouped under two main sub-headings, depending on whether the disease takes on a contagious character (epidemic) or is purely accidental and restricted to isolated cases (sporadic).

They are called **Kálakrita** when they occur at the proper time in persons who strictly observe the rules of health, and **Akálakrita**, when they appear at the improper time (morbid or premature) as the effects of unhealthy living. These diseases belong to the Providential or **Ádhi-daivikam** type†. Thus we have classified diseases into their several types.

The deranged bodily humours such as **Váyu**, **Pittam**, and **Kapham** should be looked upon as the primary sources of all diseases, since symptoms characteristic of each of them may be detected in the case of a disease of whatsoever type (which usually abates as they correspondingly subside), and also because the **Shástras** have ascribed to them the origin of all maladies that assail the human frame.

As the three qualities of **Sattva**, **Rajas**, and **Tamas**‡ are inherent in, and inseparable from, all the phenomenal appearances in the universe which are, in reality,

...but are merely modifications of their own qualities, so the three fundamental bodily humors lie at the root of, and run through, the course of all known forms of bodily disorder.

The imbalanced bodily humors (**Dosha**), in contact with the different elements (**Dhatu**) and excrements (**Mala**) of the body, along with the differences in their locations and pathological effects, give rise to the different forms of disease.*

The naming of a disease depends upon where the various elementary principles of the body are affected by the imbalanced bodily humors; it is named according to whether it is seated in the lymph-chyle, the blood, the flesh, fat, bone, or the semen.

**Rasaja Disorders:** — Disorders such as an aversion to and loss of appetite for food, indigestion, aching in the limbs, fever, nausea, a sense of fullness even without eating, heaviness of the limbs, diseases affecting the heart, jaundice, constriction of any internal passage of the body (**Margo-parodha**), emaciation of the body (cachexia), a bad taste in the mouth, weakness in the limbs, premature graying and falling out of the hair, and symptoms indicating the decay of old age, should be regarded as having their seat in the imbalanced lymph-chyle (**Rasa**).

This answers the question, "How can the imbalanced bodily humors bring about a disease of the **Adhi-vala** type—a disease which is specifically due to the imbalance of the innate and primary factors of life?"

place in which they are trapped due to an

Now it may be asked again whether the relationship
of a disease, such as fever, etc., with the deranged bodily
humors is constant and inseparable, or otherwise. All
human beings would be in danger of perpetually
falling ill if the mentioned connection (relationship)
were constant and inseparable; but in the case of their
separate existence, it is only natural that their charac-
teristic symptoms should manifest themselves separately
instead of being simultaneously present with
fever, etc., as they are found to be in reality. And
accordingly, the theory that diseases (such as fever, etc.)
and the deranged bodily humors have a separate
existence, and are not *prima facie* intimately correlated
with one another, is discredited. On the
other hand, the assumption of their separate existence
invalidates the incontestable conclusion that diseases
such as fever, etc., are produced by the deranged humors
of the body.

Hence it may be safely asserted that no disease can
occur without the direct mediation or intervention of
the deranged bodily humors. Yet the connection
(relationship) which exists between the two is neither
constant nor separable. Just as the physical phenomena of
lightning, storm, thunder, and rain cannot happen
independently of the sky (cloud); and yet they

<summary>This page argues that no disease can exist without the mediation of deranged bodily humors. It refutes the idea that diseases and humors exist independently, while noting that their relationship is neither constant nor entirely separable.</summary>

sometimes do or do not occur with the presence of a cloud; again, just as bubbles—which are in reality only modifications of the underlying water—do not swell up on its surface at all times, so the connection between a disease and the bodily humors is neither universally separable nor inseparable.

## CHAPTER XXV:

Now we shall discuss the chapter dealing with the eight different forms of surgical operations (**Ashtavidha-Shastra-Karmanya-madhyāyam**).

**Metrical Texts:**—Bhagandara, Shlaishmika Granthi, Tilakālaka, Arvuda, Arsha, Charma-Kila, Jatumani, Mānsa-Saṁghāta, Gala-Sunthikā, Valmika, Vrana-Vartma, Shataponaka, Adhrusha, Upadansha, Mānsakandha, Adhimānsaka, as well as ailments due to the lodging of a foreign body in the flesh or bone, and the sloughing of ligaments, flesh, or veins, are the diseases in which an incision (**Chhedyam**) should be made.

**Conditions of Suturing :**—An ulcer resulting from the application of fire (cautery), or any alkaline preparation (caustic), or one treated with any poisonous drug or substance, or one from whose interior the embedded <term>Shalyam</term> (foreign matter) has not been removed, should not be sewn up without being thoroughly cleansed and purified aseptically. Since any foreign matter—whether a hair, nail, or a particle of dust or bone—lying embedded in its cavity might cause abnormal suppuration, accompanied by extreme pain and excessive secretion. Hence, such ulcers should be thoroughly cleansed (and all foreign or internal morbid matter should be extracted from them) before being sewn up.

**Mode of Suturing :**—Then, having pressed the ulcer into its proper position, it should be sutured with strings of any of the following kinds, *namely*: thin cotton thread; the fibers of the <term>Ashmántaka</term> tree or hemp plants; or of the <term>Atasi</term>, <term>Murvá</term>, or <term>Guduchi</term>; or with strips of leather, plaited horsehair, or animal sinews, into any of the standard shapes (of suturing) known as the <term>Gophaná</term>, <term>Tunna-Sevani</term>, and <term>Riju-Granthi</term>, etc., or as suited to the shape and position of the ulcerated part. The margin of the ulcer should be gently pressed close with the fingers during suturing. A round needle to

the length of two fingers' width should be used in sewing up an ulcer occurring about any joint or in a part of the body where the flesh is thin and sparse. A needle with a triangular body (trihedral) measuring three fingers' width in length is recommended for an ulcer appearing on any fleshy part of the body. A semi-circular or bow-shaped needle should be used in cases where the site of the ulcer is found to be on the scrotum, on the skin of the abdomen, or near any of the <term>Marmas</term> (<gloss>vital parts</gloss>).

Needles of these three shapes should be constructed with sharp points that can be handled with the greatest ease, having a girth equal to that of the stem of a <term>Málati</term> flower.

The needle should not be inserted too close to, or too far from, the fissure or the mouth of an ulcer, as there might be a risk of the suture breaking at the slightest pressure or movement in the first instance, or causing pain in the second. An ulcer, thus properly sutured, should be covered with cotton and dusted with a powdered compound consisting of the powders of <term>Priyangu</term>, <term>Anjanam</term>, <term>Yasthyáhva</term>, and <term>Rodhra</term>, or with the ashes of a burnt piece of <term>Kshauma</term> cloth, or with the powder of the <term>Shallaki</term> fruit. Then the ulcer should be properly bandaged, and the measures and rules regarding the dietary regimen and conduct previously established in the chapter

The eight kinds of surgical operations have thus been briefly described. They will be dealt with later on in the <term>Chikitsitam</term>.

**Defective Surgical Operations:** These eight forms of operations may be accompanied by dangers of four different kinds, such as those arising from an insufficient or excessive performance, or from the slanting or oblique deviation (of the knife or the instrument), or from an act of self-injury on the part of the physician.

A physician (surgeon) performing a wrong operation on the body of his patient—either through mistake, or through the lack of necessary skill or knowledge, or out of greed, fear, nervousness, or haste, or as a consequence of being insulted or abused—should be condemned as the direct cause of many new and unforeseen ailments. A patient with any instinct of self-preservation would do well to keep away from such a physician, or from one who makes a wrong or injudicious application of the cautery, and should shun his presence just as he would shun a wildfire or a cup of fatal poison.

The symptoms which generally manifest themselves in connection with the unwise injury of any of the five vital parts or principles of the body (such as the joints, bones, veins, ligaments, etc.) are dizziness, delirium, loss of bodily functions, semi-insensibility (a comatose state), incapacity to support oneself, cessation of mental functions, heat, fainting, looseness of the limbs, difficult breathing, excruciating pain or pain characteristic of the deranged <term>Váyu</term>, discharge of blood or a thin watery secretion like the washings of meat from the injured part or organ, along with coma or the failure of all the senses. A vein (<term>Shirā</term>) severed or injured in any way is accompanied by a copious flow (hemorrhage) of deep red blood, resembling the hue of the cochineal insect, from the ulcer; and the deranged local <term>Váyu</term> readily exhibits all its essential characteristics and brings about diseases which have been enumerated under that heading in the chapter on the description of blood.

The complications that accompany the slanted insertion of a surgical instrument have been described before; accordingly, care should be taken to leave no room for these issues to occur during a surgical operation.

A patient, who may even mistrust their own parents, children, and relatives, should place absolute faith in their physician and entrust their life into the doctor's hands without the slightest fear of danger; therefore, a physician should protect the patient as if they were their own child. A surgical case may be resolved with a single incision, or it may require two, three, four, or more than that to achieve a cure. By benefiting humanity through their professional skill, a physician achieves glory and earns the praise of the good and the wise in this life, and shall live in paradise in the next.

### CHAPTER XXVI.

We shall now discuss the chapter that treats the exploration of splinters that are lost or deeply seated in the body (**Pranashta-Shalya-Vijnāniya-madhyāyam**).

**Definition:**—The term <term>Shalyam</term> is derived from the root "Shala" or "Shvala" (meaning to move swiftly) combined with the Unádi suffix "Yat." <term>Shalyas</term> may be divided into two kinds based on whether they are extrinsic (**Ágantuka**) or idiopathic (**Shárira**) in their origin.

A <term>Shalyam</term> usually acts as an impeding or obstructing agent to the entire body; therefore, the science which deals with its nature and characteristics is called the <term>Shalya-Shástram</term> (Surgery). An idiopathic (**Shárira**) <term>Shalyam</term> may be a hair, a nail, clotted blood (<term>Dhátus</term>),<note>Embolism and thrombosis have been included within the category of Shalyam by Ayurvedic pathologists.</note> excrement (<term>Mala</term>), or deranged humors of the body (<term>Dosha</term>). Conversely, an extrinsic <term>Shalyam</term> should be regarded as one that afflicts the body and originates from a source other than any of the preceding ones, including particles of iron and bone, stems of grass, bamboo scrapings, and bits of horn, etc. But an **Ágantuka** (extrinsic) <term>Shalyam</term> specifically denotes an article of iron, because it

<summary>This chapter introduces the study of "Shalyam" (splinters or foreign bodies) and defines the surgical science of Shalya-Shástram. It categorizes splinters into idiopathic types, arising from within the body like clots or hair, and extrinsic types, such as iron or bone fragments.</summary>
<keywords>Shalyam, Shalya-Shástram, surgery, extrinsic splinters, idiopathic splinters, Ayurvedic pathology, Pranashta-Shalya-Vijnāniya-madhyāyam, Dhátus, Dosha</keywords>

--- Page 343 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on extrinsic splinters (Ágantuka Shalyam), specifically focusing on why iron is the primary material used for weapons and how these objects affect the body.</meta>

<page-num>248</page-num>
<header>THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. | Chap XXVI.</header>

preeminently serves the purpose of killing and is the most irresistible of all metals. Since any degree of sharpness can be given to the point of an object made of iron, and since it can be easily shot from a distance, iron is the metal exclusively chosen for the construction of darts or arrows.

**Classification of Shafts:** — Arrows (<term>Shara</term>) may be divided into two classes based on whether they are feathered or unfeathered; and their barbs are usually shaped like trees, leaves, flowers, or fruits, or are made to resemble the mouths of birds and wild and ferocious animals.

**Flights of arrows:** — The flights or directions of an arrow (<term>Shalyam</term>) may be divided into five different kinds: upward, downward, backward (coming from behind), oblique, and straight. Whether through diminished momentum or through external resistance, an arrow may drop and penetrate the skin, arteries, or any internal channel of the body, or into any bone or its cavity, causing a wound or an ulcer (<term>Vrana</term>) at the point of penetration.

**Symptoms:** — Now hear me describe the symptoms which are exhibited in connection with an arrow-wound (<term>Shalya-Vrana</term>).<note>An arrow or an iron barb, from “Shala,” meaning to kill.</note> These symptoms may be

Symptoms similar to those described above appear when the arrow is lodged in any of the vital parts (<term>Marmas</term>) of the body. The previous symptoms are only faintly visible in a case of superficial penetration.

<page-num>251</page-num>

(<term>Shalyam</term>), when lodged in the direction of the local hair growth, in* the throat, in any internal channel of the body, or in a vein, the skin, or a muscle, or into a cavity of the bone, and not in any way affected by the action of the imbalanced bodily humours, may heal quickly and spontaneously; but it may break open and become painful again if the bodily humours become imbalanced and aggravated by a blow or physical exercise.

<note>* This is so as not to obstruct the flow of the blood or serum in the area.</note>

### **Localization :**---The exact position of a shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) embedded in the skin should be determined by applying a plaster composed of clay, <term>Másha</term>-pulse, <term>Yava</term>, <term>Godhuma</term>, and cow dung over the injured limb or part. The limb should be properly lubricated with oil and fomented (by applying heat to its surface to induce sweating) before the plaster is applied. The shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) should be considered lodged in the part that shows signs of pain, redness, or swelling (<term>Samrambha</term>) after such an application. As an alternative, the affected part should be plastered with clarified butter, common clay, and sandalwood paste. The embedded shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) is then precisely located at the spot where, due to the heat of the affected part, the clarified butter, earth, or sandalwood paste is found to have melted or dried up.

Similarly, the method of locating a shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>),

embedded in the flesh is as follows:—First, the patient should be properly lubricated and induced to sweat (<term>diaphorised</term>) with medicinal agents suited to the requirements of his case. Then, the part or the limb having been thus reduced with depletive measures, the shaft will be found to have dislodged from its seat and to be moving about (within the deeper tissues of the affected part), causing pain, redness, and swelling. In such a case, the exact location of the shaft should be identified at the spot where the pain and swelling, etc., occur. The same measures should be adopted in the case of a shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) which lies embedded in the abdominal cavity (<term>Kostha</term>), or in a bone, joint, or muscle.

In the case of a <term>Shalyam</term> lodged in a vein, in an artery, in any external channel (<term>Srota</term>) of the body, or in a ligament, the patient should be made to ride in a carriage with a broken or cut-off wheel and dragged up and down in it on an uneven road. The pain and swelling, etc., resulting from the jolting, will occur at that part of his body where the shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) is embedded.

In the case of a shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) lodged in a bone, the affected bone should be lubricated and induced to sweat with oil and heat respectively, after which it should be firmly pressed and bound up. The location of the pain or swelling caused by such a procedure will mark the exact position of the embedded <term>Shalyam</term>.

<header>Chap. XXVI. ] SUTRASTHANAṀ. 253</header>

Similarly, in the case of a shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) lodged in a joint, the same lubricating, sweat-inducing (<term>diaphorising</term>), compressing, and expanding measures should be adopted; the painful swelling caused by these actions will indicate its exact location. No definite method can be established for ascertaining the exact location of a <term>Shalyam</term> lodged in any of the vital parts of the body (<term>Marma</term>), because they coexist with the eight different locations of ulcers, such as the skin, the flesh, the bone, etc. <note>Accordingly, measures ordered to be adopted in connection with a shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) lodged in any one of these locations should be applied with the necessary changes (<term>mutatis mutandis</term>) to cases in which these <term>Marmas</term> are found to be similarly affected.</note>

**General rule:—**A painful swelling occurring at any part of the body—triggered by physical or natural activities such as riding on an elephant or on horseback, climbing a steep hill, bending a bow, gymnastic exercises, running, wrestling, walking, leaping, swimming, high-jumping, yawning, coughing, singing, spitting, burping, laughing, practicing <term>Pránáyáma</term> (regulating the breath before the practice of <term>Yoga</term>), the emission of semen, urine, or gas, or defecation—would clearly indicate the exact location of the embedded shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>).

Metals or substances of similar softness, which are naturally cold, are melted and become amalgamated with the elements of the organism under such circumstances. A hair, or a particle of hard bone, wood, stone, bamboo scraping, or clay, which remains lodged in the body as a <term>Shalyam</term>, does not melt, nor does it undergo any change or deterioration.

The physician who is fully knowledgeable about the five different courses or flights of an arrow (<term>Shalyam</term>), whether feathered or unfeathered, and has minutely observed and studied the symptoms caused by its lodgement in any of the eight different seats of ulcers (<term>Vrana</term>) in the human organism (such as the skin, etc.), is alone worthy of attending to kings and nobles.

# CHAPTER XXVII.

Now we shall discuss the chapter dealing with the methods of extracting splinters (**Shalya-paniya-madhyayam**).

There are two kinds of <term>Shalyas</term>. A <term>Shalya</term> is either loose or firmly fixed in its position within the body. We shall now describe the fifteen different methods of extracting a loose <term>Shalyam</term>, which are as follows: extraction by the body's natural expulsive functions (<term>Svabhába</term>), by suppuration or decomposition (<term>Páchanam</term>), by excision (<term>Bhedanam</term>), by bursting (<term>Dáranam</term>), by pressing (<term>Pidanam</term>), by rubbing (<term>Pramárjanam</term>), by blowing medicinal powders into the affected part with the mouth (<term>Nirdhmápanam</term>), by the administration of emetics (<term>Vamanam</term>), by the use of purgatives (<term>Virechanam</term>), by washing (<term>Prakshálanam</term>), by friction with the fingers (<term>Pratimarsha</term>), by straining as if during a bowel movement (<term>Praváhanam</term>), by sucking (<term>Áchushanam</term>), by applying a magnet (<term>Ayaskánta</term>), and by creating excitement (<term>Harsham</term>).

Embedded foreign matter is usually expelled from the eyes and other areas by inducing tearing, sneezing, belching, coughing, urination, bowel movements, and the passing of gas.

A <term>Shalyam</term>, or any other foreign matter which has

<summary>This chapter introduces fifteen distinct methods for extracting loose foreign bodies (Shalyas) from the body, ranging from surgical techniques to natural biological processes. It details how involuntary actions like sneezing or coughing help expel foreign matter from specific areas like the eyes.</summary>

<keywords>Shalya, extraction methods, Shalyapaniya-madhyayam, foreign bodies, Ayurvedic surgery, medicinal powders, magnets, bodily functions</keywords>

--- Page 352 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the fifteen methods for extracting foreign bodies (Shalyas), specifically detailing how to manage objects lodged in deep tissues, the eyes, the nostrils, and the throat.</meta>

<page-num>257</page-num>
<header>Chap. XXVII.] SUTRASTHANAM.</header>

penetrated into the deeper tissues of the flesh should be extracted by inducing suppuration in the affected area. The decaying flesh will loosen the hold of the *Shalyam*, and the weight of the secreted pus and blood will cause it to fall out.

The site or location of a fixed *Shalyam* should be opened with an incision if it is not ejected even after local suppuration has been established. If the *Shalyam* fails to come out even after the incision, the affected part should be pressed with the fingers, or medicines possessing the property of exerting pressure should be applied over its surface. A particle of any fine matter accidentally dropped into the eye should be removed with sprays of cold water, by blowing into the eye with the mouth, or by rubbing it with hair or the fingers.

A residue of digested food or mucus, a remnant of any food matter (<term>*Áhárashesha*</term>) misdirected into the nostrils, or any small splinter loosely stuck there (<term>*Anu-shalyam*</term>), should be expelled by breathing hard, by coughing upward through the nostrils (<term>*Utkásha*</term>), or by blowing through the nose. A morsel of food acting as an obstructing *Shalyam* in the cavity of the stomach (<term>*Ámáshaya*</term>) should be ejected by rubbing (<term>*Pratimarsha*</term>) the fingers against the lining of the throat or against the region of the epiglottis; while such a morsel brought down into the intestines,

<sig>33</sig>

Pus or any other diseased matter found within the cavity of an ulcer should be removed by washing it, while trapped gas, or obstructed hard stool (<term>scybala</term>), or retained urine, or an obstructed fetus, should be pushed down and expelled by means of straining.

Any disturbed <term>Váyu</term> or watery secretion trapped in any part of the body, as well as poisoned blood or impure breast milk, should be sucked out with the mouth or with a horn.

A loose, unbarbed arrow, lodged in a wound with a wide mouth and lying in an **Anuloma** direction, should be withdrawn by applying a magnet to its end. A shaft of grief, driven into the heart by any of various emotional causes, should be removed by joy and celebration.

A foreign body (<term>Shalyam</term>), whether large or small, may be withdrawn from its place in either of the two ways known as **Anuloma** and **Pratiloma**. The **Anuloma** method consists of withdrawing a <term>Shalyam</term> through a path other than the one it entered, while the opposite is called **Pratiloma**.

A <term>Shalyam</term> lodged in a place close to the spot of its penetration (<term>Arvácheenam</term>) should be extracted through the path by which it entered.

(<term>Pratiloma</term>). On the other hand, a shaft or <term>Shalyam</term> that pierces deep into any part of the body but does not come out the other side (<term>Parácheenam</term>) should be drawn out through a path other than the one it entered (<term>Anuloma</term>).

A shaft that pierces deep into any part of the body so that it reaches the other side of the wounded limb or part (but does not cut clean through because its original momentum has decreased) and remains protruding in the swollen flesh, should be extracted through a channel other than the one it originally entered (<term>Anuloma</term>), by moving it or striking it with the hand or a hammer. The swollen flesh should be opened with an incision, whenever it is possible to do so, and the embedded <term>Shalyam</term> should be drawn out by moving or striking it with the hand as described before.

A <term>Shalyam</term> lodged in any soft part of the abdomen, chest, armpits, groin, or ribs should not be cut open or struck with a hammer. Instead, an attempt should be made to remove it with the hands through the path of its penetration (<term>Pratiloma</term>). If this fails, the <term>Shalyam</term> should be extracted with surgical appliances (<term>Shastra</term>) or other surgical instruments (<term>Yantras</term>).

<page-num>261</page-num>

A <term>Shalyam</term> lodged in any other part of the body that is difficult to extract, and which produces pain and local inflammation, should be removed by cutting the part open. In the case of a shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) which has pierced into the cavity of a bone, the surgeon should firmly press the affected bone with his legs and pull out the embedded shaft with all his might by gripping it with a surgical instrument. If that fails, a strong man should be asked to firmly hold the patient, and the <term>Shalyam</term> should be pulled out with the help of a gripping surgical instrument as before.

Alternatively, the bottom of the shaft should be tied to the string of a bow that is strung and fully bent down; the <term>Shalyam</term> should then be ejected by means of a full twang of the bowstring. Alternatively, a horse should be harnessed in the fashion known as the *<term>Panchángi-vandhanam</term>* (literally, bound in the five parts of the body), and the end of the <term>Shalyam</term> should be bent down and tied to the bridle. Then the horse should be whipped so as to raise its head suddenly, thus pulling out the embedded shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) from its site of lodgment by the jerk of its head. Alternatively, a high and strong bough of a tree should be pulled down and tied to the bent end of the shaft, as in the preceding case. The bough should then be let loose, thus pulling out the shaft (<term>Shalyam</term>) with its rebounding force.

The feather of a barbed shaft, lying embedded in a bone situated in a part of the body where the presence of such foreign matter is expected not to cause any special discomfort, should first be crushed by applying pressure to the swollen or protruding flesh, and the shaft then should be gently pulled out of its place of lodgment.

In the case of a piece of shellac being accidentally stuck in the <term>pharynx</term>, a metal tube should first be inserted into the passage, and then a heated metal rod should be lowered down to the obstructing shellac through its interior. The shellac, melted by the heat of the inserted rod, would naturally stick firmly to it. This should then be solidified by an injection of cold water poured down through the aforementioned tube; after that, the rod should be withdrawn, thus carrying away the melted shellac at its end.

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

Now we shall discuss the Chapter which deals with the favorable or unfavorable prognosis of an ulcer. (**Viparitaviparita - Vrana - Vijnaniya - madhyayam**).

**Metrical Text:**— Certain fatal or unfavorable symptoms (**Arishtas**) unmistakably predict the death of an ulcer patient, just as a flower, smoke, and clouds respectively herald fruit, fire, and rain. In most cases, the ignorant cannot correctly interpret these fatal symptoms because of their extremely subtle nature, or out of ignorance or stupidity, or because such symptoms are followed very closely by the patient's death.

These fatal indications serve as certain precursors of death in a patient, unless they are warded off by the blessings of holy **Bráhmanas**, who are free from low desires or animal instincts, and are also accustomed to practicing **Yoga** and other religious penances; or death may be averted with the help of men who are initiated into the mystery of preparing life-giving elixirs (**Rasayanam**).

---

<note>The symptoms developed by the disturbed bodily humours in a person's organism at a time when they have passed beyond all medical cure—and when the body serves as a mere passive background for these phenomena, awaiting its impending dissolution—are called **Arishtas**.</note>

<vocab>ulcer, prognosis, Arishtas, Vrana, Bráhmanas, Yoga, Rasayanam, humours</vocab>

<summary>This chapter introduces Arishtas, which are fatal symptoms that predict a patient's death when an ulcer has progressed beyond medical treatment. It explains that these signs are often too subtle for the unlearned to recognize, though death may potentially be averted through spiritual intervention or the use of alchemical elixirs.</summary>

<keywords>Arishtas, prognosis, fatal symptoms, ulcer, Bráhmanas, Yoga, Rasayanam, bodily humours, Ayurveda</keywords>

--- Page 362 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the prognosis of ulcers, focusing on the timing of fatal signs and the diagnostic significance of an ulcer's smell based on humeral imbalances.</meta>

# Chap. XXVIII. ] SUTRASTHĀNAM.

Many such signs do not prove instantly fatal but lead to death over time, just as certain diseases—believed by some to be caused by the influence of harmful planets—take time to become obvious after their incubation periods. Attempting to cure a patient who is destined to die results only in failure and the mockery of the world; therefore, a wise physician should make it worthwhile to carefully observe and study these fatal signs. Any abnormality in the natural smell, color, taste (sensation, sound, touch, etc.) of an ulcer indicates that the disease is nearing a fatal end.

An ulcer gives off a pungent, sharp, or fishy smell under the respective influences of the disturbed *Váyu*, *Pittam*, and *Kapham*. An ulcer affected by corrupted blood emits a smell like that of iron (<term>Loha-gandhi</term>), while one originating from the combined action of the disturbed humors emits a smell characterized by the distinctive features of each. On the other hand, an ulcer caused by the joint action of disturbed *Váyu* and *Pittam* emits a smell like that of fried paddy; one caused by the action of disturbed *Váyu* and *Kapham* emits a smell like linseed oil; whereas one brought about by the action of disturbed *Pittam* and *Kapham* smells like sesame oil. All odors that have a somewhat fishy character should be considered the natural smells of ulcers, and any other smell should be regarded as abnormal or unnatural.

<summary>This page explains that fatal signs in an ulcer may take time to manifest and warns physicians against treating terminal patients. It details how specific abnormal smells, caused by imbalances in the humors (Váyu, Pittam, and Kapham), serve as indicators of a fatal prognosis.</summary>

<keywords>prognosis, fatal symptoms, ulcers, humors, Váyu, Pittam, Kapham, Loha-gandhi, medical ethics, diagnostic smells</keywords>

--- Page 363 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of fatal signs in ulcers, specifically focusing on the diagnostic significance of unnatural smells and the absence or presence of specific types of pain relative to the humors.</meta>

THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [Chap. XXVIII.

An ulcer emitting a sweet smell like that of wine, or fragrant aloe wood (<term>Aguru</term>), clarified butter, <term>Jāti</term> flower, <term>Champaka</term>, sandal, lotus, or any celestial flower (<term>Divyagandha</term>), should be regarded as a precursor of death. Similarly, a smell like the one which characterizes a dog, horse, mole, crow, or a bug, or like the one emitted by dry, putrid meat, or resembling the smell of earth or slime, should likewise be deemed unfavorable or fatal in an ulcer.

A physician should give up a case where an ulcer, though it has assumed a blackish, saffron, or <term>Kankustha</term> color (a sort of mountain earth) through the action of the aggravated <term>Pittam</term>, is divested of the burning, sucking, and drawing pain which is peculiar to that disease-producing condition. Similarly, an ulcer which—though brought about through the action of the deranged <term>Kapham</term>—has become cold, hard, and whitish, as is natural in one of the <term>Kaphaja</term> type, should be given up as soon as it is marked by a burning pain. Likewise, an ulcer due to the action of the deranged <term>Váyu</term>, and characterized by a blackish hue and a thin secretion, and which is found to invade the vital principles of the body, should be abandoned by a physician whenever it is found to be entirely devoid of pain.

An ulcer which makes a gurgling or groaning sound, or one which is characterized by an extreme burning sensation, or is confined to the skin and the flesh,

<summary>This page details specific odors and physical symptoms in ulcers that indicate an approaching death, particularly when the expected sensory symptoms of a humeral imbalance are absent or contradict the ulcer's appearance.</summary>

<keywords>prognosis, fatal signs, ulcers, Pittam, Kapham, Váyu, medical diagnostics, Ayurvedic medicine, Aguru, Divyagandha</keywords>
