<meta>This page continues the catalog of fatal symptoms associated with ulcers, specifically describing internal sounds, temperature contradictions, and symbolic shapes that indicate a patient's impending death.</meta>

# Chap. XXVIII. | SUTRASTHÁNAM.

and is marked by the discharge of gas with a loud sound, it is sure to end fatally. Likewise, an ulcer characterized by extreme pain, even if it is not located near any vital parts of the body, or one that is cold on the surface but accompanied by an intense burning sensation inside (and vice versa), should be considered a sign of approaching death. Similarly, an ulcer should be regarded as fatal if it is shaped like the barb of a spear, or a <term>Kunta</term> (a type of barbed dart or spear), or like a banner, chariot, horse, or elephant, or like a cow, an ox, a temple, or a palace.

A wise physician, concerned for his own reputation, should abandon a patient bedridden with an ulcer that appears to be dusted over with a type of powdered crust, or who suffers from one accompanied by a loss of flesh and strength, a cough, difficulty breathing, and an aversion to food. An ulcer that occurs at any of the vital parts of the body, secretes a large amount of pus and blood, and refuses to heal even after a course of proper and persistent medical treatment, is sure to end fatally.

## CHAPTER XXIX.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which treats of favorable or unfavorable prognosis in diseases, as known from messengers, omens, dreams, etc. **(Viparitāviparīta-Duta-Shakuna-Svapna- Nidarshanīya-madhyāyam).**

**Metrical Texts:** —The favorable or unfavorable outcome of a disease may be predicted from the appearance, speech, dress, and behavior of the messenger sent to call a physician, or from the nature of the star cluster and the lunar phase marking the time of his arrival, or from the direction of the wind (<term>Anila</term>) blowing at the time, or from the nature of omens (<term>Shakuna</term>) seen by him on the road, or from the posture, temperament, or speech of the physician himself.

A messenger belonging to the same caste as the patient* should be regarded as an auspicious omen, whereas one from a different caste would indicate a fatal or unfavorable outcome of the disease.

A eunuch, a husband of many wives, a messenger

---

<note>* A <term>Pāshanda</term> messenger should be sent to call a physician when a member of the same community falls ill; a householder, in the case of a patient of the same social order; a <term>Brāhmana</term>, in the case of a <term>Brāhmana</term> patient, and so on; while a violation of the rule would be looked upon as an evil omen.</note>

<summary>This chapter introduces the methods of predicting a disease's outcome through external indicators such as the messenger's behavior, astrology, and environmental omens. Similarly, messengers who call at the house of a physician holding a rope, club, or any other weapon in their hands, or who come dressed in black, red, yellow, wet, dirty, or torn garments, or with their upper wraps placed or arranged on their right shoulders (<term>Apasavya</term>), or clad in single cloths without such upper wraps on, should be regarded as omens of evil. A messenger, seeking to meet with a physician while he is engaged in making offerings to his deceased ancestors or to the gods, or one who calls on him at noon or at midnight, or morning or at evening, or during the occurrence of any abnormal physical phenomenon, or at an hour under the influence of any of the following asterisms (lunar mansions), *namely*, the Árdra, the Ashlesá, the Maghá, the Mulá, the two Purvás, and the Bharani, or on the day of the fourth, ninth, or the sixth phase of the moon (whether on the wane or on the increase), as well as on the last days of months and fortnights, should be considered a messenger of evil omen.

A messenger, hot and perspiring from being seated near a blazing fire, and calling upon a physician in the midday, should be deemed inauspicious in the case of a <term>Pittaja</term> (heat-related) illness; whereas a messenger of similar description should be looked upon as foreboding the favorable termination of a disease if it is due to the action of the disturbed <term>Kapham</term> (fluid/mucus). The favorable character of a messenger should be likewise determined in diseases originating through the action of the disturbed <term>Váyu</term> (wind),* etc.; and an intelligent physician is

A learned physician should thus determine the ominous character of a messenger in connection with other diseases as well.

**Messengers of happy augury :**—A fair and handsome messenger, who is dressed in clean and white garments and belongs to the same caste or spiritual clan (<term>Svagotra</term>) as the patient himself, predicts the successful outcome of the disease (for which medical aid is needed). A messenger visiting a physician either on foot or in a bullock cart, and who is contented, intelligent, and capable of acting according to the rules of decorum, time, and circumstances, and is independent and original in his thoughts and ideas, and carries ornaments and other auspicious articles on his person, is alone capable of rendering the best services in connection with the summoning of a physician. A messenger who first meets with a physician when the latter is calmly seated with his face towards the east and on clean and even ground should be regarded as a messenger of happy augury.

Similarly, the harmonious melodies of birds chirping on the branches of healthy *Kshira* trees <note>Trees that yield a milky sap</note>, bent under the weight of fruit and looking cheerful with their bounty of beautiful blossoms and foliage, should be regarded as happy omens. Likewise, the songs of birds perched on the terraces of palace towers or on the tops of banner poles, singing melodiously, or birds following the messenger with their songs, or singing while perched in auspicious directions of the heavens, or following him on his left, should equally be regarded as sights and sounds of good fortune.

A physician meeting a member of any of the low or degraded castes, or a blind or poor person, or a man who is hostile toward him, should consider the character of the disease to be unfavorable.

A gentle, cool, and fragrant breeze blowing from the direction of his destination should be regarded as a lucky omen by a physician. A wind that is hot and dry, and is filled with the foul odors of rotting matter, and which blows from the direction of his starting point, should be regarded as an evil omen.

The word "cut," used by someone else and accidentally heard by a physician on his way to the bedside of a patient suffering from Granthi (aneurysm) or Arvuda (tumor), should be regarded as a good omen; while the term "open," heard under similar circumstances and in connection with a case of Vidradhi (abscess), or Gulma (abdominal gland), or Udara (ascites), should be regarded as an equally lucky sign. Similarly, the term "stopped" is favorable in a case of dysentery or hemoptysis. Thus, the physician should interpret the omens according to the nature of each individual case.

A messenger who is a good omen foretells a favorable end to a disease, while the opposite is indicated by a messenger of an unfavorable type. Therefore, a physician should carefully observe the character of the messenger dispatched to seek his aid.

**Dreams:—** Now I will describe the dreams, dreamt by either the patient or his relatives, which portend either a fatal or a successful end to the illness. A patient who dreams of traveling south on the back of an elephant, or on a carnivorous animal, or of riding on a boar or a buffalo, or who sees himself being carried toward that direction by a dark woman with dishevelled hair and dressed in a blood-red garment—laughing and dancing—will soon meet his doom. A patient who has had any of the aforementioned dreams, under these circumstances, should get up in the morning and make a gift of <term>Másha-pulse</term>, sesame, iron, and gold to the <term>Brahmins</term>, and repeat the blessed <term>Tripáda Gáyatri</term> (<term>Mantras</term>).

After having a bad dream in the first watch of the night, a person should meditate upon a holy or auspicious subject, and then lie down again with all his senses fully controlled, and repeat the <term>Mantras</term> sacred to any of the gods. An evil dream should not be told to anyone else. The dreamer should reside in a holy temple for three consecutive nights and worship the deity with the most fervent devotion, which will nullify its evil effects.

## CHAPTER XXX.

Now we shall discuss the chapter that treats the prognosis obtained from the distorted functions of the five sensory organs (**Panchen-driyārtha-Vipratipatti-madhyāyam**).

**Metrical texts :**—A distortion or abnormality in the functions of the mind or brain (**Shilam**) and the organs of sense perception is called **Arishtam** (an unfavorable symptom foretelling death). Now hear me describe, in brief detail, the symptoms known as **Arishta** (fatal indications).

A person who hears various divine sounds even in the absence of celestial beings (such as the Siddhas, the Gandharvas, etc.), or thinks that they hear the noise of a city, the moaning of the sea, or the rumbling of a rain cloud without their actual presence or proximity—or who is unable to hear these sounds even when they are actually present and sounding, or attributes them to causes other than the actual ones—should be regarded as a doomed person. A person who interprets the noise of a city or the rustling of a forest as sounds coming from other sources, or who rejoices at the voices of their enemies and is annoyed by those of their own devoted friends, or who suddenly loses the faculty

...of hearing without any clear or obvious reason should be considered already on the threshold of death.

The person who feels cold when touching a hot or warm substance and, *vice versa*, complains of a burning sensation even when suffering from a boil or a <term>Kaphaja</term> pustule (characterized by numbness, shivering, etc.), or who shivers when their body temperature is felt to be notably high, should be regarded as being at the point of death. The person who has lost the sense of touch and does not feel any pain in any part of the body when it is struck or amputated, or who feels as if their body had been covered with particles of dust, or who suffers from discoloration of the skin marked with blue or red stripes, and who is pestered by swarms of blue flies after a bath or an application of ointment, should be regarded as one who has already passed the limits of life.

Similarly, the person whose body emits a fragrant smell without having been rubbed with any kind of perfume, or for whom sweet things taste acidic and acidic things taste sweet, or who shows symptoms of a general distortion of the sense of taste, or in whom substances of different tastes (given in their <term>officinal order</term> of listing) tend to aggravate the disturbed bodily humours, or cause their calming and a loss of appetite if eaten in the reverse

# CHAPTER XXXI.

Now we shall discuss the chapter which deals with the prognosis to be gathered from the altered condition of features (**Chháya-Viprati-patti-madhyáyam**).*

**Metrical Text:—**The man whose complexion suddenly assumes a brown, red, blue, or yellow shade should be regarded as already dead. The man who has lost all sense of modesty or propriety, and whose complexion, strength (<term>ojah</term>), and memory have suddenly undergone discoloration or extreme deterioration, should be counted with the dead. There is little chance for the life of a patient whose lower lip hangs down while the upper one is drawn or turned up, and both of them have assumed a black color like that of a <term>jamboline fruit</term>. The patient whose teeth fall out or which have assumed a reddish

<note>* Physicians of the <term>Áyurvedic</term> School, however, observe a distinction between <term>Chháyá</term> (shade of complexion), <term>Prabhá</term> (healthful glow of the complexion), and <term>Varna</term> (natural color of the complexion) itself. The <term>Chháyá</term>, or the shade of one's complexion, may be easily distinguished as clear, rough, or cool, etc., and can be detected only on a close view. The <term>Prabhá</term>, on the other hand, is visible from a distance and admits of being divided into seven different types, such as red, yellow, white, brown, greenish, pale, and black. The <term>Varna</term>, or the natural color of a person's complexion, is found to be either fair, black, or dusky leaning towards fair, according to their race and habitation. The term also includes natural modesty, appearance, and ease.</note>

<summary>This page begins Chapter 31, focusing on how sudden changes in a patient's complexion, mental state, and facial features serve as fatal omens. It specifically notes the medical distinction between the shade, glow, and natural color of the skin in Ayurvedic practice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the metrical text of Chapter 31, detailing specific physical symptoms in the tongue, nose, eyes, and limbs that serve as fatal prognostic signs.</meta>

or a dark brown color, or a color like that of a <term>Khanjana bird</term> (dark blue), should be considered as already having passed away.

The patient whose tongue has become coated, swollen, or immobile, or is black in color, should be considered to be at the gate of death. The patient whose nose has collapsed or become bent, cracked, or dry, or who makes a gurgling sound through the nostrils while breathing, should be given up as lost. A patient is certainly leaving this life if their eyes appear to be contracted, unequal, slanted, or immobile, or if they are insensitive to light or touch, sunken in their sockets, bloody, or marked by excessive <term>tearing (lachrymation)</term>. The patient whose hair appears to have been glued to his head, whose eyebrows are pulled together and hang down, and whose eyelashes are limp, should be considered as about to leave his physical body.

The patient who is unable to swallow any food or hold up his head, and who looks with a kind of fixed stare with all memory of life completely wiped out, should be considered to be dying on that very day. A wise or prudent physician should stop the medical treatment of a patient—regardless of whether they are strong or weak—who faints every time they are lifted or seated. The patient who constantly extends or draws up his legs, or keeps them in a curled-up position, should be

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [ Chap. XXXI.

is looked upon as rapidly dying. A wise physician should abandon a patient characterized by the coldness of their breath and extremities and a hurried and intermittent respiration, or who is found breathing with their mouth open or lips separated.

Similarly, a patient affected with a kind of stupor or insomnia who remains drowsy all day long, or faints at the slightest attempt to speak, should be counted among the dead. The patient who licks their upper lip, or is troubled with eructations <note>belching</note>, or holds conversations with the dead, should be considered as already having entered the region of the dead. A man who spontaneously bleeds through the roots of his hair (the pores of the skin) for reasons other than a case of poisoning should be considered as dying on that day.

A patient affected with a rising pain around the heart region, like the one which distinguishes a case of <term>Vátáshtilá</term> (the appearance of a stone-like lump rising or seated within the chest and attributed to the action of the deranged <term>Váyu</term>), accompanied by an aversion to food, etc., should already be reckoned among the dead.

An idiopathic swelling (<term>Shopha</term>) first occurring in either of the lower extremities in a male patient—not as a complication of any other disease*—as well as a similar swelling first appearing at the face, or about the region

### Chap. XXXI. ] SUTRASTHĀNAM.

of the anus in a male or a female patient, is certain to have a fatal termination.

A patient suffering from a cough or asthma, accompanied by dysentery, fever, hiccups, vomiting, and swelling of the penis and the scrotum, should be given up as lost. Excessive perspiration, a burning sensation, hiccups, <term>dyspnea</term> <note>shortness of breath</note>, and <term>hyperpyrexia</term> <note>extremely high fever</note> with a burning sensation of the body, are undoubtedly capable of extinguishing the vital spark even in a strong patient. Similarly, a patient with a black-coated tongue, the left eye sunken in its socket, and a foul smell from the mouth, should be given up as lost.

The mouth of a man who is on his way to the mansions of the god of death becomes filled with fluid <note>tears or saliva</note>, the legs are wet with perspiration, and the pupils of the eyes roll about or become listless.

The patient whose limbs suddenly become abnormally light or heavy is certain to go to the region of the son of the sun-god (<term>Yama</term>). The patient whose body emits a fishy, dirty, or even a fragrant smell—or smells like fat, oil, or <term>clarified butter</term>—is on the way to the mansions of Death.

The patient on whose forehead lice move about freely, or whose offerings the crows do not eat, or who does not find comfort in any position or place, goes to the mansions of the god of death. A patient who has

--- Page 387 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of terminal symptoms, continuing from the description of a patient who has become emaciated or suffers from complex, recurring illnesses.</meta>

# THE SUSHRUTA SAMHITA. [Chap. XXXI.

become emaciated and enfeebled, or has been suffering from a complication of such diseases as fever, dysentery, edema <note>swelling caused by fluid retention</note>, etc., with one malady following another pre-existing one, should be considered beyond the reach of medicine. A ravenous hunger or an unquenchable thirst in a weak patient, who refuses to be appeased or satisfied with sweet, wholesome, and palatable food or drink, should be regarded as a fatal sign. A patient exhibiting such symptoms as diarrhea, an excruciating headache, colic in the intestines, thirst, and a gradual failing of strength, stands in danger of imminent death. Death is due to the transitory nature of life, or it may be attributed to irregular conduct, or to the deeds of one’s previous existence transformed into the forces of fate.

Ghosts, evil spirits, *Pisháchas* <note>flesh-eating demons</note>, and monsters of various shapes and names, constantly lead men to death. These evil spirits, owing to their natural killing tendencies, nullify the effectiveness of medicines; and therefore it is futile to undertake the medical treatment of a man who exhibits any of the aforementioned fatal symptoms, which prove that he has fallen into the clutches of such evil spirits.

->Thus ends the Thirty-first Chapter of the Sutrasthánam in the Sushruta-Samhitá which deals with prognosis from perverted features.<-
